On smooth-group actions on reductive groups and spherical buildings
Jeffrey D. Adler, Joshua M. Lansky, Loren Spice

Abstract
. Let k k be a field, and suppose that Γ \Gamma is a smooth k k -group that acts on a connected, reductive k k -group G ~ {\widetilde{G}} . Let G G denote the maximal smooth, connected subgroup of the group of Γ \Gamma -fixed points in G ~ {\widetilde{G}} . Under fairly general conditions, we show that G G is a reductive k k -group, and that the image of the functorial embedding 𝒮 ⁡ ( G ) ⟶ 𝒮 ⁡ ( G ~ ) {\mathscr{S}(G)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})} of spherical buildings is the set of “ Γ \Gamma -fixed points in 𝒮 ⁡ ( G ~ ) \mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}) ”, in a suitable sense. In particular, we do not need to assume that Γ \Gamma has order relatively prime to the characteristic of k k (nor even that Γ \Gamma is finite), nor that the action of Γ \Gamma preserves a Borel–torus pair in G ~ {\widetilde{G}} .



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification

20G07,
20G15,
14L30,
20E42

1.
Introduction

Throughout this paper,
k
will denote an arbitrary field
of characteristic exponent
p\geq 1
.
Let
{\widetilde{G}}
denote a connected, reductive
k
-group.
We will let

\Gamma
be a smooth
k
-group that
acts on
{\widetilde{G}}
, but, for simplicity,
in this introduction we will just take
\Gamma
to be
an abstract, finite group.

If
\Gamma
has order prime to
p
,
then Prasad and Yu
[
prasad-yu:actions
]
*Theorem 2.1
have shown that the connected
part of the group of fixed points
G:=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}

is a reductive group.

In the case where
k
is finite or local,
one can then ask if there is a natural lifting
from representations of
G(k)
to those of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
.
For an appropriate definition of ‘natural’,
base change would be a special case of this phenomenon.

Earlier work
[
adler-lansky:lifting1
,
adler-lansky:lifting2
]

by some of the present authors
accomplishes a step toward such a lifting
when
k
is finite,
but for our intended applications we imposed
a different hypothesis on
\Gamma
,
namely, that for some extension
E
of
k
,
the action of
\Gamma
on
{\widetilde{G}}_{E}

preserves a Borel–torus pair.
That is,
\Gamma
acts
quasisemisimply
on
{\widetilde{G}}_{E}
.
(In fact, we deal there with a setting that is still more general,
where
\Gamma
acts only on the root datum of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
and
G
need not be a fixed-point subgroup.
But we don’t pursue that setting here.)

In the case where
k
is a nonarchimedean local field
with residue field
\mathfrak{f}
,
we will show
in another work
[
adler-lansky-spice:actions3
]
that,
under reasonable tameness hypotheses,
if
\Gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{E}
,
then we can identify the Bruhat–Tits bulding
\mathscr{B}(G,k)

with the fixed-point set
\mathscr{B}({\widetilde{G}},k)^{\Gamma}
.
Moreover, for such a fixed point
x\in\mathscr{B}({\widetilde{G}},k)^{\Gamma}
,
one has a quasisemisimple action of
\Gamma

on the associated
\mathfrak{f}
-group
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{x}
,
whose rational points are the reductive quotient
of the parahoric subgroup
{\widetilde{G}}(k)_{x}
of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
.
We will also examine the relationship between

{\mathsf{G}}_{x}
and
the maximal smooth, connected subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{x}^{\Gamma}
.
Since many representations of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
are constructed
out of representations of
\widetilde{\mathsf{G}}_{x}(\mathfrak{f})
,
our lifting for representations of finite groups implies
a lifting for some of the data used to construct representations
of
p
-adic groups.

But in order to accomplish any of the above, we first need to know
that
G
is a reductive group.

We already know that
G
is reductive in two situations:
the order of
\Gamma
is prime to
p
(from

[
prasad-yu:actions
]
*Theorem 2.1, as mentioned above)
or

\Gamma
acts quasisemisimply
(from
[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5).
Comparing these two hypotheses, one sees
that neither one implies the other,
suggesting that a common generalization exists.

In the present paper, we provide three overlapping results:
Theorem
A
, which proves our strongest conclusions about
quasisemisimple actions under
a rationality hypothesis;
Theorem
B
, which removes the rationality hypothesis,
is closely related to

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5, and corrects
an error in it (see Remark
3.2
);
and Theorem
C
, which is a common generalization of

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5 and

[
prasad-yu:actions
]
*Theorem 2.1, and which
moreover comes close to generalizing Theorem
A
.
Obviously, one would prefer to have a single result, but
it turns out that there is a trade-off:
we must either impose a rationality assumption,
as we do in Theorem
A
; or
relax our detailed control over the structure of the fixed-point group,
as we do in Theorem
B
; or
impose a smoothness assumption,
as we do in Theorem
C
.
Since Corollary
7.8
shows that
the smoothness assumption is only an issue in
certain specific circumstances in characteristic
2
,
we regard this as only a small imperfection.
Moreover, these are genuine restrictions, not just
an artifact of our proof.
Examples

8.12
and

8.13

show that the
equivalent statements of
Theorem
B
(
2
)
do not always hold;
and
Example
10.3.5
is a counterexample to
Theorem
C
(
2
)
if we drop the smoothness hypothesis.

Our main result improves on

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5
in a few additional ways.
First, we allow
\Gamma
to be any smooth algebraic group,
rather than just an abstract finite group.
Second, in order to handle the case of certain groups
over imperfect fields of characteristic two,
we previously needed to cite an unpublished result of Lemaire

[
lemaire:twisted-characters
]
*Théorème 4.6.
Theorem
B
(
2
),
specifically the equivalence of
(
2a
) with (
2e
),
replaces our use of that result.
Third,
as in Prasad–Yu
[
prasad-yu:actions
]
*Proposition 3.4,
but under our weaker hypotheses,
we show that the image of the functorial embedding

{\mathscr{S}(G)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})}

of spherical buildings
is the set
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
-fixed points in
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
.

This paper and a subsequent one
[
adler-lansky-spice:actions3
]

are inspired by
work of Prasad and Yu
[
prasad-yu:actions
]
.
Although the analogous results of Prasad and Yu are a special
case of our Theorem
C
,
we cannot claim to have “recovered” the former,
as we use them in our proofs in an essential way.

The outline of the paper is as follows.
In §
2
, we fix notation, and
recall some basic structural results about
root systems and algebraic groups.
In §
3
, we state our main theorems.
In §
4
, we discuss
general results on fixed-point groups and spherical buildings, and
define a notion of ‘induction’ of
groups with action.
This latter requires some foundations from
algebraic geometry, which are discussed in Appendix
A
,
written jointly with Sean Cotner.

The bulk of the paper, §
5
–
7
,
is devoted to the quasisemisimple case, i.e., the case where,
at least after base change,
there is a single Borel–torus pair that is
preserved by all elements of
\Gamma
.
In §
5
, we recapitulate and flesh out
some abstract results about quasisemisimple actions on root data,
first systematically discussed in

[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
.

In §
6
, we observe
(Proposition
6.5
)
that there is a close connection between
maximal tori in
G
and
{\widetilde{G}}
, which allows us
to build a
“
\Gamma
-equivariant structure theory for
{\widetilde{G}}
”,
proving analogues of results in
[
borel:linear
]
*§14,
especially involving root groups.
This culminates in the proof of
Theorem
A
(
0
), where we prove that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable
(the phrase commonly written in the literature as
“
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is defined over
k
”) by
pasting together root subgroups to
exhibit a large smooth subscheme that
remains large after base change.
This
allows us to prove many facts over
k
by
first base changing to its algebraic closure.
For example, the proof of
Theorem
A
(
2
), which says that
the connected, smoothed fixed-point group

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is reductive, becomes an easy consequence of

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5.

In §
6
, we use
Theorem
A
(
0
) to
study the relation between
Borel subgroups, then
parabolic subgroups, then finally
spherical buildings
for
G
and
{\widetilde{G}}
, deducing
Theorem
A
(
3
)
as, essentially, a re-statement of
Proposition
7.5
.
We then analyze the difference between the root systems
denoted, in the notation of §
6
, by

\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
and
\Phi(G,S)

in Proposition
7.6
, and, after deducing
the intermediate result Corollary
7.8
, use it to prove
Theorem
A
(
1
).

There are a few places in the paper where
our general investigations turn on a detailed understanding of
a very specific case. The first of these is
§
8
, where we handle the case that
(by Corollary
7.8
) is
the only nontrivial obstruction to smoothness of
fixed-point groups for quasisemisimple actions.
(The others are
Lemma
10.1.1
,
Proposition
10.1.6
, and
§
10.2
.)
Since this is also the case where
imperfect descent creates the most trouble, we actually
assume that we only have quasisemisimplicity after
(possibly inseparable) base change.
Proposition
8.15
discusses how close

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
comes to being smoothable, a question which,
as we will show when we prove
Theorem
B
(
2
) in
the next section, is
closely related to whether the action was quasisemisimple
before before change.

The proof of Theorem
B
in
§
9
is now almost routine, although
still involved.
It involves mostly the results of §
6
, using
the special case handled in §
8
to show that
Theorem
B
(
2
)(
2c
)
implies
Theorem
B
(
2
)(
2b
).
Our work here allows us to prove the pleasant
Corollary
9.1
, which
upgrades the classical result

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 7.5
over an algebraically closed field to handle separably closed fields, and
Corollary
9.3
, which is quite close to

[
lemaire:twisted-characters
]
*Théorème 4.6.

Finally, in §
10
,
we are ready to prove Theorem
C
.
Thanks to the work of Prasad and Yu
[
prasad-yu:actions
]
,
the only cases that can cause real difficulty are when
an absolutely almost-simple group has
an outer-automorphism group
whose order is divisible by
p
, or
that is not cyclic.
We handle the former case in
§
10.1
, which proves the important reduction result
Proposition
10.1.9
, and
the finite-group-theoretic result
Proposition
10.1.11
.
Surprisingly to us, this latter result turned out to involve
the Feit–Thompson theorem, and
part of the classification of finite simple groups (though not the full force of it).
The latter case (of a non-cyclic outer-automorphism group),
which can only occur for groups of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
and
is only really an issue in characteristic
p=2
,
is handled in §
10.2
.
Finally, §
10.3
combines the reductions in
the rest of §
10
to prove
Theorem
C
.

2.1.2
,

Example
8.12
.

8.9
, and

2.
Notation and recollections

Throughout the paper,

k
is an arbitrary field.

We write
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
for a fixed (but arbitrary) algebraic closure of
k
,

{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
for the maximal separable extension field of
k
inside
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
,
and

\Gal(k)
for the automorphism group of
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}/k
,
which we may, and do, identify with the Galois group of
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}/k
by
restriction to
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
When we refer to an algebraic extension
E/k
, we will always
assume that
E
is contained in the fixed algebraic closure
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.

We will use “
k
-scheme” as shorthand for
“affine scheme of finite type over
k
”,
and “
k
-group” as shorthand for
“affine group scheme of finite type over
k
”,
but we do not require that our
k
-groups be smooth or connected.

For each positive integer
n
,
we write
\mu_{n}
for the group scheme

\Spec(k[X]/(x^{n}-1))
of

n
th roots of unity.

When parsing a symbol involving
subscripts and superscripts not otherwise
disambiguated by parentheses,
such as, in later notation,

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma}
, or

{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
,
the subscript should be parsed before the superscript.
Thus,

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
means

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
, not
({\widetilde{G}}^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
, when
they differ;

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma}
means

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})^{\Gamma}
, not

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{der}}}
, when
they differ; and

{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
means

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
, not

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

(which is usually meaningless).

2.1.
Root systems, root data, and actions

In this subsection, we do not need a field;
instead, we are concerned only with
abstract root systems and root data.
Let
\Psi=(X^{*},\Phi,X_{*},\Phi^{\vee})
be a root datum.

We write

{a}\longmapsto{a^{\vee}}
for the duality map between
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\vee}
,
and
W(\Phi)
for the Weyl group of
\Phi
.
We say that a subset
\Phi^{\prime}
of
\Phi
is

integrally closed
if the intersection with
\Phi
of
the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-span of
\Phi^{\prime}
is
again
\Phi^{\prime}
(in which case
\Phi^{\prime}
is itself a root system).

Definition 2.1.1
.

Two elements of
\Phi
are called

strongly orthogonal
if
they are not proportional, and
neither their sum nor their difference belongs to
\Phi
.
A subset of
\Phi
is called

strongly orthogonal
if
every pair of distinct elements is strongly orthogonal.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

Suppose that

•

\Phi_{1}
is a (possibly non-reduced, possibly not closed) sub-root system of
\Phi
,

•

\Phi_{1}^{+}
is a set of positive roots for
\Phi_{1}
,
and

•

a\in\Phi_{1}
is simple with respect to
\Phi_{1}^{+}
.

Then there is a system of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
for
\Phi

that contains
\Phi_{1}^{+}
,
and for which either
a
is simple with respect to
\Phi^{+}
,
or
a
is divisible in
\Phi
and
a/2
is simple with respect to
\Phi^{+}
.

Proof (John Stembridge).
Write
X^{\vee}=\mathbb{R}\Phi^{\vee}
,
and let
C_{1}
be the chamber for
\Phi_{1}
in
X^{\vee}
determined by
\Phi_{1}^{+}
.
The
0
-set of
a
is a wall of
C_{1}
.
Let
C
be a chamber for
\Phi
in
X^{\vee}
that is contained in
C_{1}
,
and has the
0
-set of
a
as a wall;
and let
\Phi^{+}
be the corresponding set of positive roots for
\Phi
.
∎

We will need to discuss Borel–de Siebenthal theory
(see
[
borel-desiebenthal
]
*§7, p. 216, Théorème 6)
in some of the detailed computations of §
10
.
This theory describes certain full-rank subgroups, but rests on
a classification of integrally closed subsystems, which we describe now.

Definition 2.1.3
.

Fix a system
\Delta
of simple roots, and
an element
\alpha\in\Delta
.
Write
\varpi^{\vee}
for
the fundamental coweight corresponding to
\alpha
,

\alpha_{0}
for the
\Delta(B,T)
-highest root
in the irreducible component of
\Phi(G,T)
containing
\alpha
, and

n=\left\langle\alpha_{0},\varpi^{\vee}\right\rangle
for
the coefficient of
\alpha
in
\alpha_{0}
.
Put
\Delta_{\alpha}=\Delta\cup\left\{-\alpha_{0}\right\}\smallsetminus\left\{\alpha\right\}
.
We call the integrally closed subsystem of
\Phi

generated by
\Delta_{\alpha}

the
Borel–de Siebenthal subsystem

associated to
(\Delta,\alpha)
, and we call

\Delta_{\alpha}
itself
the
Borel–de Siebenthal basis
.

Remark 2.1.4
.

(a)

Preserve the notation of Definition
2.1.3
.
The Borel–de Siebenthal subsystem associated to

(\Delta,\alpha)
is
precisely the set of all roots
\beta
in
\Phi
such that

\left\langle\beta,\varpi^{\vee}\right\rangle
is divisible by
n
,
or, equivalently, lies in
\left\{0,\pm n\right\}
.
It is easily verified that
the Borel–de Siebenthal basis is
actually a system of simple roots for
the Borel–de Siebenthal subsystem associated to
\alpha
.

(b)

With two minor corrections,
the maximal integrally closed subsystems of

\Phi
are described in
[
springer-steinberg:conj
]
*§4.5
in terms of
what we have called Borel–de Siebenthal subsystems.
First, in the notation there,
the maximal subsystem that they denote by

\langle h,a_{2},\dotsc,\widehat{a_{i}},\dotsc,a_{r}\rangle

should actually be

\langle h,a_{1},\dotsc,\widehat{a_{i}},\dotsc,a_{r}\rangle
.
That is, there should be only
one simple root omitted, not two.
Second, their result classifies the
maximal such subsystems only up to conjugacy in
the Weyl group.
With this caveat, we may say the following.
If
\Phi^{\prime}
is a maximal integrally closed subsystem of
\Phi

such that

{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\prime}
has finite index in
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi
, then there are
a system of simple roots
\Delta
for
\Phi
and
a root
\alpha\in\Delta
such that
the coefficient of
\alpha
in the

\Delta
-highest root of the irreducible component of
\alpha

containing
\Phi
is prime, and

\Phi^{\prime}
is the Borel–de Siebenthal subsystem associated to

(\Delta,\alpha)
.

Remark
2.1.5
is immediately motivated by
Remark
2.1.4
(
b
),
and, more particularly, by our needs in
Propositions

10.1.7
and

10.1.11
.

Remark 2.1.5
.

Suppose that
\Phi
is reduced and irreducible, and
admits an automorphism
\gamma
of prime order, say
p
.
By inspection

[
bourbaki:lie-gp+lie-alg_4-6
]
*Chapter VI, Plates I–IX,
we have that

\Phi
is of type

\mathsf{A}_{n}
,
\mathsf{D}_{n}
, or
\mathsf{E}_{6}
, and

p
equals
2
; or

\Phi
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
, and

p
equals
3
.
In each case,
p^{2}
does not divide
the order of the automorphism group.

We now consider two further possible pieces of information.
First,
if the group of diagram automorphisms of
\Phi
has
a nontrivial subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}
of order relatively prime to
p
that
is normalised by
\gamma
, then

p
equals
2
,
\Phi
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
, and
the natural map from
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle\ltimes\Gamma^{\prime}
to
the group of diagram automorphisms of
\Phi
is
an isomorphism.

Second,
if instead there is a simple root of
\Phi
whose
coefficient
\ell
in the highest root is
prime, then

\Phi
is of type

\mathsf{D}_{n}
or
\mathsf{E}_{6}
, and

\ell
equals
2
; or

\Phi
is of type

\mathsf{D}_{4}
or
\mathsf{E}_{6}
, and

\ell
equals
3
.
In particular, the only possibilities where

\ell
is different from
p
are

\mathsf{E}_{6}
, with
p=2
and
\ell=3
; and

\mathsf{D}_{4}
, with
p=3
and
\ell=2
.

Definition 2.1.6
.

Let
{\widetilde{\Psi}}
be a root datum,

\Gamma
an abstract group,
and

{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\Aut({\widetilde{\Psi}})}

a homomorphism.
We say that the action of
\Gamma
on
{\widetilde{\Psi}}
,
or sometimes by abuse of notation the pair
({\widetilde{\Psi}},\Gamma)
,
is
quasisemisimple
if there is a
system of positive roots in the root system of
{\widetilde{\Psi}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma
.

2.2.
Groups and actions

We will follow
[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Definition 5.5
in calling a homomorphism of
k
-groups a quotient map,
or just quotient, if
it is surjective and faithfully flat.
By
[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*
§A.12 and
Proposition 5.47,
a surjective homomorphism
{G}\longrightarrow{H}
of
k
-groups is
always a quotient map if
H
is smooth.
We shall frequently use this fact without explicit mention.

Let
G
be a
k
-group.

We denote by

G^{\circ}
the maximal connected subgroup of
G

(i.e., its identity component).
Passing to identity components commutes with base change,
in the sense that
(G^{\circ})_{E}
equals
(G_{E})^{\circ}
for
every field extension
E/k

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Proposition 1.34.

We denote by

G_{\textup{{sm}}}
the maximal smooth subgroup of
G

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Lemma C.4.1 and Remark C.4.2.
Many operations involving smoothing that one might expect to commute
actually do not.

First,

(G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
(which is smooth)
contains

(G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
(which is both smooth and connected), but

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Remark C.4.2 gives
an example showing that they need not be equal.
If
k
is perfect, then

(G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is
the maximal reduced subscheme of
G^{\circ}
, hence is connected,
so equals
(G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
.
Remember that, when there is a difference,
we will always understand
G_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}

to mean
(G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
,
not
(G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
Thus,
G_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is always
the maximal smooth, connected subgroup of
G
.

Second,

(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}

contains, but need not equal,

(G_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
We have by
[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Lemma C.4.1
that
(G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
(G_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
;
in particular, we have equality

(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}=(G_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
if

k
is perfect.

Definition 2.2.1
.

The
k
-group
G
is called
smoothable

if
(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
(G_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.

For example, every group of multiplicative type is smoothable

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Corollary A.8.2.
If
k
is perfect, then every
k
-group is smoothable.

Remark 2.2.2
.

If
G^{\circ}
is smoothable, then

((G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

((G^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}=((G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
, and so
is connected.
That is,
(G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is connected, hence equals

(G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
.
Therefore,
((G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals
((G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
.

Conversely, if

((G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals
((G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
, then

((G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, which is always contained in

((G^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}=(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
is contained in
((G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, so

(G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is contained in
(G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
.
Since the reverse containment always holds, we have equality

(G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}=(G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
.
Therefore,

((G^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

((G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=((G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}=((G^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
That is,
G^{\circ}
is smoothable.

Finally, if
G
is smoothable, then

((G_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=((G_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\circ}

equals

((G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
, so

G^{\circ}
is smoothable.
The converse of this statement does not hold.
We thank Sean Cotner for explaining the following example.
If
k
is imperfect and

t
is an element of
k^{\times}\smallsetminus(k^{\times})^{p}
, then
the group
G=\Spec k[X]/(X^{p^{2}}-tX^{p})
of

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*§1.57 has smoothable
identity component

G^{\circ}=\alpha_{p}=\Spec k[X]/(X^{p})
and

trivial
maximal smooth subgroup, while

(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}=\Spec k[X]/(X^{p}-\sqrt[p]{t}X)

is nontrivial.

The literature, including
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
,
digne-michel:non-connected
,
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
,
often works exclusively with smooth group schemes,
and so does not mention passage to the maximal smooth subgroup.
Moreover, since the maximal smooth subgroup of a
k
-group can
be unexpectedly small
(as seen, for example, in Remark
2.2.2
),
one often wants to base change to
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
before passing to
the maximal smooth subgroup.
Thus, for example, the reference to

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
in
[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Lemma 3.4
is really to the maximal smooth subgroup

((({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
of
the base-changed fixed-point group

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
Since this is now a
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
-group, it makes sense to ask
whether it is defined over
k
, and
the content of
[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Lemma 3.4 is that
it is.
Then it is easy to show that the descent to
k
must be
the maximal smooth subgroup
(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
of

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
; that is, that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable, in our sense.
In this paper, we always say explicitly when
we are performing base change or
passing to the maximal smooth subgroup.

We write
\underline{\Lie}(G)
for the Lie-algebra-valued functor

{A}\longmapsto{\ker({G(A[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}))}\longrightarrow{G(A)})}

on
k
-algebras
of
[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §4, 1.2 and 4.2, and put

\Lie(G)=\underline{\Lie}(G)(k)
.
Since
k
is a field, the canonical map from
the vector group

{A}\longrightarrow{\Lie(G)\otimes_{k}A}

to
\underline{\Lie}(G)
is an isomorphism

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §4, Proposition 4.8 and (b), and
we freely treat it as equality.

If
G
acts on a
k
-scheme
Z
, and

X
is a closed subscheme of
Z
,
then we write
\stab_{G}(X)
for
the stabilizer of
X
in
G

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 1.81.
If
G
is acting on
Z=G
by conjugation,
we write
N_{G}(X)
in place of
\stab_{G}(X)
.

If
H
is a
k
-group acting on
G
, then we write
G^{H}
for
the
H
-fixed-point subgroup of
G
, i.e.,
the maximal subgroup of
G
on which

H
acts trivially

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Theorem 7.1.
For all
a\in{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(H)
, we will permit ourselves to write

\underline{\Lie}(G)_{a}
for the vector group

{A}\longmapsto{\Lie(G)_{a}\otimes_{k}A}
.
By
[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §4, Proposition 2.5,
we have that
\Lie(G^{H})
equals
\Lie(G)^{H}
.
If
H
is a subgroup of
G
, then we write

C_{G}(H)
for the centralizer of
H
in
G

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Proposition 1.92.
Thus, if
H
is a subgroup of
G
acting on
G
by conjugation,
then
C_{G}(H)
equals
G^{H}
.
We will sometimes allow ourselves more generally
to write
C_{G}(H)
for
G^{H}

whenever
H
is a
k
-group acting on
G
.

Write
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(G)=\Hom(G,\GL_{1})

for the abstract group of characters of
G
,
and
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(G)=\Hom(\GL_{1},G)

for the set of cocharacters of
G
.

If
G
is a
k
-group equipped with
a representation on
X
, in the sense of

[
jantzen:alg-reps
]
*Part I, §2.7,
then we write
X^{G}
for the
G
-fixed subspace of
X

and, for every
a\in{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(G)
,

X_{a}
for the
a
-weight space for
G
in
X

[
jantzen:alg-reps
]
*Part I, §2.10(1, 1
′
).
If
X^{\prime}
is a subset of
X
, then, as in

[
jantzen:alg-reps
]
*Part I, 2.12(1)
(except that we use
C
in place of
Z
), we write

C_{G}(X^{\prime})
for the fixer of
X^{\prime}
in
G
.
We have by

[
jantzen:alg-reps
]
*Part I, 7.11(10) that

\Lie(C_{G}(X^{\prime}))
equals

C_{\Lie(G)}(X^{\prime})
, i.e.,
the annihilator in
\Lie(G)
of
X^{\prime}
.

We write
\Phi(X,G)
for
the set of nonzero weights of
G
on
X
, i.e.,
those nonzero
a\in{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(G)
such that

X_{a}
is nonzero.
This will be most interesting when
G
is a torus, but
we do not require this.

If
G=S
is a group of multiplicative type,
then
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)
is a lattice, and
it depends only on
S_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Put
V(S)={\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
.

If
S
acts on
G
, then
we write
\Phi(G,S)
for
\Phi(\Lie(G),S)
, even if

G
is not smooth.

As usual, we call a
k
-group
G

reductive
if
it is smooth and
there is no nontrivial smooth, connected, unipotent, normal subgroup
of
G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
If
G
is reductive, then
we write
G_{\textup{{ad}}}
for its adjoint quotient
G/Z(G)
, and

G_{\textup{{sc}}}
for the simply connected cover of
G_{\textup{{der}}}
.

If
S
is a torus in
G
,
then we put
W(G,S)=N_{G}(S)/C_{G}(S)
, even if
this is not a Coxeter group.

Lemma 2.2.3
.

Suppose that
G
is connected, reductive, and quasisplit.
Let
S
be a maximal split torus in
G
, and put

T=C_{G}(S)
.
Then
W(G,S)
is contained in
W(G,T)
, and

W(G,S)(k)
equals
W(G,T)(k)
.

Proof.
We have that
C_{G}(S)
and
C_{G}(T)
both equal
T
,
so
N_{G}(S)
normalizes
C_{G}(S)=T
and hence lies in

N_{G}(T)
.
Thus,
W(G,S)=N_{G}(S)/T
is a subgroup of

W(G,T)=N_{G}(T)/T
.

Every automorphism of
T
preserves
its maximal split torus
S
.
(Remember that “automorphism of
T
”
means

“automorphism of
T
defined over
k
.”)
Thus, if
w
belongs to
W(G,T)(k)
and

n
is a representative of
w
in
N_{G}(T)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, then

n
normalizes
S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence belongs to

N_{G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}(S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})=N_{G}(S)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Therefore, the image
w
of
n
in
W(G,T)(k)
belongs to

N(G,S)(k)
.
∎

Put
Z(G)=C_{G}(G)
.
Thus, for example, if the characteristic exponent

p
of
k
is greater than
1
, then

Z(\SL_{p})
is the infinitesimal group scheme
\mu_{p}
, not
its underlying maximal smooth group scheme, which is trivial.

For every
k
-algebra
A
,
write
\Aut(G_{A})
for
the abstract group of automorphisms of

G_{A}\mathrel{:=}\Spec(k[G]\otimes_{k}A)
; and then
write
\underline{\Aut}(G)
for
the automorphism group functor,
defined by
\underline{\Aut}(G)(A)=\Aut(G_{A})

for all
k
-algebras
A

[
SGA-3.1
]
*Exposé I, no. 1.7, p. 10).
Write
\Int
for the inner-automorphism map

{G}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Aut}(G)}

given by

{g}\longmapsto{({h}\longmapsto{ghg^{-1}})}
,

\underline{\Inn}(G)
for the (sheaf-theoretic) image of
G
, so that

\Int
is an isomorphism of
G_{\textup{{ad}}}=G/Z(G)
onto
\underline{\Inn}(G)
, and

\Inn(G)=\underline{\Inn}(G)(k)
.
Thus,
\Int(G(k))
is contained in, but need not equal,

\Inn(G)
.
We shall call an automorphism of
G

inner
if
it belongs to
\Inn(G)
, and
outer
if
it is trivial or does not belong to
\Inn(G)
.

Write
[\cdot,\cdot]
for the commutator map

{G\times G}\longrightarrow{G}

given by

{(g,h)}\longmapsto{ghg^{-1}h^{-1}}
, and

G_{\textup{{der}}}
for the derived subgroup of
G
, i.e.,
the subgroup generated by the image of
[\cdot,\cdot]
.

Remark 2.2.4
.

If
G
is connected and reductive,
then the group functor
\underline{\Aut}(G)
is
an affine group scheme over
k
, but is

not
of finite type over
k
unless
G
is semisimple;
and

\underline{\Inn}(G)
is the identity component
\underline{\Aut}(G)^{\circ}

[
SGA-3.3
]
*Exposé XXIV, Théoreme 1.3(i) and Corollaire 1.7.

Remark 2.2.5
.

If
G
is smooth and
all elements of
G({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
are semisimple, then

G
is linearly reductive, in the sense of

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Definition 12.52.
This follows from

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 17.25 and
the description of linearly reductive groups in

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, Proposition 3.4

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*
Corollary 22.43
.

If
G
is reductive
and

S
is a maximal split torus in
G
,
then there is a root datum

\Psi(G,S)\mathrel{:=}({\mathbf{X}}^{*}(S),\Phi(G,S),{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S),\Phi^{\vee}(G,S))
,

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Theorem C.2.15.

A minimal
parabolic
subgroup
B
of
G
containing
S
determines
a system of simple roots
\Delta(B,S)
in
\Psi(G,S)
, and
we write
\Psi(G,B,S)
for the associated based root datum.

Definition 2.2.6
.

A
reductive datum
is a triple

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma,\varphi)
, where

{\widetilde{G}}
is a connected, reductive
k
-group,

\Gamma
is a smooth
k
-group, and

{\varphi}\colon{\Gamma\times{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
is
an action of
\Gamma
on
{\widetilde{G}}
.
We usually denote a datum by
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
, leaving

\varphi
implicit.

A morphism
from one reductive datum
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
to
another
({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})

is a pair of group homomorphisms

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}}
and

{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\Gamma^{\prime}}
with
obvious compatibility properties.
In this paper,
we will only consider the case where

\Gamma
equals
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
the morphism
{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is the identity, so
we omit it from the notation.

Although it is perfectly fine if the group
\Gamma
in
a reductive datum
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is constant,
which is the original motivating case for this paper,
we do
not
assume this, and do
not

identify
\Gamma
with its set of
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
-points.
If
\gamma
is an element of
\Gamma(k)
, then

\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
always means
the
algebraic
subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
generated by
\gamma
,

not
an
abstract
subgroup of
\Gamma(k)
.
This leads to surprising notation such as

\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k)
.
If
\gamma
has finite order, then this is indeed
the abstract subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
generated by
\gamma
;
but, otherwise,
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k)
is usually strictly bigger than
that abstract subgroup.

Definition 2.2.7
.

Let

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
be a reductive datum.

(a)

A
Borel–torus pair
in
{\widetilde{G}}
is a pair
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})

of a Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}

(i.e., a subgroup such that
{\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a maximal
smooth, connected, solvable subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
)
and a maximal torus
{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{B}}
.
A
parabolic–Levi
pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
is a pair
({\widetilde{P}},{\widetilde{M}})
,
where
{\widetilde{P}}
is a parabolic subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}

(i.e.,
{\widetilde{P}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
contains a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
)
and

{\widetilde{M}}
is a Levi component of
{\widetilde{P}}

(i.e., a subgroup such that
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
maps isomorphically onto
the maximal reductive quotient of
{\widetilde{P}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
).
The group
{\widetilde{G}}
always admits at least one parabolic–Levi pair,
the trivial pair
({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{G}})
,
but need not admit a Borel–torus pair; it admits such a pair
if and only if

{\widetilde{G}}
is quasisplit.

(b)

We say that the action, or again sometimes
by abuse of notation
the pair
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
, is

quasisemisimple
if there is a
Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma
.
(This is equivalent to saying that

{\widetilde{G}}
is quasisplit,
there is a
\Gamma
-stable maximal torus
{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
the action of

\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
on the root datum

\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

is quasisemisimple.
See Remark
6.7
.)
An automorphism
\gamma
of
{\widetilde{G}}
is called

quasisemisimple
, or said to

act quasisemisimply
on
{\widetilde{G}}
,
if

({\widetilde{G}},\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle)
is quasisemisimple.

(c)

We say that
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is
exceptional
if
it is quasisemisimple, and
there is
a maximal split torus
S
in
G\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
such that
some root in
\Phi(G,S)
is
divisible in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
An automorphism
\gamma
of
{\widetilde{G}}
is called

exceptional
, or said to

act exceptionally
on
{\widetilde{G}}
, if

({\widetilde{G}},\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle)
is exceptional.

Remark 2.2.8
.

If
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is a reductive datum, then
Remark
2.2.4
gives that
the action of
\Gamma^{\circ}
factors uniquely through

{\Int}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Aut}({\widetilde{G}})}

to give a map
{\Gamma^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}
.

(a)

There is an action of

\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
on
the set of almost-simple components of
{\widetilde{G}}
.

(b)

If
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is preserved by
\Gamma
, then
the image of
\Gamma^{\circ}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}

normalizes
{\widetilde{B}}
and
{\widetilde{T}}
, hence
is contained in
{\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}})
.
Since it is smooth and connected, the image is a torus.
More generally, if we
write
\Gamma^{\prime}
for the subgroup of
\Gamma
that
acts on
{\widetilde{G}}
by inner automorphisms, then
the image of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
need not

be
a torus, but is still contained in
{\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}})
.

(c)

Suppose that

{\widetilde{G}}
is quasi-split and

\Gamma
is a split torus, or,
slightly more generally, a torus whose image under

{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}
is split.
Then the image of
\Gamma
is
contained in a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
that
is contained in a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
,
and this Borel–torus pair pulls back to
a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma
.
That is,
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.

(d)

The conclusion of (
c
)
can fail if
we relax the assumption that

\Gamma
is a torus to
the assumption that
it is of multiplicative type.
Indeed, if
p
is odd, then
the subgroup of
\PGL_{2}
generated by the images there of

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

and

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is of multiplicative type, but is not contained in
any torus, and so does not preserve any Borel–torus pair
in
\PGL_{2}
.

3.
Statements of the main theorems

We introduce the common
Notation
3.1
to be used in
the statement of each of our main theorems.
In the terminology of Definition
2.2.6
,
the conditions on
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
\Gamma
say precisely that

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is a reductive datum over
k
.

Notation 3.1
.

Let

k
be a field,

{\widetilde{G}}
a connected, reductive
k
-group, and

\Gamma
a smooth
k
-group acting on
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Put
G=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

Recall the notion of the spherical building of a reductive group from
[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§2.
See §
4.4
for more details.

Theorem
A
proves our most comprehensive results,
including the notoriously ill-behaved case of
certain outer actions on
groups of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
in characteristic
2
,
under the assumption that
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.
Theorems
B
and
C

generalize this in two ways.

Theorem
B
shows what happens if we replace
quasisemisimplicity of
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
by
the weaker hypothesis of
quasisemisimplicity of

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
.
In this case, we know that the analogue of
Theorem
A
(
2
)
does not hold; see
Example
8.12
, due to
Alex Bauman and Sean Cotner.
We describe the possible failure of reductivity in
Theorem
B
(
1
),
and provide many necessary and sufficient conditions for
the stronger hypothesis of rational quasisemisimplicity to hold in
Theorem
B
(
2
).
(It would be pleasant to be able to replace the two-part condition
Theorem
B
(
2
)(
2c
) by just
the condition that
G
is reductive, but
Example
8.13
shows that
this is not sufficient.)
Because of this list of necessary and sufficient conditions,
Theorem
B
subsumes
Theorem
A
, except for
Theorem
A
(
3
).
That part does not literally make sense in
the general setting of Theorem
B
, but
we do have Conjecture
9.2

concerning an appropriate replacement.

Therem
C
still further weakens our assumption to
“local quasisemisimplicity”, where we require only that
every point of
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
acts
quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
; but
this is too general for our techniques to handle, so
we must impose a smoothness requirement for
the action of each element of
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
.
(This assumption is vacuously satisfied unless
we are in characteristic
2
, and moreover
encounter the difficult case mentioned above of
a certain kind of outer action on
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
.)
This restriction is not a failure of our proof techniques, but
an indication of a genuine counterexample to
Theorem
C
(
2
).
See Example
10.3.5
.
We do not know whether the analogue of
Theorem
A
(
0
)
holds in the situation of
Theorem
C
if
we drop the extra smoothness assumption from the latter.
Other than this, Theorem
C

subsumes Theorem
A
.

Theorem
A
overlaps significantly with

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5.
Much of our proof technique is quite different
(although we do wind up citing

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5 itself in
the proof of
Proposition
7.1
(
a
),
on which Theorem
A
(
2
)
relies), and
our result is somewhat more general, in that
it allows for an action by an algebraic group, rather than
a finite group.

Remark 3.2
.

Theorem
A
also corrects an error in

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5,
which should have included the smoothness hypothesis
of Theorem
C
,
which, as remarked above, is automatic in most cases.
Without such a hypothesis, the group
G
of
Notation
3.1
is not necessarily reductive,
and one instead has the weaker conclusion of
Theorem
B
(
1
).
The error comes from the citation of

[
lemaire:twisted-characters
]
*Théorème 4.6,
whose proof rests on
Proposition 4.5 and thence on Lemme 4.5 of the same reference.
The last result asserts in particular that,
over a separably closed field,
a unipotent element of a reductive group lies in
a (rational) Borel subgroup.
This can fail for the so called

bad
unipotent elements, in the sense of

[
tits:unipotent-2
]
*§3.1.
For example, if

k
is an imperfect field of characteristic
2
and

t\in k^{\times}
is a nonsquare in
k^{\times}
, then
the image of

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
t&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(k)
is
bad

[
tits:unipotent-2
]
*Example 3.5, and so
is a counterexample to

[
lemaire:twisted-characters
]
*Lemme 4.5.

We will prove Theorem
A
in
§§
6
,
7
, as we
build up a considerable ‘equivariant’ structure theory for
reductive groups with quasisemisimple action.
Specifically,
Theorem
A
(
0
,
2
)
is proven at the end of
§
6
;
Theorem
A
(
1
)
is proven after
Corollary
7.8
; and
Theorem
A
(
3
)
is proven after
Proposition
7.5
.
We are then ready for Theorem
C
,
the essential idea of which is to combine
Theorem
A
with results of

[
prasad-yu:actions
]
.

Theorem A
.

Let
k
,
\Gamma
,
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
G
be as in
Notation
3.1
.
Suppose that
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.

(0)

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.

(1)

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
equals

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
unless

p
equals
2
and
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is exceptional.

(2)

G
is connected and reductive.

(3)

The functorial map from the spherical building
\mathscr{S}(G)
of
G

to the spherical building
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
of
{\widetilde{G}}

identifies
\mathscr{S}(G)
with

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.

Theorem B
.

Let
k
,
\Gamma
,
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
G
be as in
Notation
3.1
.
Suppose that
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is quasisemisimple.

(1)

G
is an extension of
a reductive group by
a split unipotent group.

(2)

The following statements are equivalent.

(a)

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is quasisemisimple.

(b)

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.

(c)

G
is reductive, and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(G)
is of multiplicative type.

(d)

There is a torus
T
in
G
such that

T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a maximal torus in
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

(e)

There are a

\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-stable maximal torus
{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and a

\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
-stable Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
containing

{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.

Since quasisemisimplicity of an action is
preserved under base change,
the assumption of quasisemisimplicity of
({\widetilde{G}}_{E},\Gamma_{E})
is
weaker the larger
E
is.
Thus, one might wonder if Theorem
B
could
be made stronger by weakening its hypothesis to require
quasisemisimplicity of
({\widetilde{G}}_{E},\Gamma_{E})
for
some field extension
E/k
, not necessarily algebraic.
In this case, we would have that
\mathscr{B}^{\Gamma}(E)

was nonempty, where
\mathscr{B}
is the variety of
Borel–torus pairs in
{\widetilde{G}}

(a homogeneous variety for which point stabilisers are
maximal tori); so the Nullstellensatz would give that

\mathscr{B}^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
was also nonempty, hence that

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
was also quasisemisimple.
That is, we would not gain any additional power from
such a replacement.

Theorem C
.

Let
k
,
\Gamma
,
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
G
be as in
Notation
3.1
.
Suppose, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
and

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.

(1)

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
equals

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

(2)

G
is reductive.

(3)

The functorial map from the spherical building
\mathscr{S}(G)
of
G

to the spherical building
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
of
{\widetilde{G}}

identifies
\mathscr{S}(G)
with

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.

Lemma
4.4.2
will give a version of
Theorems

A
(
3
)
and

C
(
3
)
that
does not require passing to
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
to identify the image of
\mathscr{S}(G)
.

Remark 3.3
.

Let
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
be a reductive datum over
k
such that
every element of
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is semisimple.
Then the hypotheses of Theorem
C

are satisfied

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 7.5.

In this case,
\Gamma
is linearly reductive
by Remark
2.2.5
.
Thus
Theorem
C
(
1
)
can be strengthened to the statement that
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
is
smooth, not just smoothable

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2); and
Theorem
C
(
2
) follows from

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.12.
We do not know if
Theorem
C
(
3
)
has already appeared in the literature in this setting, but
its special case where

\Gamma
is generated by a single inner automorphism is

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*Proposition 5.1.

4.
Generalities

Throughout this section, we continue with the
field
k
of characteristic exponent
p
from
§
2
.
Let
{\widetilde{G}}
be a smooth, connected
k
-group.
We will assume in §
4.3
that

{\widetilde{G}}
is reductive, but we do not do so yet.

4.1.
Fixed points

We will soon
(after Lemma
4.1.1
)
take
\Gamma
to be a smooth
k
-group acting on
{\widetilde{G}}
,
but we do not do so quite yet.

The proof of Lemma
4.1.1
is essentially
contained in
[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5.
Compare Corollary
4.1.2
to

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.14(1).

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite abstract subgroup of
\Aut({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that
is preserved by
\Gal(k)
.
Suppose that

{\widetilde{Z}}
is a finite, central subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
such that

{\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is preserved by
\Gamma
.
Write
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
and
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma}
for the
descents
to
k
of the
\Gal(k)
-stable groups

{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma}
and
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma}
.
Then the map

{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}

is an isogeny.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume,
upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.

It is clear that the kernel of

{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}

is finite, so we need only show that
the map is surjective.

Put

{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}={\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}}
,

G=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
and

G^{\prime}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Write
\phi
for the quotient map
{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}}
.
The action of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}
on
{\widetilde{G}}
restricts to
an action of
G^{\prime}
on
G
,
so that
\phi(G)
is normal in
G^{\prime}
.
Then we need to show that
G^{\prime}/\phi(G)
is trivial.

Since
G^{\prime}
is smooth and connected, so is
G^{\prime}/\phi(G)
.
Thus, since
k
is separably closed,
it suffices to show that
(G^{\prime}/\phi(G))(k)
is finite.
Since
\phi(G)
is smooth and
k
is separably closed,
we have that

{G^{\prime}(k)}\longrightarrow{(G^{\prime}/\phi(G))(k)}
is surjective.
Since this map is trivial on
\phi(G(k))
, hence factors through

{G^{\prime}(k)}\longrightarrow{G^{\prime}(k)/\phi(G(k))}
,
it suffices to show that

G^{\prime}(k)/\phi(G(k))
is finite.

Since
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k)
equals
{\widetilde{G}}(k)^{\Gamma}
, and
analogously for
{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}
, we have
the exact sequence

\textstyle{{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\Gamma,{\widetilde{Z}}(k)).}

Since
\Gamma
and
{\widetilde{Z}}(k)
are finite,
so is

H^{1}(\Gamma,{\widetilde{Z}}(k))
;
so
({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}(k)/\phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k))
is finite.
It thus suffices to show that
the kernel of

{G^{\prime}(k)/\phi(G(k))}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}(k)/\phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k))}

is finite.
The kernel is

(\phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k))\cap G^{\prime}(k))/\phi(G(k))
,
which is contained in
\phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k))/\phi(G(k))
.
This latter is the image under
\phi
of

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k)/G(k)=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}(k)/({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}(k)=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}/({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})(k)=\pi_{0}(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})(k)
,
which is finite.
Thus,
G^{\prime}(k)/\phi(G(k))
is finite, as desired.
∎

For the remainder of §
4.1
,
let
\Gamma
be a
k
-group acting on
{\widetilde{G}}
.

The linear reductivity hypothesis of
Corollary
4.1.2
is satisfied whenever

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is a quasisemisimple reductive datum over
k
,
or even just if

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is quasisemisimple,
by Remark
2.2.8
(
b
),
so this result may be considered an analogue of

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Lemma 9.2(a).
The main difference between
Lemma
4.1.1
and
Corollary
4.1.2
is that,
in the former,
\Gamma
is a finite abstract group, whereas
in the latter,
\Gamma
is an algebraic group with
a condition imposed on its identity component.

Corollary 4.1.2
.

Suppose that the image of

\Gamma^{\circ}
in

\underline{\Aut}({\widetilde{G}})
is
linearly reductive;

{\widetilde{Z}}
is a central subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is preserved by
\Gamma
; and

{\widetilde{Z}}
is finite, or
{\widetilde{G}}
is reductive.
Then

{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}

is a quotient map.

Proof.
Suppose first that
{\widetilde{Z}}
is finite.
We have by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2)
that
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
and
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
are smooth,
and by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.14(1)
that

{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma^{\circ}})^{\circ}=(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}

is surjective.
Since

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
equals

({(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\pi_{0}(\Gamma)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
and analogously for

{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}}
,
we may replace

{\widetilde{G}}
by
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,

{\widetilde{Z}}
by its intersection with
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
and

\Gamma
by
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Then Lemma
4.1.1
gives the result.

Now drop the assumption that
{\widetilde{Z}}
is finite, and
suppose instead that
{\widetilde{G}}
is reductive.
We use this assumption only to conclude that

{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
is a torus.
By rigidity of tori

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 12.37,
the action of
\Gamma
on

{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
factors through an action of
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)
.
Let
E/k
be a finite, separable extension such that

({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E}
is split and

\pi_{0}(\Gamma)_{E}
is constant.
Since
\Gal(E/k)\ltimes\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(E)
is finite,
and since
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}}\cdot{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
is
a (
\Gal(E/k)\ltimes\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(E)
)-stable subspace of

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
,
we have that
there is a (
\Gal(E/k)\ltimes\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(E)
)-stable complement

V

to

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}}\cdot{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
in

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
.
Write
{\widetilde{A}}
for the quotient of
{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
such that

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{A}}_{E})
is
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E})\cap V
.
Since
{\widetilde{A}}
is a quotient of
{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, it
comes equipped with a quotient map
{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{A}}}
.
Then
{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{A}}\times{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}}}
is
a
\Gamma
-equivariant, central isogeny, so
Lemma
4.1.1
gives that

{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{A}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}\times(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}

is also a central isogeny.
The result follows.
∎

Corollary 4.1.3
.

Preserve the hypotheses and notation of
Corollary
4.1.2
.
If
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma}
is smooth, then

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
equals

({\widetilde{Z}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

Proof.
Corollary
4.1.2
gives that

{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})^{\circ}}

is surjective.
Since the image of
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
in

{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}}
lies in

(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
, it follows that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is contained in

{\widetilde{Z}}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, hence in
its intersection

{\widetilde{Z}}^{\Gamma}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
with

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
, hence in its identity component

({\widetilde{Z}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
The reverse containment is obvious.
∎

Corollary 4.1.4
.

Preserve the hypotheses and notation of Corollary
4.1.2
.
If
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable, then

(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}})^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.
The converse holds if
({\widetilde{Z}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is also smoothable,
which is automatic if
{\widetilde{G}}
is reductive.

Proof.
Put
{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}={\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{Z}}
.
Write
\pi
for the quotient map

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}}
.

We make a few general observations.
If
{\widetilde{H}}
is a connected subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
then
{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}^{\circ}
is connected, and

{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}/{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}^{\circ}
is a quotient of
the étale group
{\widetilde{Z}}/{\widetilde{Z}}^{\circ}
, hence étale.
The characterization of the identity component of a group as
the unique connected, normal subgroup with étale quotient

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Proposition 1.31(a)
shows that

{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}^{\circ}
equals
({\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}})^{\circ}
.
Similarly, if
{\widetilde{H}}
is a smooth subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
then
{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}_{\textup{{sm}}}
is smooth, and

{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}/{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}_{\textup{{sm}}}
is a quotient of
the infinitesimal group
{\widetilde{Z}}/{\widetilde{Z}}_{\textup{{sm}}}
, hence infinitesimal.
Although it is not true in general that
the maximal smooth subgroup of a group is
the unique smooth, normal subgroup with infinitesimal quotient,
this fails only in one direction
(the maximal smooth subgroup need not be normal);
if
a group has a smooth, normal subgroup with infinitesimal quotient, then
that subgroup is the maximal smooth subgroup.
Therefore,
({\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
{\widetilde{H}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
Analogous reasoning works over
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.

Corollary
4.1.2
gives that

\pi(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
equals
(({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
and

\pi(({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
equals

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Remark
2.2.2
gives that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable if and only if

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
and analogously for
{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}
and
{\widetilde{Z}}
.
Thus, if
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable, then we have
the equalities

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=\pi(({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})=\pi((({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}{}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},

so that
({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}{}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.

If
({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}{}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable, then we analogously have
the equality

\pi(({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})=\pi((({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}),

so

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
Several equalities follow:

•

of the groups

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
and

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\cdot{\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}

of
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
-fixed points;

•

then of their maximal smooth subgroups

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
and

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\cdot({\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
;

•

then of their identity components

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
and

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\cdot({\widetilde{Z}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

If additionally
({\widetilde{Z}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable, then
this shows that

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
equals

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\cdot(({\widetilde{Z}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, so that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.
∎

4.2.
Semisimple elements in groups and algebras, and
their centralizers

Lemma
4.2.1
is related to

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2).

Lemma 4.2.1
.

If

\mathfrak{s}
is a commutative subalgebra of
\Lie(G)

such that all elements of
\mathfrak{s}
are semisimple,
then

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})
is smooth, and
there is a maximal torus
T
in
G
such that

\mathfrak{s}
is contained in
\Lie(T)
.

Proof.
First suppose that
k
is algebraically closed, and
reason by induction on
\dim(\mathfrak{s})
.
If the dimension is
0
, then
\mathfrak{s}
, and
the result, are trivial.
Thus we may, and do, suppose that the dimension is positive,
choose a codimension-
1
subspace
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
of

\mathfrak{s}
, and assume that we have
already proven the result for
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
.

In particular, there is a maximal torus
T_{1}
in
G
such that

\mathfrak{s}_{1}
is contained in
\Lie(T_{1})
.
Then
T_{1}
is contained in
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}_{1})
, so
the ranks of
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}_{1})
and
G
are equal, i.e.,
every torus that is maximal in
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}_{1})

remains maximal in
G
.
We have that
\mathfrak{s}
is contained in

C_{\Lie(G)}(\mathfrak{s}_{1})=\Lie(C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}_{1}))
;
and

C_{C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}_{1})}(\mathfrak{s})
equals

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})
.
Thus we may, and do, assume, upon
replacing
G
by
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}_{1})
, that

\mathfrak{s}_{1}
is contained in
\Lie(G)^{G}
.

Let
X_{1}
be an element of
\mathfrak{s}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{s}_{1}
,
so that
C_{G}(X_{1})
equals
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})
.
Then
[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 9.1(2) gives that

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})=C_{G}(X_{1})
is smooth, and

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 11.8 gives that

X_{1}
belongs to the Lie algebra of
a maximal torus
T
in
G
.
Since
T
is
G(k)
-conjugate to
T_{1}

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Theorem C.2.3,
and
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
is contained in
\Lie(T_{1})\cap\Lie(G)^{G}
,
we have that

\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, and hence

\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{1}\oplus kX_{1}
, is
contained in
\Lie(T)
.

Now drop the assumption that
k
is algebraically closed.
Obviously
\mathfrak{s}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is
still commutative, and
every element of it is a commuting sum of semisimple elements,
hence semisimple.
By the case of
k
algebraically closed, which
we have already proven, we have that

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=C_{G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}(\mathfrak{s}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{a}}}})

is smooth.

Now we argue as in
[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 11.8.
Let
T
be a maximal torus in
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})
, and write

C=C_{C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})}(T)^{\circ}
for the corresponding
Cartan subgroup of
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})
.
Then

C
is nilpotent by
[
borel:linear
]
*Corollary 11.7, so

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 10.6(3, 4) gives that

C_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is the direct product
T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\times U
, where

U
is the unipotent radical of
C_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
For each
X\in\mathfrak{s}
, we have that

X\otimes_{k}1\in\Lie(C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
belongs to

\Lie(C_{G}(\mathfrak{s})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}=\Lie(C_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, hence
may be written as a

commuting
sum

X\otimes_{k}1=X_{T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}+X_{U}
, where

X_{T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
belongs to
\Lie(T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, hence is semisimple, and

X_{U}
belongs to
\Lie(U)
, hence is nilpotent.
This is therefore the Jordan decomposition of
X\otimes_{k}1
, which
we already know is semisimple, so that

X\otimes_{k}1
equals
X_{T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
, hence belongs to

\Lie(T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})=\Lie(T)\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
,
so
X
belongs to
\Lie(T)
.
∎

Corollary
4.2.2
is related to

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*
Propositions A.8.12 and A.8.14(1).

Corollary 4.2.2
.

Suppose that
G
is reductive.
If
{G}\longrightarrow{G^{\prime}}
is a central quotient and

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is a commutative subalgebra of
\Lie(G^{\prime})

such that all elements of
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
are semisimple,
then the group

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
is reductive, and
the restriction of the quotient
{G}\longrightarrow{G^{\prime}}

to
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
is a quotient

{C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}}
.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume, upon replacing

k
by
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, that

k
is algebraically closed.
By Lemma
4.2.1
,
there is a torus, hence a maximal torus
T^{\prime}
, in
G^{\prime}
such that

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is contained in
\Lie(T^{\prime})
.

Write
T
for the pre-image of
T^{\prime}
in
G
.
Then
T
is a maximal torus, and, since
the restriction of

{\Lie(G)}\longrightarrow{\Lie(G^{\prime})}

to any root space for
T
in
\Lie(G)
is
an embedding in
\Lie(G^{\prime})
, we have that
inflation to
T
provides
a bijection of

\Phi(C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),T)=\Phi(C_{\Lie(G)}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),T)
with

\Phi(C_{\Lie(G^{\prime})}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),T^{\prime})=\Phi(C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),T^{\prime})
.
If
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Phi(C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),T^{\prime})
has
inflation
\alpha\in\Phi(C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),T)
, then
the image of
the root group
U_{\alpha}
for
T
in
G
is
the root group
U_{\alpha^{\prime}}
for
T^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
, which
is contained in

C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
.
Since the action of
G
on
\Lie(G^{\prime})
factors through
the map
{G}\longrightarrow{G^{\prime}}
, we have that

U_{\alpha}
is contained in
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
, hence in

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
.

The argument of

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 13.19 shows that

C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
is reductive,
and
[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 13.20 shows that
it is generated by

T^{\prime}
and those root groups for
T^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}

corresponding to
roots in
\Phi(C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),T^{\prime})
.
Thus

{C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}}

is surjective, hence a quotient map.
Then
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
is a smooth
(by Lemma
4.2.1
), connected
extension of the reductive group

C_{G^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
by

\ker({G}\longrightarrow{G^{\prime}})
, which is central in
G
and so
diagonalizable,
so
C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
is reductive.
∎

As remarked in §
2.1
,
we will need to discuss Borel–de Siebenthal theory in
some of the detailed computations of §
10
.
Although Remark
4.2.4
seems to be well known,
we could not find its contents stated in the form that we need them.

Definition 4.2.3
.

Suppose that
G
is quasisplit.
Fix a Borel–torus pair
(B,T)
in
G
,
let
S
be the maximal split torus in
T
, and fix
an element
a\in\Delta(B,S)
.
Write
\varpi^{\vee}
for
the fundamental coweight corresponding to
a
,

a_{0}
for the
\Delta(B,S)
-highest root
in the irreducible component of
\Phi(G,S)
containing
a
, and

n=\left\langle a_{0},\varpi^{\vee}\right\rangle
for
the coefficient of
a
in
a_{0}
.
Then we call
C_{G}(\varpi^{\vee}(\mu_{n}))^{\circ}

the Borel–de Siebenthal subgroup of
G

associated to
(B,T,a)
.

Remark 4.2.4
.

(a)

Preserve the notation and hypotheses of Definition
4.2.3
.
In the terminology of
Definition
2.1.3
, we have that

\Phi(C_{G}(\varpi^{\vee}(\mu_{n}))^{\circ},T)

is the Borel–de Siebenthal subsystem of

\Phi(G,S)
associated to

(\Delta(B,S),a)
.
In particular,
Remark
2.1.4
(
a
)
gives that

Z(C_{G_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\varpi^{\vee}(\mu_{n}))^{\circ})
equals

\varpi^{\vee}(\mu_{n})
.

(b)

Let
H
be a proper connected, reductive subgroup of
G
that
contains a maximally split, maximal torus
T
in
G
, and such that
the maximal split, central torus in
H
is
central in
G
.
Write
S
for the maximal split torus in
T
.
Then
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(H,S)
has finite index in
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(G,S)
.
If
\Phi(H,S)
is
integrally closed in
\Phi(G,S)

(which is automatic except if

p
equals
2
or
3

[
borel-tits:reductive-groups
]
*Remarque 2.5), then
Remark
2.1.4
(
b
)
gives that there are
a Borel subgroup
B
of
G
that contains
T
, and
a root
\alpha\in\Delta(B,S)
, such that
the coefficient of
\alpha
in the

\Delta(B,S)
-highest root in
the irreducible component of
\Phi(G,S)
containing
a
is
prime, and

H
is contained in
the Borel–de Siebenthal subgroup of
G
associated to

(B,T,\alpha)
.

Lemma
4.2.5
seems
to be well known, but we do not know a reference.

Lemma 4.2.5
.

Let
{\widetilde{G}}
be a connected, reductive group such that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
is isomorphic to a product of
projective general linear groups, and
let
\Gamma
be a smooth, diagonalizable subgroup of

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.
Then
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is a Levi subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.

Proof.
Suppose that

(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d})
is a vector of positive integers such that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
is isomorphic to

\prod_{i=1}^{d}\PGL_{n_{i}}
.
Write
\Gamma_{i}
for the projection of
\Gamma
on
the
i
th factor.
Since the pre-image of
\Gamma_{i}
in
\GL_{n_{i}}
is
contained in a split torus in
\GL_{n_{i}}

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Theorem 12.12,
we have that
\Gamma_{i}
itself is contained in
a split torus in
\PGL_{n_{i}}
, so

\Gamma\subseteq\prod\Gamma_{i}
is contained in
a split torus
{\widetilde{T}}
in
\prod\PGL_{n_{i}}={\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.
We may, and do, arrange, by enlarging
{\widetilde{T}}
, that
it is maximal.

The restriction to
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
of the adjoint quotient

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}

is surjective onto
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.14(1),
and we have shown that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
contains the kernel
Z({\widetilde{G}})
of
the quotient, so

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is the full pre-image in

{\widetilde{G}}
of
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
.
It thus suffices
to prove the result under the assumption that

{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint, hence isomorphic to

\prod\PGL_{n_{i}}
.
Since this isomorphism identifies

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
with

\prod(\PGL_{n_{i}}^{\Gamma_{i}})^{\circ}
,
we may, and do, work one factor at a time, and so assume that

{\widetilde{G}}
is (isomorphic to)
\PGL_{n}
.

We have
by
[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.14 that

{\widetilde{M}}\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is reductive.
Since
\Gamma
is contained in
{\widetilde{T}}
, hence in
{\widetilde{M}}
, we have that

\Gamma
is central in
{\widetilde{M}}
; so
we have the containments

{\widetilde{M}}\subseteq C_{\widetilde{G}}(Z({\widetilde{M}}))^{\circ}\subseteq C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma)^{\circ}={\widetilde{M}}
,
hence equality
{\widetilde{M}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(Z({\widetilde{M}}))^{\circ}
.
Put
{\widetilde{S}}=Z({\widetilde{M}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
so that
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}})
is a Levi subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
It suffices to show that
{\widetilde{M}}
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}})
.
Note that
Z({\widetilde{M}})/{\widetilde{S}}
is finite as
X^{*}({\widetilde{S}})
and
X^{*}(Z({\widetilde{M}}))
have equal rank;
let
n=|Z({\widetilde{M}})/{\widetilde{S}}|
.

Suppose
\alpha
belongs to
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(C_{{\widetilde{G}}}({\widetilde{S}}),{\widetilde{T}})
.
Then
\alpha
is trivial on
{\widetilde{S}}
, so

n\alpha
is trivial on
Z({\widetilde{M}})
;
that is,
n\alpha
belongs to
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{M}}))
, which
equals
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi({\widetilde{M}},{\widetilde{T}})

since
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint.
It follows that
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}}),{\widetilde{T}})/{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi({\widetilde{M}},{\widetilde{T}})
is finite.
But since
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})
is of type
\mathsf{A}
,
the torsion part of
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})/{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi({\widetilde{M}},{\widetilde{T}})
,
and hence that of
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}}),{\widetilde{T}})/{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi({\widetilde{M}},{\widetilde{T}})
, is trivial.
Thus
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi({\widetilde{M}},{\widetilde{T}})
equals
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}}),{\widetilde{T}})
,
so
{\widetilde{M}}
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}})
.
∎

4.3.
Induction of reductive data

We define a notion of induction of
reductive data that is adjoint to
the natural notion of restriction.
Our definition is motivated by that of induction for modules;
see
[
jantzen:alg-reps
]
*Part I, §3.3.
Filling in the details requires some background, which
we provide in Appendix
A
.

After Remark
4.3.3
, we will
fix a reductive datum
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
over
k
, but
we do not do so yet.

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
({\widetilde{G}}_{1},\Gamma_{1})
be a reductive datum over
k
, and

\Gamma
a smooth
k
-group admitting

\Gamma_{1}
as an open subgroup.
Write
\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
for
the group
k
-sheaf

\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,{\widetilde{G}}_{1})
,
which is a connected, reductive
k
-group by
Proposition
A.29
,
equipped with the action of
\Gamma
described in
Remark
A.17
.
We say that
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is
induced from
\Gamma_{1}
if
it arises in this way, up to isomorphism.
More generally, we may use the same notation for
any
k
-group
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
with
\Gamma_{1}
-action,
even if it is not connected and reductive.
Then
\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}(\cdot)
may be viewed as a functor
in a natural way; namely, if

{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
is another
k
-group with
\Gamma_{1}
-action and

{f_{1}}\colon{({\widetilde{G}}_{1},\Gamma_{1})}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{H}}_{1},\Gamma_{1})}
is a morphism, then we define

{\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}(f_{1})}\colon{(\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{1},\Gamma)}\longrightarrow{(\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{H}}_{1},\Gamma)}

to be the map

{\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,{\widetilde{G}}_{1})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,{\widetilde{H}}_{1})}

given by post-composition with
f_{1}
.

Remark 4.3.2
.

Preserve the notation of Definition
4.3.1
.
Lemma
A.21
shows that

\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{der}})
is
the derived subgroup of
\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
; that, if

{\chi}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{sc}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{1}}

is the simply connected cover of
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{der}}
, then

\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}(\chi)
is
the simply connected cover of

(\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{1})_{\textup{{der}}}
; and that, if

{\pi}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}}
is
the adjoint quotient of
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
, then

\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}(\pi)
is
the adjoint quotient of
\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
.

Remark 4.3.3
.

Preserve the notation of Definition
4.3.1
.
Suppose that
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
has a maximal torus
{\widetilde{T}}_{1}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma_{1}
.
Then Lemma
A.21
gives that

{\widetilde{T}}=\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{T}}_{1}
is
a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is preserved by
\Gamma
.
Remark
A.35
provides a
(
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
)-equivariant identification of

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
with

{\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})]\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma_{1}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})]}{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})
, and then dually of

{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
with

\Hom_{{\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma_{1}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})]}({\mathbb{Z}}[\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})],{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}))
.
Thus, for every

\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
and

{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
it makes sense to speak of
the element

\gamma\otimes{\widetilde{\alpha}}
of
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and
the element
(\gamma\otimes{\widetilde{\alpha}})^{\vee}
of

{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that vanishes at
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})

unless
\gamma^{\prime}
belongs to
\gamma\Gamma_{1}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
in which case it sends
\gamma^{\prime}
to

(\gamma^{\prime\,{-1}}\gamma{\widetilde{\alpha}})^{\vee}
.
With the notation of Remark
A.30
,
we have for each
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that

\{\sigma(\gamma)\otimes{\widetilde{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathopen{}\sigma\in\Gal(k),{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})\}

equals
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\gamma\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{1\,\gamma\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})
.
Thus
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
equals

\{\gamma\otimes{\widetilde{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathopen{}\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}}),{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})\}
.
Further,

{\gamma\otimes{\widetilde{\alpha}}}\longmapsto{(\gamma\otimes{\widetilde{\alpha}})^{\vee}}

realizes
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
as a root system in
the sublattice of
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that it spans.

For the remainder of §
4.3
, let

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
be a reductive datum over
k
.

Lemma 4.3.4
.

Let
{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
be a
smooth, connected, normal, semisimple subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Write
\Gamma_{1}
for the stabilizer of
{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
in
{\widetilde{G}}
.

(a)

The subgroup
\Gamma_{1}
of
\Gamma
is
open, and
the functor
\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}(\cdot)

on the category of
k
-groups equipped with an action of
\Gamma_{1}

is exact.

(b)

There is a unique map
{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}}

that restricts to the adjoint quotient of
{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
and
annihilates
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
It is
\Gamma_{1}
-equivariant, and
annihilates
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1})
.

Lemma
A.19
gives a map

{\psi}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}}

corresponding to the map

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}}
of (
b
).
Write
{\widetilde{N}}
for the smallest
\Gamma
-stable subgroup
of
{\widetilde{G}}
containing
{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
.

(3)

{\widetilde{N}}
is semisimple, and

{\widetilde{N}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is generated by those almost-simple components

{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
that
admit a
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
-conjugate contained in

{\widetilde{N}}_{1}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.

(4)

The restriction of
\psi
to
{\widetilde{N}}
is
a central isogeny onto its image.
The multiplication map

{\ker(\psi)_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}\times{\widetilde{N}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
is a central isogeny.

Proof.
Remark
2.2.8
(
a
) gives that

\Gamma_{1}
contains the identity component of
\Gamma
, hence
is open.
Since a sequence of

k
