-groups with
\Gamma_{1}
-, or
\Gamma
-, action is
exact if and only if it is exact as a sequence of
fppf group sheaves over
k
,
Corollary
A.26
gives that

\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}(\cdot)
is exact.
This shows (
a
).

If
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
is an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
that
admits a
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
-conjugate contained in
{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
,
then
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
is contained in
{\widetilde{N}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
On the other hand, the subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
generated by
all such almost-simple components
is preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, hence by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
;
contains
{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Theorem 21.51; and
is preserved by
\Gal(k)
, hence descends to
a
\Gamma
-stable subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that contains
{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
.
It is therefore precisely
{\widetilde{N}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
Thus
{\widetilde{N}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and so
{\widetilde{N}}
, is
smooth and connected.
This shows (
3
).

The classical structure theory of reductive groups

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*
Theorem 21.51 and Proposition 21.61(c)
shows that
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is the almost-direct product of

{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
and

C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})=(C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,
so there is a unique map

{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\,\textup{ad}}={\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}}

that restricts to the adjoint quotient on

{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
, and
annihilates
(C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
This is a stronger uniqueness statement than that asserted in
(
b
), but we still need to show existence.
Our stronger uniqueness statement implies that our map

{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}}
is fixed by

\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma_{1}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, hence
descends to a map as in (
b
).
Since

{\widetilde{G}}
equals
{\widetilde{N}}_{1}\cdot C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
we have that

C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1})
equals
Z({\widetilde{N}}_{1})\cdot C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
and so is
annihilated by this map.
This shows (
b
).

For each
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, we have the direct product

\prod\gamma({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}})

over all almost-simple components
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
.
Although replacing
\gamma
by a right
\Gamma_{1}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
-translate
can affect the order of the factors, it does not affect
the overall product.
Thus, it makes sense to consider the product

\prod_{\gamma\in(\Gamma/\Gamma_{1})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}\prod_{{\widetilde{G}}_{1}}\gamma({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}})
.
Lemma
A.21
allows us to identify

\psi
with the product map

{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}\longrightarrow{\prod_{\gamma\in(\Gamma/\Gamma_{1})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}\prod_{{\widetilde{G}}_{1}}\gamma({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}})}
,
where each component map

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\gamma({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}})}
is the canonical
projection on an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{ad}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
.
Again, the classical structure theory of reductive groups
shows that this map restricts to
a central isogeny of
{\widetilde{N}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
onto its image, and
annihilates all
almost-simple components of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
not contained in
{\widetilde{N}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,
which are therefore contained in

\ker(\psi_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=(\ker(\psi)_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
This shows (
4
).
∎

Lemma 4.3.5
.

Preserve the notation and hypotheses of
Lemma
4.3.4
.
Suppose further that

{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
is an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}
, and

\Gamma/\Gamma_{1}
is constant.
Then there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant, central isogeny

\phi
from

\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
onto

{\widetilde{N}}
such that

\psi\circ\phi
is
the adjoint quotient of
\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
.

Proof.
Regard the inclusion

{{\widetilde{N}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
as
an element of
\Hom_{\Gamma_{1}}({\widetilde{N}}_{1},{\widetilde{G}})
,
hence, by Lemma
A.20
,
a
\Gamma
-fixed element of

\Mor_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,\underline{\Hom}({\widetilde{N}}_{1},{\widetilde{G}}))
.

The inclusion

{(\Gamma/\Gamma_{1})(k)}\longrightarrow{(\Gamma/\Gamma_{1})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}

is a bijection, so,
for every
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
such that

\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
belong to distinct

\Gamma_{1}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
-cosets, we have that

\gamma{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
and
\gamma^{\prime}{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
are
the base changes to
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
of
distinct almost-simple factors of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
That is, the element of

\Mor_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,\underline{\Hom}({\widetilde{N}}_{1},{\widetilde{G}}))

corresponding to the inclusion
{{\widetilde{N}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
satisfies
the commutativity condition of
Corollary
A.34
, which therefore
produces a
\Gamma
-equivariant homomorphism

{\phi}\colon{\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
given by Equation (

∗

‣
A.34
).

Write
\iota
for the map

{{\widetilde{N}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}}
of
Definition
A.32
.
Equation (

∗

‣
A.34
) shows two things.
First, the image of
\phi_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is
contained in the product of the
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
-conjugates of

{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
, hence is contained in
{\widetilde{N}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
; but

\phi\circ\iota
is the identity, so the image of
\phi
is
a
\Gamma
-stable subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
containing
{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
, hence
containing
{\widetilde{N}}
.
That is, the image of
\phi
is precisely
{\widetilde{N}}
.
Second, the diagram

\textstyle{{\widetilde{N}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}}

commutes, so that
\psi\circ\phi
is the map
corresponding by functoriality to
the adjoint quotient

{{\widetilde{N}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{N}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}}
.
Remark
4.3.2
shows that

\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
is semisimple and

\psi\circ\phi
is
the adjoint quotient of

\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
, hence, in particular,
is surjective.
In particular, the kernel of
\phi
is central in
the semisimple group
\Ind_{\Gamma_{1}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{N}}_{1}
, hence
finite, so that
\phi
is a central isogeny onto its image.
∎

Corollary
4.3.6
can be re-phrased
informally as follows.
With the notation
\widehat{G}
introduced there,
if
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint, then
the various maps
\psi
of Lemma
4.3.4

piece together to a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\widehat{G}}
; whereas,
if
{\widetilde{G}}
is simply connected, then
the various maps
\phi
of Lemma
4.3.5

piece together to a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

{\widehat{G}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
.

Corollary 4.3.6
.

Suppose that
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)
is constant.
For each
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
-orbit
i
of
almost-simple components of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
fix a representative
{\widetilde{G}}_{i}
of
i
,
put
\Gamma_{i}=\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{i})
, and
let
{\psi_{i}}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\Ind_{\Gamma_{i}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{i\,\textup{ad}}}

and
{\phi_{i}}\colon{\Ind_{\Gamma_{i}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{i}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}

be the maps of Lemma
4.3.4
and
Lemma
4.3.5
.
Put
\widehat{G}=\prod_{i}\Ind_{\Gamma_{i}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{i}
.
If
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint, then the map
(\psi_{i})_{i}

is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\widehat{G}}
.
If
{\widetilde{G}}
is simply connected,
then the map taking

({\tilde{g}}_{i})_{i}
to the product of the
\phi_{i}({\tilde{g}}_{i})
is a

\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

{\widehat{G}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
.

Proof.
These maps are
\Gamma
-equivariant by construction.
Since
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)
is constant, so that
the inclusion
{\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)}\longrightarrow{\pi_{0}(\Gamma)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
is
an isomorphism,
Remark
4.3.3

shows that
each of these maps induces
an isomorphism on root data, hence is
an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 4.3.7
.

Preserve the notation and hypothesis of Corollary
4.3.6
.
If
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint, then

{(\psi_{i})_{i}}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\widehat{G}}

restricts to an isomorphism of

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
onto
\widehat{G}^{\Gamma}
, whose
composition with the isomorphism

{\widehat{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\prod{\widetilde{G}}_{i}^{\Gamma_{i}}}

of Lemma
A.20
is an isomorphism of

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
onto
\prod_{i}{\widetilde{G}}_{i}^{\Gamma_{i}}
.

4.4.
Spherical buildings

Recall the notion of the spherical building
\mathscr{S}(G)
of a reductive
k
-group

G
from
[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§2.
If
S
is a split
k
-torus,

E/k
is a field extension,

T
is a split
E
-torus,
and
{S_{E}}\longrightarrow{T}
is an embedding, then
we obtain a corresponding embedding
{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{E})}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(T)}
,
which, when pre-composed with the natural isomorphism

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{E})}
, furnishes an embedding

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(T)}
, hence

{V(S)}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow{V(T)}
.
This induces an embedding

{\mathscr{S}(S)=(V(S)\smallsetminus\left\{0\right\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow{(V(T)\smallsetminus\left\{0\right\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}=\mathscr{S}(T)}

of spherical apartments

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§1.
If we take

S
to be a maximal torus in
G

and

T
to be a maximal torus in
C_{G}(S)_{E}
, then
we see that every apartment of
\mathscr{S}(G)
embeds
in an apartment of
\mathscr{S}(G_{E})
.
If
b
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(S)
, viewed as
an apartment
\mathscr{A}(S)
in
\mathscr{S}(G)
, and
we write

P_{G}(b)
for the corresponding parabolic subgroup of
G

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§1 and

b_{E}
for the corresponding element of
\mathscr{A}(T)
, then
the analogous parabolic subgroup
P_{G_{E}}(b_{E})
of
G_{E}
equals

P_{G}(b)_{E}

(as can be verified on the level of Lie algebras).
Thus, two apartments that are glued in
\mathscr{S}(G)
are
also glued in
\mathscr{S}(G_{E})
.
We thus obtain a canonical map
{\mathscr{S}(G)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(G_{E})}
.
We now make three observations that, together, show that

{\mathscr{S}(G)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(G_{E})}
is an embedding
(i.e., injection):

•

Any two elements of
\mathscr{S}(G)
lie in a common apartment

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*(2.3).

•

For any tori
S
and
T
as above,
the restriction to
\mathscr{S}(S)
of

{\mathscr{S}(G)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(G_{E})}
is
the embedding
{\mathscr{A}(S)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{A}(T)}
.

•

The map from an apartment into the full spherical building is
an embedding

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*Lemma 2.2(ii).

With this in mind, we use the map
{\mathscr{S}(G)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(G_{E})}
to regard

\mathscr{S}(G)
as a subset of
\mathscr{S}(G_{E})
.
It thus makes sense to ask if an element of
\mathscr{S}(G)
is fixed by
an automorphism of
G_{E}
(acting on
\mathscr{S}(G_{E})
), even if
that automorphism is not the base change to
E
of
an automorphism of
k
.

Recall that
\mathscr{S}
is a functor from
the category of reductive
k
-groups and embeddings
to
the category of sets and injections

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§4.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Let

E/k
be a finite, separable field extension.

(a)

Let
H_{1}
be a reductive
E
-group.
If

S_{1}
is a maximal (
E
-)split torus in
H_{1}
, and

S
is the maximal (
k
-)split torus in
\WRes_{E/k}S_{1}
, then

S
is a maximal split torus in
\WRes_{E/k}H_{1}
, and
the Weil adjunction

{\Hom_{k}(\GL_{1,k},\WRes_{E/k}S_{1})}\longrightarrow{\Hom_{E}(\GL_{1,E},S_{1})}

restricts to an isomorphism

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{1})}
.
The map
{S_{1}}\longmapsto{S}
is a bijection between
the maximal split tori in
H_{1}
and
\WRes_{E/k}H_{1}
.
The resulting maps
{\mathscr{S}(S)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(S_{1})}
fit together into
a (
\Gal(E/k)\ltimes H_{1}(E)
)-equivariant bijection

{\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{1})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(H_{1})}
.

(b)

Let
H
be a reductive
k
-group.
The inclusion
{\mathscr{S}(H)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(H_{E})}
and
the functorial map
{\mathscr{S}(H)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{E})}

are identified by the bijection in (
a
).

Proof.
We have by

[
borel-tits:reductive-groups
]
*§6.21(i) that
the (
E
-)rank of
H_{1}
is the (
k
-)rank of
\WRes_{E/k}H_{1}
,
so the construction in the statement produces
maximal tori in
\WRes_{E/k}H_{1}
.
Since the inclusion of

{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)
in

{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(\WRes_{E/k}S_{1})=\Hom_{k}(\GL_{1,k},\WRes_{E/k}S_{1})

is an equality, we have that

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{1})}
is a bijection.
Since the Weil adjunction is given by composition with the co-unit

{(\WRes_{E/k}S_{1})_{E}}\longrightarrow{S_{1}}
, we have that

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{1})}
respects addition, hence is
an isomorphism.

That the map
{S_{1}}\longmapsto{S}
is a bijection is

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.5.15(2).
We thus have a bijection between
apartments in
\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{1})
and
apartments in
\mathscr{S}(H_{1})
such that
there is a bijection between corresponding pairs of apartments.
This family of bijections is
(
\Gal(E/k)\ltimes H_{1}(E)
)-equivariant, in
the obvious sense.
To obtain the desired
H_{1}(E)
-equivariant bijection

{\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{1})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(H_{1})}
, we need only show that
the way that apartments are glued matches.

The discussion of

[
borel-tits:reductive-groups
]
*§6.20
furnishes an isomorphism

{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(S_{1})}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(S)}

(the one denoted there by
\beta
, not by
\alpha
)
dual to our map
{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{1})}
, and

[
borel-tits:reductive-groups
]
*§6.21(i)
shows that it identifies

\Phi(H_{1},S_{1})

with

\Phi(\WRes_{E/k}H_{1},S)
in such a way that
the Weil restriction of
the subgroup of
H_{1}
associated to a quasi-closed
set of roots in
\Phi(H_{1},S_{1})
is
the subgroup of
\WRes_{E/k}H_{1}
associated to the corresponding
set of roots in
\Phi(\WRes_{E/k}H_{1},S)
.
We do not go into the details of this latter point, only note that
it shows that, if
b
belongs to
V(S)
and

b_{1}
is the corresponding element of
V(S_{1})
, then

P_{\WRes_{E/k}H_{1}}(b)
is

\WRes_{E/k}P_{H_{1}}(b_{1})
.
In particular, the canonical identification of

(\WRes_{E/k}H_{1})(k)
with
H_{1}(E)
identifies

P_{\WRes_{E/k}H_{1}}(b)(k)
with

P_{H_{1}}(b_{1})(E)
.
The gluings of the apartments thus match, as desired.
This shows (
a
).

For (
b
), since
a spherical building is made by gluing together spherical apartments,
it suffices to check this for
H
a split torus
S
.
Write
T
for the maximal split torus in
\WRes_{E/k}S_{E}
.
We have the map

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)=\Hom_{k}(\GL_{1,k},S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{E})=\Hom_{E}(\GL_{1,E},S_{E})}

used to define
{\mathscr{S}(S)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(S_{E})}
, as well as the map

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{E})=\Hom_{E}(\GL_{1,E},S_{E})}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(\WRes_{E/k}S_{E})=\Hom_{k}(\GL_{1,k},\WRes_{E/k}S_{E})}

used to define
{\mathscr{S}(S_{E})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}S_{E})}
.
The composite of these maps on cocharacter lattices is
exactly the functorial map

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(\WRes_{E/k}S_{E})={\mathbf{X}}_{*}(T)}

used to define the functorial map

{\mathscr{S}(S)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}S_{E})}
.
This shows (
b
).
∎

For the remainder of §
4.4
,
we fix a reductive datum
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
over
k
.

Lemma
4.4.2
allows us
to state the conclusions in
Theorems

A
(
3
)
and

C
(
3
)
without passing to
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, as long as

\Gamma(k)
is Zariski dense in
\Gamma
.

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Suppose that

\Gamma(k)
is Zariski dense in
\Gamma
.
Then
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
equals

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma(k)}
.

Proof.
It is clear that
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma(k)}
contains

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.
Suppose conversely that

{\widetilde{b}}
is an element of
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma(k)}
.
Although we have agreed to regard the map

{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
as
an inclusion, in this proof we will write

{\widetilde{b}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
for emphasis when
we regard
{\widetilde{b}}
as an element of
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma(k)
, we have that

\gamma P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}})=P_{\widetilde{G}}(\gamma{\widetilde{b}})
equals

P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}})
.
Thus
P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}})
is preserved by
\Gamma(k)
, hence
by
\Gamma
.
In particular, the image of
\Gamma^{\circ}
in

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
under the map of Remark
2.2.8
(
a
) lies in

N_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}}))
, which, by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Propositions 2.2.9 and 3.5.7,
equals
P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}({\widetilde{b}})
.
In particular, for every

\gamma\in\Gamma^{\circ}({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, the action of
\gamma
on

{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, hence on
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, is by an element of

P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}({\widetilde{b}})({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
.
Such an element lifts to

P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}})({k^{\textup{{a}}}})=P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}({\widetilde{b}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, and so
fixes
{\widetilde{b}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
by the definition of the spherical building

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§2.

Since
\Gamma(k)
is Zariski dense in
\Gamma
, we have that
every connected component of
\Gamma
contains
an element of
\Gamma(k)
; so also
every connected component of
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
contains
an element of
\Gamma(k)
.
Thus, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, we can write

\gamma=\gamma_{0}\gamma_{1}
, with

\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma^{\circ}({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
and

\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma(k)
.
We have shown that both
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
fix

{\widetilde{b}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, so
\gamma
does as well.
That is,
{\widetilde{b}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
,
so
{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.
∎

Lemma
4.4.3

is related to complete reducibility,
in the sense of
[
serre:cr
]
*Definition 2.2.1.

Lemma 4.4.3
.

Suppose that
{\widetilde{H}}
is a reductive subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
containing

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma(k)})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Each of a pair of points in
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma(k)}
that are
opposite in
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
, in the sense of

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§3,
belongs to
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{H}})
.

Proof.
Let
{\widetilde{b}}_{\pm}
be opposite points in

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma(k)}
.
Thus
P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}}_{\pm})
are
opposite parabolic subgroups of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Write
{\widetilde{M}}
for their intersection, which is
a Levi component of both.
If
{\widetilde{S}}
is a maximal split torus in
{\widetilde{M}}
, then,

{\widetilde{b}}_{\pm}
belong to
\mathscr{A}({\widetilde{S}})
, and,
by the definition of ‘opposite’,
they satisfy

{\widetilde{b}}_{-}=-{\widetilde{b}}_{+}
there.
Since the intersection of
{\widetilde{M}}
with
the (solvable) radical of
P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}}_{\pm})
is
the radical of
{\widetilde{M}}
, i.e., its center,
we have that the maximal torus
{\widetilde{A}}
in
the intersection of
{\widetilde{S}}
with the radical of
P_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{b}}_{\pm})
is
central in
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Conversely, it is clear that
the maximal split, central torus in
{\widetilde{M}}
is
contained in
{\widetilde{S}}
, hence in
{\widetilde{A}}
, so
we have equality.
In particular,
{\widetilde{A}}
is preserved by
\Gamma(k)
.
We have by
[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*Lemma 1.2(ii) that

{\widetilde{b}}_{\pm}
belong to
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{A}})
, hence to

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{A}})^{\Gamma(k)}
.
Thus there is a homomorphism from

\Gamma(k)
to
the multiplicative group
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
that measures
the (common, because they are opposite)
scaling factor by which
\gamma
acts on the rays

{\widetilde{b}}_{\pm}\in(V({\widetilde{A}})\smallsetminus\left\{0\right\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
By rigidity of tori
[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 12.37,
the action of
\Gamma(k)
factors through
the finite group
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
.
Since
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
has no nontrivial, finite subgroup,
the homomorphism

{\Gamma(k)}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
is trivial.
That is,
\gamma
acts trivially on the rays
{\widetilde{b}}_{\pm}
,
which are therefore contained in

({\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{A}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R})^{\Gamma(k)}={\mathbf{X}}_{*}(({\widetilde{A}}^{\Gamma(k)})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
.
That is,
{\widetilde{b}}_{\pm}
belong to

\mathscr{S}(({\widetilde{A}}^{\Gamma(k)})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
, and so to

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{H}})
.
∎

Lemma 4.4.4
.

Let
H
be a reductive
k
-group.
Then
\mathscr{S}(H)
equals
\mathscr{S}(H_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gal(k)}
.

Proof.
We have that

\mathscr{S}(H_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
equals
\bigcup\mathscr{S}(H_{E})
,
the union over all finite, Galois field extensions
E/k
, so
it suffices to prove that

\mathscr{S}(H)
equals
\mathscr{S}(H_{E})^{\Gal(E/k)}
.
By Lemma
4.4.1
(
b
),
it suffices to show, for every such extension
E/k
, that
the functorial map
{\mathscr{S}(H)}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{E})}
is
a bijection onto
\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{E})^{\Gal(E/k)}
.

Functoriality implies that the image of
\mathscr{S}(H)

lies in
\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{E})^{\Gal(E/k)}
.
If
b_{+}
belongs to

\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{E})^{\Gal(E/k)}
, then
the corresponding parabolic subgroup

P_{\WRes_{E/k}H_{E}}(b_{+})
,
which by
[
borel-tits:reductive-groups
]
*Corollaire 6.19
is of the form
\WRes_{E/k}P_{1}^{+}
for some
parabolic subgroup
P_{1}^{+}
of
H_{E}
,
is preserved by the
algebraic
action of
\Gal(E/k)
,
so that
P_{1}^{+}
is preserved by the
field
action of
\Gal(E/k)

and hence is of the form
P^{+}_{E}
for some
parabolic subgroup
P^{+}
of
H
.
If
P^{-}
is an opposite parabolic subgroup of
H
, then

\WRes_{E/k}P^{-}
is a parabolic subgroup of
\WRes_{E/k}H_{E}
that is
opposite to
P_{\WRes_{E/k}H_{E}}(b_{+})
and
preserved by
\Gal(E/k)
.
By
[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§3, there is
a unique point
b_{-}\in\mathscr{S}(\WRes_{E/k}H_{E})
that is
opposite to
b_{+}
and
satisfies
P_{\WRes_{E/k}H_{E}}(b_{-})=\WRes_{E/k}P^{-}
.
By uniqueness,
b_{-}
is also fixed by
\Gal(E/k)
, so
Lemma
4.4.3
gives that

b_{+}
belongs to the spherical building of

(\WRes_{E/k}H_{E})^{\Gal(E/k)}
.
This latter group is precisely the image of
H
in

\WRes_{E/k}H_{E}
, so

b_{+}
belongs to the image of
\mathscr{S}(H)
.
∎

5.
Quasisemisimple actions on root systems

Let
{\widetilde{\Psi}}
be a (possibly non-reduced) root datum.
In §
5
, unlike in most of the rest of the paper,
we let
\Gamma
be an abstract group, and

{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\Aut({\widetilde{\Psi}})}
a quasisemisimple action that
factors through a finite quotient.
(Note that
\Gamma
is not a
k
-group;
indeed, there is no longer a field
k
in sight.)

We are only interested in the root system of
{\widetilde{\Psi}}
, so,
whenever convenient, we may
replace the root datum

({\widetilde{X}},{\widetilde{\Phi}},{\widetilde{X}}^{\vee},{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee})

by the corresponding ‘adjoint’ datum
with character lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}
and
root system
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
In particular, we may, and do, assume that

\Aut({\widetilde{\Psi}})
is finite.
Then,
since all of our constructions depend only on the action of
\Gamma
,
we may always replace
\Gamma
by its image in

\Aut({\widetilde{\Psi}})
and so work with a finite acting group; but
occasionally it is handy not to have to do so.

Below, we present a collection of results concerning
the action of
\Gamma
on a root system.
In applications, we will often have an action of a
smooth
k
-group
\Gamma

on a connected, reductive group
{\widetilde{G}}
,
and we will apply these results to the natural action of

\Gal(k)\ltimes\pi_{0}(\Gamma)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})

on the (absolute) root datum of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
See Notation
6.16
.

The pair
({\widetilde{\Psi}},\Gamma)
has an associated “quotient root datum”

\Psi=(X,\Phi,X^{\vee},\Phi^{\vee})
,
constructed in
[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*Theorem 7.
(In
[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
,
they write
\Psi
for what we call
{\widetilde{\Psi}}
,
and
\bar{\Psi}
for what we call
\Psi
.)
It is characterized by the facts that

X
is the maximal torsion-free quotient of the module of co-invariants

{\widetilde{X}}_{\Gamma}
,
and
\Phi
is the image in
X
of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
In particular,
X^{\vee}
is the module of invariants

({\widetilde{X}}^{\vee})^{\Gamma}
.
We write
i_{\Gamma}^{*}
for the quotient morphism

{{\widetilde{X}}}\longrightarrow{X}
, so that
i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{\Phi}})
equals
\Phi
.
(The behavior of the transpose map
{X^{\vee}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{X}}^{\vee}}

on
\Phi^{\vee}
is somewhat more complicated;
see
Proposition
5.2
(
4
)
and
Lemma
5.5
(
a
).)
For
{\widetilde{a}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}
,
we refer to
a=i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{a}})

as the
restriction
of
{\widetilde{a}}
,
and to
{\widetilde{a}}
as an
extension
of
a
.

Remark 5.1
.

Let
\Phi^{\prime}
be an integrally closed subsystem of
\Phi
, and
choose a system
\Phi^{\prime\,{+}}
of
positive roots for
\Phi^{\prime}
.
If we write
{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\prime}

(respectively,
{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\prime\,{+}}
) for
the set of extensions of elements of
\Phi^{\prime}

(respectively,
\Phi^{\prime\,{+}}
), then

{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\prime}
is
an integrally closed subsystem of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, and

{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\prime\,{+}}
is
a system of positive roots for
{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\prime}
, so that
the action of
\Gamma
on

({\widetilde{X}},{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\prime},{\widetilde{X}}^{\vee},{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\prime\,\vee})
is
quasisemisimple.
The “quotient root datum” is

(X,\Phi^{\prime},X^{\vee},\Phi^{\prime\,\vee})
.

Proposition
5.2
is essentially some of
[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*§2, rephrased in our language.

Proposition 5.2
.

(a)

\Phi
is a (possibly non-reduced) root system.

(b)

{{\widetilde{\Phi}}/\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\Phi}
is a bijection.

(c)

{{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}}\longmapsto{i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+})}

is a bijection from

\Gamma
-stable systems of positive roots in
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

to systems of positive roots in
\Phi
, with
inverse bijection

{\Phi^{+}}\longmapsto{(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}(\Phi^{+})}
.

Fix
a\in\Phi
, and write

{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
for the set of elements of

{\widetilde{\Phi}}
whose restriction is
an integer multiple of
a
.
This is an integrally closed subsystem of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.

(4)

If
a
is not multipliable in
\Phi
, then
each irreducible component of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
is
of type
\mathsf{A}_{1}
, and
contains exactly one extension of
a
, which
spans it.

a^{\vee}
equals

\sum\limits_{i^{*}_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{a}})=a}{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}
.

(5)

If
a
is multipliable in
\Phi
,
then one of the following holds.

(i)

There is some positive integer
n
such that
every irreducible component of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
intersecting
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
is
of type
\mathsf{BC}_{n}
, and
intersects
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
in
an irreducible component of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
of type
\mathsf{BC}_{1}
.
Each such component contains exactly one extension of
a
, which
spans it.

(ii)

There is some positive integer
n
such that
every irreducible component of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
intersecting
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
is
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
, and
intersects
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
in
an irreducible component of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2}
.
Each such component contains exactly two extensions of
a
, which
form a system of simple roots for it.

Proof.
Parts
(
a
),
(
b
),
and
(
c
)
are
[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*Theorem 7, Lemma 6, and Lemma 14,
respectively.
Part
(
4
) comes from combining

[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*Notation 4 with the definition
before
[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*Theorem 7.
Part
(
5
) is

[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*Remark 12.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

A system
{\widetilde{\Delta}}
of simple roots for
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

that is preserved by
\Gamma

is
a fortiori
a basis of
{\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, so that

{\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is induced as a
\Gamma
-module.
Since
({\widetilde{\Psi}}^{\vee},\Gamma)
is also quasisemisimple,
we also have that
{\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee}
is induced.

Definition 5.4
.

In the situation of Proposition
5.2
(
5
),
an
exceptional (unordered) pair
for
({\widetilde{\Psi}},\Gamma)

is the multiset of order 2 whose underlying set
is the intersection of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{a}
with
the set of positive roots in an irreducible component of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
We say that the exceptional pair
extends
a
.

Thus,
in the situation of
Proposition
5.2
(
5
)(
5ii
)
(respectively,
Proposition
5.2
(
5
)(
5i
)),
an exceptional pair consists of 2 distinct elements
(respectively, a single element of multiplicity 2).
In either case, if
\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}
is
an exceptional pair, then

{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose that
a\in\Phi
is multipliable.

(a)

a^{\vee}
equals
2\sum({\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime})^{\vee}
,
the sum taken over all exceptional pairs

\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}

for
({\widetilde{\Psi}},\Gamma)
extending
a
.

(b)

The set of exceptional pairs extending
a
is nonempty, and
permuted transitively by
\Gamma
.

(c)

Either
all exceptional pairs extending
a
have 2 distinct elements,
or
all exceptional pairs extending
a
have 1 element with
multiplicity 2.

Proof.
Claim
(
a
) comes from combining

[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*Notation 4 with the definition
before
[
adler-lansky:data-actions
]
*Theorem 7.
Claim
(
b
)
follows immediately from
Proposition
5.2
(
b
,
5
).
Claim (
c
)
follows immediately from
(
b
).
∎

Definition 5.6
.

Suppose that
a\in\Phi
is multipliable.
Say that
a
is
split
(respectively,
inert
)
for
({\widetilde{\Psi}},\Gamma)

if some
(hence, by
Lemma
5.5
(
b
),
every)
exceptional pair extending
a
consists of 2 distinct elements
(respectively, consists of a single element of multiplicity 2).
When
({\widetilde{\Psi}},\Gamma)
is understood, we may just say that

a
is split or inert, without further qualification.

Remark 5.7
.

If
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is reduced, then
every multipliable element of
\Phi
is split.
On the other hand,
if the action of
\Gamma
is trivial, then
every multipliable element of
\Phi={\widetilde{\Phi}}
is inert.

Lemma 5.8
.

Suppose that
a\in\Phi
is multipliable.

(a)

Every extension of
a
belongs to
exactly one exceptional pair
extending
a
.

(b)

The map

{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}\longmapsto{{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}}

is a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection from
the set of exceptional pairs extending
a
onto
the set of extensions of
2a
.

(c)

If
\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}
is an
exceptional pair, then
the stabilizers in
\Gamma
of

\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}
and of

{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}
are equal.

Proof.
The claims
(
a
,
b
)
follow immediately from
Proposition
5.2
(
b
,
5
).
In particular, if we write

\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
and

\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}}
for
the appropriate stabilizers, then
it is clear that

\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}

is contained in

\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}}
.
We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left\{\text{exceptional pairs extending $a$}\right\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}{\hbox{\kern-138.14728pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\line@@}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces==\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces==\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\right\}.}\hbox{\kern 0.0625pt\raise-7.51782pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\left\{\text{extensions of ${\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}$}\right\}.}$}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\text{extensions of ${\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}$}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces

Proposition
5.2
(
b
)
and
Lemma
5.5
(
b
)
show that the vertical arrows are bijections,
and
(
b
) shows that
the bottom arrow is a bijection,
so the top must also be a bijection.
That gives (
c
).
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

For every
a\in\Phi
, we have that

a^{\vee}
equals

\sum_{i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{a}})=a}{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}

if
a
is non-multipliable or inert, and

a^{\vee}
equals

2\sum_{i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{a}})=a}{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}

if
a
is split.

Proof.
If
a
is non-multipliable, then this is
Proposition
5.2
(
4
).
If
a
is inert, then, for every exceptional pair

\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}
extending
a
, we have that

{\widetilde{a}}
equals
{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}
, so that

2({\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime})^{\vee}

equals
{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}
.
If
a
is split, then, for every exceptional pair

\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}
extending
a
, we have by
Proposition
5.2
(
5
)(
5ii
)
that
2({\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime})^{\vee}
equals

2({\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime\,\vee})
.
In either case,
Lemma
5.5
(
a
)
gives the result.
∎

Corollary 5.10

(Corollary to
Proposition
5.2
and
Lemma
5.5
)
.

The sublattices

{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}
and

({\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee})^{\Gamma}

of
{\widetilde{X}}^{\vee}

are equal.

Proof.
Proposition
5.2
(
4
) and
Lemma
5.5
(
a
)
show that

\Phi^{\vee}
, hence
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}
, is contained in

{\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee}\cap X^{\vee}={\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee}\cap({\widetilde{X}}^{\vee})^{\Gamma}\subseteq({\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee})^{\Gamma}
.

Conversely, we have by Remark
5.3
that
the sums of
\Gamma
-orbits of elements of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee}

span
({\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{\vee})^{\Gamma}
,
so it suffices to show that such sums lie in
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}
.

Fix
{\widetilde{a}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, and let

a=i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{a}})
.
If
a
is non-multipliable in
\Phi
, then
Proposition
5.2
(
b
,
4
) gives that
the sum of the
\Gamma
-orbit of
{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}
equals

a^{\vee}
, and so
belongs to
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}
.
If
{\widetilde{a}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is multipliable in
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, then

2{\widetilde{a}}
is not, so we have just shown that
the sum of the
\Gamma
-orbit of

\tfrac{1}{2}{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}=(2{\widetilde{a}})^{\vee}
, and hence of

{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}
, belongs to

{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}
.

Now suppose that
{\widetilde{a}}
is
non-multipliable in
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, but

a
is
multipliable in
\Phi
.
Then
a
is split, so
Corollary
5.9
gives that

\frac{1}{2}a^{\vee}
is
the sum of the coroots corresponding to the extensions of
a
in
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, which, by
Proposition
5.2
(
b
),
is the sum of the
\Gamma
-orbit of
{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}
; but

\frac{1}{2}a^{\vee}
equals
(2a)^{\vee}
, and so
belongs to
{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

(a)

If
a
and
b
are elements of
\Phi
such that

a+b
belongs to
\Phi
, then,
for every
extension
{\widetilde{a}}
of
a
, there is an
extension
{\widetilde{b}}
of
b
such that

{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.

(b)

{{\widetilde{\Delta}}}\longmapsto{i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{\Delta}})}

is a bijection from

\Gamma
-stable systems of simple roots in
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

to systems of simple roots in
\Phi
, with
inverse bijection

{\Delta}\longmapsto{(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}(\Delta)}
.

Proof.
We begin with (
a
).
If
a
equals
b
and

\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}
is
an exceptional pair containing
{\widetilde{a}}

(which exists, by
Lemma
5.8
(
a
)), then

{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
Thus we may, and do, suppose that
a
and
b
are distinct.

Suppose first that

a
and
b
are not orthogonal
(in addition to being distinct).
Then
\left\langle{\widetilde{a}},b^{\vee}\right\rangle=\left\langle a,b^{\vee}\right\rangle
is negative

[
bourbaki:lie-gp+lie-alg_4-6
]
*
Ch. VI, no. 1.3, p. 149, Corollaire to Théorème 1.
Corollary
5.9
gives that
there is some extension
{\widetilde{b}}
of
b
such that

\left\langle{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{b}}^{\vee}\right\rangle
is negative; and another
application of

loc. cit.

gives that
{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.

Finally, suppose that
a
and
b
are orthogonal.
Then
\left\langle a+b,a^{\vee}\right\rangle
equals
2
, so

-a
and
a+b
are
not
strongly orthogonal; and,
in fact,
-a+(a+b)=b
belongs to
\Phi
.
Thus, we have just shown that there is an extension

{\widetilde{c}}
of
a+b
such that

-{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{c}}

belongs to
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
Put
{\widetilde{b}}=-{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{c}}
.

For (
b
), suppose first that

{\widetilde{\Delta}}
is a
\Gamma
-stable
system of simple roots for
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

with corresponding system of positive roots

{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}
.
Put
\Delta=i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{\Delta}})
.
We have by
Proposition
5.2
(
c
)
that
\Phi^{+}\mathrel{:=}i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+})
is
a system of positive roots for
\Phi
, and that

{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}
equals

(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}({\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+})
.
If
\Delta
is not simple, then there exist
a,b\in\Phi^{+}
such that

a+b
belongs to
\Delta
.
By (
a
), we have that there are
extensions
{\widetilde{a}}
and
{\widetilde{b}}
of
a
and
b
,
necessarily in
{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}
, such that

{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to

(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}(a+b)\in(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}(\Delta)
,
we have by
Proposition
5.2
(
b
)
that

{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Delta}}
.
This contradicts the simplicity of
{\widetilde{\Delta}}
, so
\Delta
must be simple.

Now suppose conversely that
\Delta
is
a system of simple roots for
\Phi
with
corresponding system of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
.
Put
{\widetilde{\Delta}}=(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}(\Delta)
, which is
clearly preserved by
\Gamma
.
Again by
Proposition
5.2
(
c
),
we have that

{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}\mathrel{:=}(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}(\Phi^{+})
is
a
\Gamma
-stable system of positive roots for
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
It is clear that there do not exist

{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{b}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}
such that

{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Delta}}
.
Now fix
{\widetilde{a}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}\smallsetminus{\widetilde{\Delta}}
, and put

a=i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{a}})
, which belongs to

\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Delta
.
By simplicity, there is some
b\in\Delta
such that

a-b
belongs to
\Phi^{+}
.
By (
a
), there is some extension

{\widetilde{b}}
of
b
, necessarily in
{\widetilde{\Delta}}
, such that

{\widetilde{a}}-{\widetilde{b}}
belongs to
{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{+}
.
This shows (
b
).
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

The nodes of the Dynkin diagram of
\Phi
are
in bijection with the
\Gamma
-orbits of
nodes of the Dynkin diagram of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
, and
two nodes of the Dynkin diagram of
\Phi
are adjacent
if and only if
they are restrictions of adjacent nodes of
the Dynkin diagram of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.

Proof.
Fix a system
{\widetilde{\Delta}}
of simple roots for
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
.
Lemma
5.11
(
b

gives that
\Delta\mathrel{:=}i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{\Delta}})

is a system of simple roots for
\Phi
, and
Proposition
5.2
(
b
) shows that
we may identify
\Delta
with the set of
orbits of
\Gamma
on
{\widetilde{\Delta}}
.

Now suppose that
{\widetilde{a}}
and
{\widetilde{b}}
belong to

{\widetilde{\Delta}}
, and
write
a
and
b
for their respective restrictions.
Proposition
5.2
(
b
) again,
and Corollary
5.9
, give that

\left\langle b,a^{\vee}\right\rangle
is a positive multiple of

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma/{\stab_{\Gamma}{\widetilde{a}}}}\langle{\widetilde{b}},\gamma{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}\rangle
.
In particular, the sum is
0
, so that

a
and
b
are not adjacent, unless
some
\Gamma
-conjugate of
{\widetilde{a}}

is adjacent to
{\widetilde{b}}
.

To complete the proof,
we may, and do, assume, upon replacing
{\widetilde{a}}
by
a
\Gamma
-conjugate if necessary, that

{\widetilde{a}}
and
{\widetilde{b}}
are adjacent.
An examination of the irreducible root systems
(and their diagram automorphisms), say in

[
bourbaki:lie-gp+lie-alg_4-6
]
*Chapter VI, Plates I–IX,
shows that, if there is
a diagram automorphism of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
that
moves
{\widetilde{a}}
to a node of
the Dynkin diagram of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
that
is adjacent to
{\widetilde{b}}
, then
every diagram automorphism of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

preserving the irreducible component to which
{\widetilde{b}}
belongs
fixes
{\widetilde{b}}
.
Thus

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma/{\stab_{\Gamma}{\widetilde{a}}}}\langle{\widetilde{b}},\gamma{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}\rangle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma/{\stab_{\Gamma}{\widetilde{a}}}}\langle\gamma{\widetilde{b}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}\rangle
is
a positive multiple of
\langle{\widetilde{b}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\vee}\rangle
,
hence so is
\left\langle b,a^{\vee}\right\rangle
.
In particular,
a
and
b
are adjacent.
∎

Corollary 5.13
.

The map
{{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}}\longmapsto{i_{\Gamma}^{*}({\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1})}

is a surjection from
the irreducible components of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
onto
the irreducible components of
\Phi
.
It induces a bijection between
the
\Gamma
-orbits of irreducible components of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
and
the irreducible components of
\Phi
, with inverse map sending
an irreducible component
\Phi_{1}
of
\Phi
to
the
\Gamma
-orbit of any irreducible component of

(i_{\Gamma}^{*})^{-1}(\Phi_{1})
.

Remark 5.14
.

Suppose that
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}
is a reduced irreducible component of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}

and that the corresponding irreducible component

\Phi_{1}
of
\Phi
is non-reduced.
Write

\Gamma_{1}
for the subgroup of
\Gamma
that
preserves
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}
, and

\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
for the subgroup of
\Gamma_{1}
that
acts trivially on it.
Proposition
5.2
(
b
,
5
)
gives that

{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}
is of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
for some
n
,
and
\Gamma_{1}/\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
has order
2
.
Let
\gamma_{0}
be an element of
\Gamma_{1}\smallsetminus\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
.

We shall use the terminology (motivated by (
a
) below) that
an element
{\widetilde{a}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}
is

pre-multipliable
if

{\widetilde{a}}
and
\gamma_{0}{\widetilde{a}}
are
neither equal nor orthogonal, and

pre-divisible
if

{\widetilde{a}}
is fixed by
\gamma_{0}
.
This condition is independent of the choice of
\gamma_{0}
.

(a)

An element of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}
is pre-divisible
(respectively, pre-multipliable)
if and only if
its restriction is divisible (respectively, multipliable)
in
\Phi_{1}
.
If
{\widetilde{a}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}
is pre-multipliable, then

\left\{{\widetilde{a}},\gamma_{0}{\widetilde{a}}\right\}
is an exceptional pair.

(b)

For every
{\widetilde{a}}\in{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{1}
, we have that

\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{a}})
equals

\Gamma_{1}
or
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
, according as

{\widetilde{a}}
is or is not pre-divisible.

6.
Quasisemisimplicity and smoothability

Proposition
5.2
is
phrased entirely in the abstract language of actions on root data,
but, in this paper, we are most interested in actions on groups.
Recall the field
k
with characteristic exponent
p
from
§
2
.
Throughout the rest of the paper
(not just §
6
),

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is a reductive datum over
k
,
in the sense of
Definition
2.2.6
.
Put
G=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

Recall the definition of quasisemisimplicity from
Definition
2.2.7
(
b
)
(although we do not assume that
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is
quasisemisimple until Proposition
6.5
).
Lemma
6.1
shows that
quasisemisimplicity can be checked on the level of
almost-simple components, at least
after passing to a sufficiently large
separable extension of
k
.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple, then

({\widetilde{G}}_{1},\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1}))
is quasisemisimple for every
almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
If
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)
is constant, then the converse holds.

Proof.
If
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma
and

{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
is a smooth, connected, normal subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
, then

({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}}\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{1})
is
a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
that is
preserved by
\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})
.
In particular,
({\widetilde{G}}_{1},\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1}))
is quasisemisimple.

Now suppose that

({\widetilde{G}}_{1},\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1}))
is quasisemisimple for
every almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Consider the set of triples

({\widetilde{G}}_{1},{\widetilde{B}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}}_{1})
, where

{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
is an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}
and

({\widetilde{B}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}}_{1})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
that
is preserved by
\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})
.
Remark
2.2.8
(
a
) and
Remark
2.2.8
(
b
)
give that

\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
acts on the set of such triples.
By assumption, the natural map from such triples to
almost-simple components of
{\widetilde{G}}
is surjective, and
obviously it is
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
-equivariant.
Arbitrarily
choose a
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
-equivariant section.
Then the subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
generated by

Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
and the various
{\widetilde{B}}_{1}

(respectively
{\widetilde{T}}_{1}
) arising as
a component of a triple in the image of the section is
a Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}

(respectively a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{B}}
) that is
preserved by
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
, hence by
\Gamma
,
since
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)
is constant.
∎

Lemma
6.2
is vacuous unless

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple, but
we still find it convenient
(for Lemma
6.3
)
to state the lemma before
making that assumption.

Lemma 6.2
.

If
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is preserved by
\Gamma
, then
there is a cocharacter
\delta
of the
maximal split torus in
({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}

such that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)
equals
{\widetilde{T}}
and
the parabolic subgroup

P_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)
of
{\widetilde{G}}
associated to
\delta

[
springer:lag
]
*Proposition 8.4.5
is
{\widetilde{B}}
.

Proof.
Consider the cocharacter

\sum_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})}{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\vee}

of
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
We abuse notation by denoting this cocharacter by

\delta_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, even though
we have not yet defined a cocharacter
\delta
of which
it is the base change.
Since
\delta_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is fixed by
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
and since
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is Zariski dense in
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,
we may regard it as a
\Gamma
-fixed cocharacter
\delta

of
{\widetilde{T}}
.
It is therefore a cocharacter of
{\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma}
, hence of

({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, hence of
its maximal split torus.

Since

\left\langle{\widetilde{\alpha}},\delta_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\right\rangle
equals
2
for
all
{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Delta({\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
we have that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}=C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}(\delta_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
equals
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
and

P_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}=P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}(\delta_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
equals
{\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}

[
springer:lag
]
*Proposition 8.4.5,
hence that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)
equals
{\widetilde{T}}
and

P_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)
equals
{\widetilde{B}}
.
∎

Lemma
6.3
shows one convenient way to recognize
quasisemisimple actions.
Proposition
6.5
(
a
) and
Lemma
6.6

provide converses to parts of Lemma
6.3
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that

S
is a split torus in
G
and that

({\widetilde{B}}^{\prime},{\widetilde{T}})
is a Borel–torus pair in

C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
that is preserved by
\Gamma
.
Then there is a Borel–torus pair

({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is preserved by
\Gamma
.
In particular, if there is a split torus
S
in

G
such that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
is a torus, then

C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
is contained in
a
\Gamma
-stable Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
, and

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemismple.

Proof.
By Lemma
6.2
,
there is a cocharacter
\lambda^{\prime}
of

(C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=C_{G}(S)
such that

{\widetilde{B}}^{\prime}
is the associated parabolic subgroup

P_{C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)}(\lambda^{\prime})
of
C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
, and

C_{C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)}(\lambda^{\prime})
is
{\widetilde{T}}
.
For every

{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})\smallsetminus\Phi(C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
the affine subspace
V_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
of
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
on which

{\widetilde{\alpha}}
equals
\left\langle{\widetilde{\alpha}},\lambda^{\prime}\right\rangle
is proper;
so the complement

({\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})\smallsetminus\bigcup_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})\smallsetminus\Phi(C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})}V_{\widetilde{\alpha}}

is nonempty. Let
\lambda^{\perp}
be an element of the complement.
After multipling by a positive integer, we may, and do, assume that

\lambda^{\perp}
belongs to
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Since
S
is split, so that the natural map

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
is an isomorphism,
we may, and do, regard

\lambda^{\perp}
as an element of
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)
.
Put
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda^{\perp}
.
Then
\left\langle{\widetilde{\alpha}},\lambda_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\right\rangle
is nonzero for all

{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, so

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}(\lambda_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
equals
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and

P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}(\lambda_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
The analogous facts without base change to
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
follow, so

(P_{\widetilde{G}}(\lambda),{\widetilde{T}})
is
a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is preserved by
\Gamma
.
∎

Lemma
6.4
shows that,
when checking the quasisemisimplicity of
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
,
we may always replace
{\widetilde{G}}
by a simply connected or adjoint group.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
{\widetilde{N}}
be a normal subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is preserved by
\Gamma
.
If
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple, then
so is
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}},\Gamma)
.
The converse holds if

{\widetilde{N}}
is central in
{\widetilde{G}}
.

Proof.
The first statement follows from

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 11.14(1).
This also shows that, if

({\widetilde{B}}^{\prime},{\widetilde{T}}^{\prime})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}}
that
is preserved by
\Gamma
, then
there is some Borel–torus pair
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
in
{\widetilde{G}}
that
maps onto
({\widetilde{B}}^{\prime},{\widetilde{T}}^{\prime})
, so that

{\widetilde{B}}\cdot{\widetilde{N}}
and
{\widetilde{T}}\cdot{\widetilde{N}}
are
preserved by
\Gamma
.
If
{\widetilde{N}}
is central in
{\widetilde{G}}
, then it is contained in

{\widetilde{B}}
and
{\widetilde{T}}
, so
the second statement follows.
∎

Throughout
the rest of
§
6

and
§
7
,
we assume that

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple; so, in particular,

{\widetilde{G}}
is quasisplit.
In
§§
8
,
9
,
we do not impose this assumption directly, although our goal is
Theorem
B
(
2
) that concludes
quasisemisimplicity.

Proposition 6.5
.

(a)

Let
{\widetilde{T}}
be a
\Gamma
-stable maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}

that is contained in a
\Gamma
-stable Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Put
T=({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, and
let
S
be the maximal split torus in
T
.
Then

T
equals
{\widetilde{T}}\cap G
and is a maximal torus in
G
,

S
is a maximal split torus in
G
, and

{\widetilde{T}}
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
.

(b)

Let
S
be a maximal split torus in
G
.
Then
C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
is the unique
maximal torus
{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}
containing
S
, and

{\widetilde{T}}\cap G
is the unique maximal torus in
G
containing
S
.
We have that
{\widetilde{T}}
is
\Gamma
-stable and
contained in a
\Gamma
-stable Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.

(c)

The set of
\Gamma
-stable maximal tori in
{\widetilde{G}}
that are
contained in a
\Gamma
-stable Borel subgroup is
permuted transitively by
G(k)
.

Proof.
Since all groups of multiplicative type are smoothable, we have
by Remark
2.2.2
that

T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, which is
a maximal torus in
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}

by
[
adler-lansky:lifting-1
]
*Proposition 3.5(ii).
Since

G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is
contained in
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, we have that

T
is a maximal torus in
G
.

Let
\delta
be the cocharacter of
S
constructed in
Lemma
6.2
, so that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)
equals
{\widetilde{T}}
.
Since the first and last terms in the
obvious sequence of containments

{\widetilde{T}}\subseteq C_{\widetilde{G}}(T)\subseteq C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)\subseteq C_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)

are equal, all the containments are equalities.
Thus
{\widetilde{T}}\cap G
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)\cap G=C_{G}(S)
, which is
smooth by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2),
connected by

[
borel:linear
]
*Corollary 11.12, and
contained in
{\widetilde{T}}
.
Therefore
{\widetilde{T}}\cap G
is a torus in
G
, hence
contained in the maximal torus
T
in
G
.
The reverse containment being obvious, we have the equality

{\widetilde{T}}\cap G=C_{G}(S)=T
.
In particular, since
S
is the maximal split torus in
T
,
in fact
S
is maximal split in
G
.
This shows (
a
), and
(
b
) for one choice of
maximal split torus
S
in
G
.
Since the maximal split tori in
G
are
G(k)
-conjugate
by
[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Theorem C.2.3,
we have shown
(
b
) in general, and
(
c
).
∎

For the remainder of §
6
, fix
a maximal split torus
S
in
G
.
By Proposition
6.5
(
b
),
there are unique maximal tori

T
in
G
and

{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}

containing
S
, as well as a
\Gamma
-stable Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
containing
{\widetilde{T}}
.
Let
{\widetilde{S}}
be the maximal split torus in
{\widetilde{T}}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

If
D
is a subgroup of
S
, then

(C_{\widetilde{G}}(D)^{\circ},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.
If
\mathfrak{d}
is a subspace of
\Lie(S)
, then

(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{d})^{\circ},\Gamma)
is
quasisemisimple.

Note
.

We do not assume that
D
is a torus, or even smooth.
We have that
C_{\widetilde{G}}(D)^{\circ}
is reductive by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.12, and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{d})^{\circ}
is reductive by
Corollary
4.2.2
.

Proof.
We have that
S
is a split torus in

(C_{\widetilde{G}}(D)^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
(respectively,

(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{d})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
), and

C_{C_{\widetilde{G}}(D)^{\circ}}(S)

(respectively,

C_{C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{d})^{\circ}}(S)
)
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
, which is a torus by
Proposition
6.5
(
b
).
Then Lemma
6.3
gives the result.
∎

Remark
6.7

allows us to apply the results of
Proposition
5.2
and
Lemma
5.5
in
the setting of connected, reductive groups.

Remark 6.7
.

We can ‘restrict’ an element of

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
for example, an element of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, to

{\widetilde{S}}
by restricting from
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
to
{\widetilde{S}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and then
using the fact that

{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{S}})}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{S}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})}

is an isomorphism.
Similarly, we can ‘restrict’ from
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
or
T
to
S
.

Proposition
6.5
(
b
)
gives that
{\widetilde{T}}

is
preserved by
\Gamma
.
By rigidity of tori

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 12.37,

\Gamma^{\circ}
fixes
{\widetilde{T}}
pointwise,
so
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)
acts on
{\widetilde{T}}
; and
the action of
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
on
the absolute root datum
\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

factors through
the finite quotient
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.

We have that
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
and
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)

are the sets of restrictions to
T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and to
S

of elements of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
and
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})
.
This is just the definition, together with the fact that,
by Proposition
6.5
(
b
)
(or Proposition
5.2
(
a
)),
no element of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
has
trivial ‘restriction’ to
S

(so that also no element has trivial restriction to
T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
).

Write

\Psi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})
,

\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and

\Psi({\widetilde{G}},S)

for the “quotient root data” of

\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
by

\Gal(k)
,

\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and

\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, respectively.
The maps

•

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{S}})}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gal(k)}}
,

•

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}}
,
and

•

{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}}

are all isomorphisms, which we may use to identify
the character lattices of the root data with

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{S}})
,

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(S)
, respectively,
in which case their root systems are identified with

\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})
,

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and

\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
,
respectively.
We denote the corresponding duality map

{\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)}
by

{a}\longmapsto{a^{\vee}}
,
and denote its image by
\Phi^{\vee}({\widetilde{G}},S)
;
and similarly for
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

(and for
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})
, though that is just
the classical construction of
relative root systems).

Using the above identifications of root systems,
we may also refer to exceptional pairs in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

that ‘extend’ roots in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
, as in Definition
5.4
.

Note that restriction from
{\widetilde{S}}
to
S
cannot
always be thought of
as in §
5
,
because
{\widetilde{S}}
need not be preserved by
\Gamma
,
but
must in general rather be thought of as
‘extension’ from
{\widetilde{S}}
to
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, followed by
‘restriction’ from
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
to
S
.
Thus, for example, it does not always make sense to say that
the fibers of the restriction map

{\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})}\longrightarrow{\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)}

are
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
-orbits; but it does make sense to say, and,
even better, by
Proposition
5.2
(
b
),
is true,
that, for every
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
,
the set of elements of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that
‘restrict’ to
a
is a
(
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
)-orbit, and that
the set of elements of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})
that
restrict to
a
is parametrized by the

\Gal(k)
-orbits in that
(
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
)-orbit.

Remark 6.8
.

Since
\Gamma
is smooth, we have that

\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is Zariski dense in
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, so

({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
equals
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
, and hence

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is the co-invariant module

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
.

If
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint (respectively, simply connected), then

\Gamma
permutes
the basis of
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
given by
the
{\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-simple roots
(respectively, the dual to the basis of
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
consisting of

{\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-simple coroots),
so that the co-invariant module

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})={\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
is
torsion free.
Thus
{\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma}
is a torus, hence smooth.

We will apply
Lemma
6.9
and
Corollary
6.10
only to
the (
\Gamma\ltimes{\widetilde{G}}
)-module
{\widetilde{V}}=\Lie({\widetilde{G}})
.
However, the slight extra generality in our statements
involves no extra difficulty in the proof.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
{\widetilde{V}}
be a
(\Gamma\ltimes{\widetilde{G}})
-module such that

\Phi({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
equals

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Fix
{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and
write
a
for its ‘restriction’ to
S
.
Write
\pi_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
for
the
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-equivariant projection of
{\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
on
its
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
-weight space.
The restriction of
\pi_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
is
a
{\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma}(k)
-equivariant,
k
-linear isomorphism

{{\widetilde{V}}^{\Gamma}_{a}}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\stab_{\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})}}

of
k
-vector spaces.

Proof.
Put
\Sigma=\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
It is clear that
\pi_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
is

\stab_{\Sigma}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
-equivariant, so maps

{\widetilde{V}}_{a}
into

({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\stab_{\Sigma}({\widetilde{\alpha}})}
.
The map in the other direction that sends

{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}

to

\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma/{\stab_{\Sigma}({\widetilde{\alpha}})}}\sigma{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in({\widetilde{V}}_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Sigma}={\widetilde{V}}_{a}^{\Gamma}

is clearly a section, and
Proposition
5.2
(
b
) shows that
it is also a retraction.
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

Preserve the notation and hypotheses of
Lemma
6.9
.
Suppose that
({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
is one-dimensional.
Let
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
act on
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
through
the projection on
\Gal(k)
, and write

k_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
for the fixed field in
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
of

\stab_{\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
.

(a)

a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{V}}^{\Gamma},S)
if and only if
the
k_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
-vector space

({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\stab_{\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})}

is one-dimensional.

(b)

Suppose that
\Gal(k)
fixes
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
, and put

{\widetilde{V}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}=({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\Gal(k)}
.
Then
a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{V}}^{\Gamma},S)
if and only if

\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
acts trivially on

{\widetilde{V}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, in which case
the projection

{{\widetilde{V}}_{a}^{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{V}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.
Put
\Sigma=\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Since

({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
is one-dimensional over
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,
we have that its space of
\stab_{\Sigma}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
-fixed points is
at most one-dimensional over
k_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
.
Thus
(
a
) follows from
Lemma
6.9
.

For (
b
), suppose that

\Gal(k)
fixes
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
.
Then

\stab_{\Sigma}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
equals

\Gal(k)\ltimes\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
, so
the dimension over
k
of

({\widetilde{V}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\stab_{\Sigma}({\widetilde{\alpha}})}
is
the dimension over
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
of

({\widetilde{V}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\otimes{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})}
.
In particular, by (
a
),
we have that
a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
if and only if

({\widetilde{V}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})}

is one-dimensional, i.e., if and only if

\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
acts trivially on
(the one-dimensional
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-vector space)

{\widetilde{V}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
; and, when this happens,
Lemma
6.9

gives that the projection

{{\widetilde{V}}^{\Gamma}_{a}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{V}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}}

is an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

We show how to apply Corollary
5.13
in our situation.

If
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
is an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}
, then

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is
the union of the
\Gal(k)
-orbit of
an irreducible component
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
and
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1},S)
is
the set of ‘restrictions’ of the elements of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
to
S
,
hence is an irreducible component of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.

Conversely, if

\Phi
is an irreducible component of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
and

{\widetilde{\Phi}}
is an element of the corresponding
(
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
)-orbit of irreducible components of

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, then
the
\Gal(k)
-orbit of
{\widetilde{\Phi}}
corresponds to
an irreducible component of

\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})
, hence to
an almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Then
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1},S)
is the set of restrictions to
S
of
elements of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1},{\widetilde{S}})
, hence equals
\Phi
.

Remark 6.12
.

Suppose that
{\widetilde{G}}
is split.
Then

\Gal(k)
acts trivially on
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}({\widetilde{T}})
, and
the almost-simple components of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
are
absolutely almost simple and so are
permuted by
\Gamma(k)
.
Let
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
be an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
,
and
write
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
for the subgroup of
\Gamma
that
acts on
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
by inner automorphisms.
Suppose that
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1},T)
is not reduced.

(a)

Since

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}})
is reduced,
Proposition
5.2
(
5
)
gives that
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
is split adjoint of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
,
i.e., isomorphic to
\PGL_{2n+1}
,
for some positive integer
n
, and that
not every element of
\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
acts
by inner automorphisms on
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
.
Since
\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})/\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
maps into
the automorphism group of the Dynkin diagram of
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}

(with respect to
({\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
) and so has
cardinality at most
2
, this implies that

\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})/\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is an étale group of order
2
.

(b)

If
{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\beta}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}})

are such that
{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\beta}}
belongs to

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}})
, then
the fact that

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{1}}
induces isomorphisms on
appropriate root groups and weight spaces,
together with
explicit computation in
\mathfrak{pgl}_{2n+1}
,
shows that
the unique
{\widetilde{T}}
-equivariant isomorphisms

{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{G}})_{\widetilde{r}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{r}}}

for
\widetilde{r}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime},{\widetilde{T}})

fit together into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\times{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\beta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{U}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\beta}}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{G}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\times\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{G}})_{\widetilde{\beta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{G}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\beta}}},\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the top arrow is the group commutator and
the bottom arrow is the Lie-algebra commutator;
and that the latter gives a
k
-linear isomorphism

{\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\otimes_{k}\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{\widetilde{\beta}}}\longrightarrow{\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\beta}}}}
.

Definition 6.13
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is a subset of
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(S)
, then
we write
{\widetilde{G}}_{\mathcal{R}}
for
the derived subgroup of
the identity component of
the centralizer of

\bigcap_{a\in\mathcal{R}}\ker(a)
, so that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{\mathcal{R}},S)
equals

{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
We define
{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}
similarly for
a subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
of
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{S}})
.

If
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
is
a system of positive roots for

{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)

(for example, if there is some system

\Delta
of simple roots for
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
such that
every element of
\mathcal{R}
is
a non-negative integer multiple of
an element of
\Delta
), then
Remark
5.1
gives that
the set of all roots of
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
whose ‘restriction’ to
S

lies in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
is
a (
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
)-stable system of positive roots for

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{\mathcal{R}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Write

{\widetilde{B}}_{\mathcal{R}}
for the corresponding Borel subgroup of

{\widetilde{G}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, and

{\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{R}}
for the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{B}}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is a singleton
\left\{a\right\}
, then
we may write
{\widetilde{G}}_{a}
,
{\widetilde{B}}_{a}
, and
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
in place of

{\widetilde{G}}_{\mathcal{R}}
,
{\widetilde{B}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, and
{\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Remark 6.14
.

Preserve the notation and hypotheses of Definition
6.13
,
including the assumption that

{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
is
a system of positive roots for

{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.

We have by
Proposition
6.5
(
b
)
that
S
has no fixed points in
\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{R}})
, so

{\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{R}}
is an

S
-stable, smooth, connected subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
whose
Lie algebra is
the sum of the weight spaces for
S
on
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})

corresponding to weights in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
By
[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition 3.3.6,
these properties characterize
{\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{R}}
uniquely; in fact,

{\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{R}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is the unique

S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-stable, smooth, connected subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
whose
Lie algebra is
the sum of the weight spaces for
S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
on
\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

corresponding to weights in

{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{R}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.

In particular,
if
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{R}\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
is empty, then

{\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{R}}
is trivial.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
{\widetilde{H}}
be a subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}
.
Write
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}
for the set of
non-divisible elements of
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
, i.e.,
elements
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
such that

a/2
does not belong to
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
.

(a)

An element of
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
is
multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
if and only if it is
multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.

(b)

\Lie({\widetilde{H}})
equals

\Lie({\widetilde{H}}\cap{\widetilde{T}})\oplus\sum_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}}\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a})
.

(c)

\Phi({\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is the set of elements of

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
whose
‘restriction’ to
S
lies in

\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
.

(d)

If
{\widetilde{H}}
is smooth and connected, then
it is generated by

{\widetilde{H}}\cap{\widetilde{T}}
and

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
as
a
ranges over
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}
.

(e)

If
{\widetilde{H}}
is semisimple, then
it is generated by

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
as
a
ranges over
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}
.

(f)

If
{\widetilde{H}}
is smooth and
contained in the unipotent radical of
a Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
containing
{\widetilde{T}}
, then

{\widetilde{H}}
is connected and
directly spanned by

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
as
a
ranges over
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}
,
in any order.

Note
.

The terminology ‘directly spanned’ in
(
f
) is as in
[
borel:linear
]
*§14.3.
The Borel subgroup in (
f
) is not
assumed to be preserved by
\Gamma
.

Proof.
Suppose that
a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
, and
let
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
be a weight of
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
on

\Lie({\widetilde{H}})_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
Since
\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
is one dimensional and
intersects
\Lie({\widetilde{H}})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
nontrivially, it is contained in

\Lie({\widetilde{H}})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
Since
\Lie({\widetilde{H}})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is preserved by

\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, we have by
Proposition
5.2
(
b
) that

\Lie({\widetilde{H}})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
contains
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence that

\Lie({\widetilde{H}})
contains
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}
.

It is clear that, if
a
is multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
, then
it is multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
For the converse, suppose that
a
is multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
Let
\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}
be
an exceptional pair for

(\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}),\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}}))

‘extending’
a
.
Since
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is reduced,
we have by
Proposition
5.2
(
5
)
that we are in case
(
5ii
),
hence, by Remark
6.12
(
b
), that
the commutator map

{\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\otimes_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}\longrightarrow{\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}

is an isomorphism.
Since

\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
and
\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}

are both contained in
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,
hence in
\Lie({\widetilde{H}})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, so is their commutator
