\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}
.
That is,
2a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
.
This shows (
a
).

We have shown that
\Lie({\widetilde{H}})
contains

\sum_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}}\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}\oplus\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{2a}=\sum_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}}\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a})
, and
it is clear that it contains
\Lie({\widetilde{H}}\cap{\widetilde{T}})
.
This shows the containment
\supseteq
in
(
b
).
The reverse containment follows from the equality

\Lie({\widetilde{H}})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}=(\Lie({\widetilde{H}}\cap{\widetilde{T}})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}})\oplus\bigoplus_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})}\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}

and the fact that,
if
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
is an element of
\Phi({\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
and
we write
a
for the ‘restriction’ of
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
to
S
, then

\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
is contained in

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and, if
a
is divisible, in

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a/2})\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
This shows
(
b
,
c
).

In the situation of (
f
),
we have by
[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 14.4(2a) that

{\widetilde{H}}
is connected.
Thus we may, and do, assume for the remainder of the proof that

{\widetilde{H}}
is smooth and connected.

Together with (
b
),
Remark
6.14
and

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition 3.3.6
give that
{\widetilde{H}}
contains every root subgroup
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}

corresponding to an element
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
.
Therefore, the subgroup
{\widetilde{H}}^{\prime}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
generated by

{\widetilde{H}}\cap{\widetilde{T}}
, and
those
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
with
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}
,
is contained in
{\widetilde{H}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{H}}\cap{\widetilde{T}}
is the fixed-point subgroup of
{\widetilde{T}}
on
the
{\widetilde{T}}
-stable, smooth, connected subgroup
{\widetilde{H}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
we have that it is smooth

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2), so

{\widetilde{H}}^{\prime}
is smooth.
Since

\Lie({\widetilde{H}}^{\prime})
contains

\Lie({\widetilde{H}}\cap{\widetilde{T}})\oplus\sum_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}}\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a})=\Lie({\widetilde{H}})
,
it follows that
{\widetilde{H}}^{\prime}
equals
{\widetilde{H}}
.
This shows (
d
).

In the situation of (
f
), we have by

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 14.4(2a)
(which we have already used to show that
{\widetilde{H}}
is connected)
that

{\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is directly spanned by

{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
as
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
ranges over

\Phi({\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, in any order, but also
for every
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)
that

{\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is directly spanned by

{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
as
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
ranges over
the ‘extensions’ of
a
and
2a
in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
again in any order.
Grouping the elements of
\Phi({\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

according to their ‘restrictions’ to
S
thus shows that
(
f
) holds
after base change to
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence already holds rationally.

Finally, suppose that
{\widetilde{H}}
is semisimple.
Then the subgroup
{\widetilde{H}}^{\prime\prime}
of
{\widetilde{H}}
generated by
those
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
with
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}},S)_{\textup{nd}}
has the property that,
for every
{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
with
‘restriction’ to
S
equal to
a
or
2a
,
the base-changed group
{\widetilde{H}}^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
contains

{\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
, hence its subgroup
{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is a split, semisimple group, it is
generated by its root subgroups, so

{\widetilde{H}}^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
equals
{\widetilde{H}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and hence

{\widetilde{H}}^{\prime\prime}
equals
{\widetilde{H}}
.
This shows (
e
).
∎

Notation 6.16
.

Since
\Gamma^{\circ}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
acts trivially on
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}

(by Remark
2.2.8
(
b
)),
we have that
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
acts on

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
If
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
is a subset of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})
, then
we write
\Gamma_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}
for
the descent to
k
of
the subgroup of
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
generated by

\Gamma^{\circ}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and
the subgroup of
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
preserving
the subset of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

consisting of elements whose ‘restriction’ to
{\widetilde{S}}
belongs to

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
.

If
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
is a singleton
{\widetilde{a}}
, then we
may write
\Gamma_{\widetilde{a}}
in place of
\Gamma_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}
.

Remark 6.17
.

If
\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}
is
an exceptional pair for

(\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}),\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}}))
, and

{\widetilde{a}}
and
{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}
are the ‘restrictions’ to
{\widetilde{S}}
of

{\widetilde{\alpha}}
and
{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
, then
Lemma
5.8
(
c
)
shows that

\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
and

\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}+{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}}

are both the descent to
k
of the subgroup of
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
generated by

\Gamma^{\circ}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and
the intersection of the stabilizers in
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
of
the irreducible components of

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that
contain some
\Gal(k)
-conjugate of

{\widetilde{\alpha}}
or
{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
, so
they are equal.

If
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
and
{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
are
fixed by
\Gal(k)
, so that

\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is
the descent to
k
of

\Gamma_{{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\,\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}}
, and
analogously for

\Gamma_{\widetilde{a}}
and
\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}}
, then
Proposition
5.2
(
b
)
gives that

\Gamma_{\widetilde{a}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})=\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}})
and

\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})=\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}({\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime})

are the same index-
2
subgroup of

\stab_{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}=\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Thus

\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
are
the same index-
2
subgroup of

\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
, so

\Gamma_{\widetilde{a}}
and
\Gamma_{{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}}
are
the same index-
2
(open) subgroup of

\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
.

Corollary 6.18
.

Fix an element
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
If
a
is non-multipliable, then let

{\widetilde{\alpha}}={\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
be a weight of
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
on

\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
If
a
is multipliable, then let

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}
be
an exceptional pair for

(\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}),\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}}))

‘extending’
a
.
In either case, write

{\widetilde{a}}
and
{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}
for
the ‘restrictions’ to
{\widetilde{S}}
of

{\widetilde{\alpha}}
and
{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
.
Then

{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is
an almost-simple factor of

{\widetilde{G}}_{a}
.
The corresponding map

{\psi_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}_{a}}\longrightarrow{\Ind_{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}\,\textup{ad}}}

from Lemma
4.3.4

is a central isogeny onto its image, and
restricts to an isomorphism of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
on its image, which is
contained in

\Ind_{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
.
If
\Gamma/\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is constant, then

\psi_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is surjective, and
restricts to an isomorphism of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
onto

\Ind_{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
.

Proof.
Remark
6.11
and
Proposition
5.2
(
4
,
5
)
give that
{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is
an almost-simple factor of
{\widetilde{G}}_{a}
, and,
together with
Lemma
4.3.4
(
3
), that

{\widetilde{G}}_{a}
is
the smallest
\Gamma
-stable subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}

containing
{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
.
Therefore,
Lemma
4.3.4
(
4
)
gives that
\psi_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is
a central isogeny onto its image.
Since
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
is unipotent, its intersection with

\ker(\psi_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}})
, which is
central in
{\widetilde{G}}_{a}
and so of multiplicative type, is
trivial, so that the restriction of
\psi_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
to

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
is an isomorphism onto its image.
Thus,
if we write
{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}
for the image of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
in

{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}\,\textup{ad}}
under the map of
Lemma
4.3.4
(
b
), then,
since all weights of
S
on
\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a})
lie in

{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\cdot a
,
it follows that all weights of
S
on
\Lie({\widetilde{U}}^{\prime})

also lie in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\cdot a
.
However, the set of elements of

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}},{\widetilde{S}})=({\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{a}}+{\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime})\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})

that restrict to an element of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\cdot a
is

({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}{\widetilde{a}}+{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime})\cap\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}})=\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}},{\widetilde{S}})
, for each of which
the corresponding root group for
{\widetilde{S}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}
is
contained in
{\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
.
That is, the image of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
in

{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}\,\textup{ad}}
is
contained in
{\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
, so
the image of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
in

\Ind_{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is
contained in

\Ind_{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
.

If
\Gamma/\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is constant, then
Lemma
4.3.5

implies that
\psi_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is surjective.
It follows from

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 11.14(1) and
the fact that

\Ind_{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is
the unipotent radical of a Borel subgroup of

\Ind_{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}}^{\Gamma}{\widetilde{G}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{a}},{\widetilde{a}}^{\prime}\right\}}
that
it is the image of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
.
∎

Although it must be well known, we could not find a reference for
the statement in Lemma
6.19

about derived subgroups of root groups even when

\Gamma
acts trivially, so that we are just talking about
ordinary relative-root subgroups.

Lemma 6.19
.

Fix
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
The derived subgroup of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
is
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
, which is
central in
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
.
The quotient
({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
carries a unique

S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-equivariant linear structure
(i.e.,
S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-equivariant isomorphism with

\underline{\Lie}(({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
whose derivative is the identity).
This linear structure is also

(\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
-equivariant.
It descends to linear structures on

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
and

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.
Since
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
is a root system
(by Remark
6.7
), we have that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)\cap{\mathbb{Z}}\cdot a
is contained in

\pm\left\{a,2a\right\}
.
In particular,

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*
Proposition 3.3.5 and Example 3.3.2 give that

{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
is central in
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
.
We have by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Lemma 3.3.8 that there is
a unique
S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-equivariant linear structure on

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
; and uniqueness shows that
it is fixed by both
\Gal(k)
and
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
hence by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}

(because
\Gamma\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}
is smooth).
It follows that the
S_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-equivariant linear structure on

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}

descends to linear structures on

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
and

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})^{\Gamma}
, as claimed.

Since
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
is commutative,

{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
contains
the derived subgroup of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
.
If
a
is not multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
, then

{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
is trivial, so the reverse containment, and
hence equality, is clear.
Thus we may, and do, assume that
a
is multipliable.
Let
\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}
be
an exceptional pair for

(\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}),\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}}))

‘extending’
a
.
By Remark
6.12
(
b
),
the commutator map

{{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\times{\widetilde{U}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{U}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}}

is surjective; so, since

{\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
and
{\widetilde{U}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}
are
contained in
{\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
, we have that

{\widetilde{U}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}
is contained in

({\widetilde{U}}_{a})_{{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\,\textup{der}}=({\widetilde{U}}_{a\,\textup{der}})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
It follows that
{\widetilde{U}}_{a\,\textup{der}}
, which is
contained in
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
, is not
the trivial group, so that

\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{a\,\textup{der}},S)
equals
\left\{2a\right\}
.
Then
Lemma
6.15
(
d
)
gives that

{\widetilde{U}}_{a\,\textup{der}}
contains, hence equals,
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
.
∎

Lemma 6.20
.

Fix
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
If
a
is not multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
, or

a
does not belong to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
, or

p
is odd, then

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}
carries a unique

T
-equivariant linear structure.

Note
.

Lemma
6.20
can fail if

a
is split for
(\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}),\Gal(k))
,
even if

p
is odd and

\Gamma
is trivial.
For example, suppose that

E/k
is a quadratic, Galois extension,
write
{\widetilde{G}}
for the quasisplit
\SU_{3,E/k}

and
\Gamma
for the trivial group,
with (necessarily) its trivial action on
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Thus,
G\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
equals
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Let
S={\widetilde{S}}
be a maximal split torus in
G={\widetilde{G}}
.
Then there are multipliable elements of

\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)=\Phi(G,S)
, and,
for every such element
a
, we have that

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}
is the full
a
-root subgroup for

{\widetilde{S}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}
, and that

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{der}}}={\widetilde{U}}_{a\,\textup{der}}
equals

{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
, which is nontrivial, by
Lemma
6.19
.
In particular,
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
is not even commutative, so
certainly not a vector group.

Proof.
Lemma
6.15
(
f
)
(applied, here and later, with

{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
in place of
k
, hence

T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
in place of
S
)
gives that

{\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is
directly spanned by

{\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}
as

\alpha
ranges over the ‘extensions’ of
a
in

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
If
a
does not belong to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
, then

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
equals
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
, and
the result follows from Lemma
6.19

(with
2a
replacing
a
).
Thus we may, and do, assume that

a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
Then every weight of
T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
on
\Lie(G)_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is
an ‘extension’ of
a
that
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, so,
by Proposition
5.2
(
b
),
all such ‘extensions’ do.
If
a
is non-multipliable, then
so is every ‘extension’.

Thus we may, and do, assume, upon replacing

a
by an ‘extension’
\alpha\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

and

k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
(hence
S
by
T
), that

\alpha
is non-multipliable or

p
is odd.
Now
[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Lemma 3.3.8
will allow us to conclude if we can show that

\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma},T)
equals
\left\{\alpha\right\}
.
If
\alpha
is not multipliable, then
this is obvious
(since
\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha},T)
contains
the positive-integer multiples of
\alpha
in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},T)
,
and
\alpha
is the only such).
Thus we may, and do, assume that

p
is odd and

\alpha
is multipliable.

Since
k
is separably closed, we have that

\Gamma/\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}}
is constant.
Therefore, by Corollary
6.18
(and using its notation),
we have by Lemma
A.20
that

{\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma}
is
T
-equivariantly isomorphic to

{\widetilde{U}}_{\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}}^{\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}}}
.
By Remark
5.14
(
a
),
there is an element
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma(k)
that
swaps
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
and
{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
, hence belongs to

\Gamma_{\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}}(k)
.
Let
X
be a nonzero element of

\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{\alpha}^{\Gamma}
.
By Lemma
6.9
,
the
{\widetilde{T}}
-equivariant projections

{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
and
{\widetilde{X}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}
of

X
on the indicated weight spaces for
{\widetilde{T}}
in
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})
are
also nonzero.
By Remark
6.12
(
b
),
the commutator

{\widetilde{Y}}\mathrel{:=}[{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{X}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}]
is also nonzero.
On the other hand, since
X
is preserved by
\gamma_{0}
,
we have that

\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}})
equals

{\widetilde{X}}_{\gamma_{0}{\widetilde{\alpha}}}={\widetilde{X}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}
,
and, similarly,

\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{X}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}})
equals

{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
, so

\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{Y}})
equals

[{\widetilde{X}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}},{\widetilde{X}}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}]=-{\widetilde{Y}}
.
Since
p
is odd, we have that

{\widetilde{Y}}
does not equal
-{\widetilde{Y}}
, so that

\gamma_{0}
does not act trivially on

\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}
.
Since
\gamma_{0}
preserves
{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
,
Corollary
6.10
(
b
)
gives that the restriction
2\alpha
of

{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
to

T
does not
belong to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)
.
That is,
\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma},T)
equals
\left\{\alpha\right\}
,
as required.
∎

Usually, dealing with the case where
p
equals
2
is harder than
dealing with the case where
p
is odd.
Proposition
6.21
(
3
)
shows that, unusually,
dealing with multipliable roots is easier when
p
equals
2
,
in the sense that a root in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
that
is multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
remains multipliable in

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
See
Proposition
7.6
(
b
) and

[
adler-lansky-spice:actions3
]
*Lemma
for
applications of Proposition
6.21
.

Proposition 6.21
.

Suppose that
p
equals
2
and
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)

is multipliable.
Write
(\cdot)^{[2]}
for the
2
-power map on

\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, Proposition 3.4

(a)

(\cdot)^{[2]}
is a
(\Gamma\ltimes{\widetilde{T}})
-equivariant map

{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{2a})}
that
factors uniquely through

{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})}
.

Let
\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}},{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}\right\}
be
an exceptional pair for

(\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}),\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}}))

‘extending’
a
.

(2)

With the notation of
Lemma
6.9
,
we have that

\pi_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}({\widetilde{X}}^{[2]})
equals

[\pi_{\widetilde{\alpha}}({\widetilde{X}}),\pi_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}({\widetilde{X}})]

for all functorial points
{\widetilde{X}}
of
\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})
.

(3)

(\cdot)^{[2]}
does not take the value
0
on

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})^{\Gamma}\smallsetminus\left\{0\right\}
.
In particular, if

a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
, then
so does
2a
.

Proof.
It is clear that the
2
-power map is
compatible with base change, and
functorial

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, 1.1.
Thus

((\cdot)^{[2]})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is (
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
)-,
hence (
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
)-,
equivariant,
so
(\cdot)^{[2]}
is (
\Gamma\ltimes{\widetilde{T}}
)-equivariant.
In particular, we have by

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, Définition 3.3(
p
-AL 1) that

(\cdot)^{[2]}
doubles weights of
S
, hence
carries
\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})
into
\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{2a})
.
Since

{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
is a vector group by Lemma
6.19
,
we have that

\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{2a})
is annihilated by
(\cdot)^{[2]}

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, Exemple 2.2(1).
Since
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}
is central in
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
by
Lemma
6.19
, and since

{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})}
is
surjective, we have by

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, Définition 3.3(
p
-AL 3)
(which simplifies to

({\widetilde{X}}+{\widetilde{Y}})^{[2]}={\widetilde{X}}^{[2]}+[{\widetilde{X}},{\widetilde{Y}}]+{\widetilde{Y}}^{[2]}
for all
functorial points
{\widetilde{X}}
and
{\widetilde{Y}}
of
\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})

when
p
equals
2
)
that the
[2]
-power map factors uniquely through

{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})}
.
This shows (
a
).

We now prove (
2
).
The
2
-power map
((\cdot)^{[2]})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
annihilates

\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{\widetilde{\beta}})
for every

{\widetilde{\beta}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

(indeed, for every

{\widetilde{\beta}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
).
Thus applying

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, Définition 3.3(
p
-AL 3)
iteratively to the computation of the
2
-power of an element of

\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})=\sum_{{\widetilde{\beta}}\in\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})}\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{\beta})

shows that it is a sum of iterated commutators of vectors in
the various
\underline{\Lie}({\widetilde{U}}_{\beta})
.
By
Proposition
5.2
(
5
)(
5ii
),
the only nontrivial (i.e., with more than one term)
expression of

{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}
as
a sum of elements of
\Phi({\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}},{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is
the obvious one, so
the equality in (
2
) follows.

Now
(
2
),
Lemma
6.9
,
and
Remark
6.12
(
b
) give that

\pi_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}^{\prime}}(X^{[2]})
,
hence
X^{[2]}
itself,
is nonzero for every
X\in\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})^{\Gamma}
.
This shows (
3
).
∎

Corollary
6.23
says that
an inert, multipliable root in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)

disappears upon smoothing, i.e., does not belong to
\Phi(G,S)
.
However, we can say more than this about the structure of

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}
, and do so in
Proposition
6.22
.
Specifically, we view

(

) as
a computation of the connecting map in the usual

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §3, Proposition 1.3
on Hochschild cohomology, in the sense of

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*Ch. II, §3, 1.1.
Note that
Proposition
6.22
(
b
)
is stronger than
Proposition
6.21
(
3
),
but we use the latter in the proof of the former.

Proposition 6.22
.

Preserve the notation and hypotheses of

, and

to regard

as
a

{{\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}}

\textstyle{{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}^{\Gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Proof.
If

Thus we may, and do, assume that

.
Then every weight of

on

in

By Definition

, since

as

.

)(

of

by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,

by

, and

Put

Since

In particular,

Notice that, if we replace

a subgroup of

Choose a nonzero element

For each

Since

If

By inspection, the diagram

\textstyle{({\widetilde{U}}_{1}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2})^{\Gamma_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

)(

acts trivially on

, we have that

is fixed by
\Gamma_{2}
.
Since

(

\Gamma_{2}

.

Corollary 6.23
.

Preserve the notation and hypotheses of
Proposition
6.22
.
Then
({\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.
Lemma
6.20
gives that

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
contains
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}^{\Gamma}
.
Proposition
6.22
(
a
,

)
gives that

\textstyle{{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Frob}
\textstyle{(({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})^{\Gamma})^{(2)}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}

is trivial, hence, since
\Frob
is injective on
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-points, that

{{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{U}}_{a}/{\widetilde{U}}_{2a})^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
is trivial.
Since
({\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{sm}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is
the Zariski closure of
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, we have that

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{sm}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is contained in

({\widetilde{U}}_{2a}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence that

({\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is contained in
{\widetilde{U}}_{2a}^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Corollary 6.24
.

For every
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
, we have that

{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}
is smoothable and connected.

Proof.
Lemma
6.15
(
f
)
shows that
{\widetilde{U}}_{a\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is directly spanned by
subgroups
{\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}
as
\alpha
ranges over
the ‘extensions’ of
a
in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
so we may, and do, assume,
upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence
S
by
T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and

a
by an ‘extension’
\alpha
, that

k
is separably closed.

If
\alpha
is not multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},T)
or

is odd, then
Lemma
6.20

shows that
{\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma}
is smooth and connected.
Otherwise,

6.23
gives that

({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
{\widetilde{U}}_{2\alpha}^{\Gamma}
and

(({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}=({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals

{\widetilde{U}}_{2\alpha_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}=({\widetilde{U}}_{2\alpha}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, so that

{\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma}
is smoothable.
Since the maximal reduced subscheme

(({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}=({\widetilde{U}}_{2\alpha}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

of
({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is connected
(by Lemma
6.20
), also

({\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, hence
{\widetilde{U}}_{\alpha}^{\Gamma}
, is
connected.
∎

Theorem 0
.

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.

Proof.
Let
{\widetilde{B}}
be a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that
contains
S
and
is preserved by
\Gamma
.
Lemma
6.15
(
f
)
gives that
the groups
{\widetilde{U}}_{a}
as
a
ranges over
\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)

directly span the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{B}}
, and
the groups
{\widetilde{U}}_{-a}
as
a
ranges over
\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)

directly span the unipotent radical of the Borel subgroup

{\widetilde{B}}^{-}
opposite to
{\widetilde{B}}
with respect to
{\widetilde{T}}
.
Therefore,
the multiplication map

{\prod_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)}{\widetilde{U}}_{a}\times{\widetilde{T}}\times\prod_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)}{\widetilde{U}}_{-a}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}

is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism of schemes onto
the open subscheme
{\widetilde{B}}\cdot{\widetilde{B}}^{-}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
, so
the multiplication map

{\prod_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)}{\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma}\times{\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma}\times\prod_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)}{\widetilde{U}}_{-a}^{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}}

is an isomorphism of schemes onto
an open subscheme of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
.
Therefore,
the multiplication map

{\prod_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)}({\widetilde{U}}_{a}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\times({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\times\prod_{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{B}},S)}({\widetilde{U}}_{-a}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}}

is an isomorphism onto
an open subscheme
V
of
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
.
Since subgroups of tori are always smoothable

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Corollary A.8.2,
we have by Corollary
6.24
that
there is a smooth subscheme
V^{\prime}
of
V
such that

V^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is the maximal reduced subscheme of
V_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
In particular, since

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
contains
V^{\prime}
, we have that

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is
a closed subscheme of
the irreducible scheme
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
that
contains a nonempty open subset
V^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
of
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
hence is all of
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
That is,
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable by Remark
2.2.2
.
∎

Theorem 0
.

G
is reductive.

Proof.
Note that
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is quasisemisimple.

Theorem
A
(
0
)
and
Remark
2.2.2

give that

G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, so we may, and do, assume,
upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, that

k
is algebraically closed.

Let
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
be a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma
.
We have by
Remark
2.2.8
(
b
) that
the action of
\Gamma^{\circ}
on
{\widetilde{G}}
factors through

{{\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Aut}({\widetilde{G}})}
to give a map

{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}})}
.
Then the image of
\Gamma^{\circ}
in
{\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}})
is
a smooth, connected subgroup of a torus, hence is itself a torus;
so

[
borel:linear
]
*§13.17, Corollary 2(a) gives that

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
is reductive, and

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 11.15 gives that

{\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
is a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
.
In particular,
({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}},{\widetilde{T}})
is
a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
that is
preserved by
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
.
Thus, applying
[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5(i,ii) to
the action of the abstract, finite group
\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)
on

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
gives that

G=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\pi_{0}(\Gamma)(k)}
is reductive.
∎

7.
Fixed points for quasisemisimple actions

We continue to work with
the field
k
of characteristic exponent
p
, and
reductive datum
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
over
k
, from
§
6
, and again put

G=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

Throughout this section, we assume that

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.

Proposition
7.1

is very close to
[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Proposition 3.5
and
[
digne-michel:non-connected
]
*Théorème 1.8,
but stated in a way that is more convenient for our purposes.
In particular, it takes into account questions about fields of definition.
It also allows us to translate Proposition
5.2

to the language of reductive groups.

Proposition 7.1
.

(a)

G
is quasisplit, and
split if
{\widetilde{G}}
is split.

(b)

If
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
is a
\Gamma
-stable Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
,
then

({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
and
({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
are smoothable,
and

(B,T)\mathrel{:=}(({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ},({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})

equals
({\widetilde{B}}\cap G,{\widetilde{T}}\cap G)

and is a Borel–torus pair in
G
.
The map
{\overline{\pi}}\colon{({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})}\longmapsto{(B,T)}

is a surjection from the set of
\Gamma
-stable Borel–torus pairs in
{\widetilde{G}}

onto the set of Borel–torus pairs in
G
.

Proof.
Let
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
be a
\Gamma
-stable Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
put
(B,T)=(({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ},({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
.
As in the proof of Theorem
A
(
0
),
we have by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Corollary A.8.2
that
({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable; so
we have by Remark
2.2.2
that

T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Proposition
6.5
(
a
)
shows that
{\widetilde{T}}\cap G
equals
T
and

C_{G}(S)=C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)\cap G
equals
{\widetilde{T}}\cap G=T
,
where
S
is the maximal split torus in
G
, so that

T
is a maximal torus in
G
.

Let
\delta
be the cocharacter of
T
constructed in
Lemma
6.2
,
so that
{\widetilde{B}}
equals
P_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)
.
Then
{\widetilde{B}}\cap G
equals

P_{\widetilde{G}}(\delta)\cap G=P_{G}(\delta)

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*p. 49.
Since
G
is reductive
(by Theorem
A
(
2
)),
we have by

[
springer:lag
]
*Proposition 8.4.5 that

{\widetilde{B}}\cap G
is
a parabolic subgroup of
G
; but it is also
solvable (because it is a subgroup of
{\widetilde{B}}
), hence is
a Borel subgroup of
G
.
In particular,
{\widetilde{B}}\cap G
is
a smooth, connected subgroup of

{\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}={\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma}
, and so of

({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Since the reverse containment is obvious,

{\widetilde{B}}\cap G
equals
({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Since Remark
2.2.2
gives that

G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
the same argument that showed that

{\widetilde{B}}\cap G
equals
({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
shows that

(({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=({\widetilde{B}}\cap G)_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}={\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\cap G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

equals

({\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
; so
another application of
Remark
2.2.2
gives that

({\widetilde{B}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.

Since
(B,T)
is a Borel–torus pair in
G
,
in particular
G
is quasisplit.
Further, if
{\widetilde{G}}
is split, then so is
{\widetilde{T}}

(because it is a maximal torus in a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
), so
the maximal torus
T
in
G
is split, so

G
is split.
This shows (
a
) and
part of (
b
).
Note that

G(k)
acts on
the set of
\Gamma
-stable Borel–torus pairs in
{\widetilde{G}}
,

\overline{\pi}
is
G(k)
-equivariant, and

G(k)
acts transitively on
the set of Borel–torus pairs in
G

(the rational conjugacy of Borel subgroups is

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Theorem C.2.5, and then
the rational conjugacy, in that Borel subgroup, of
maximal tori in a Borel subgroup is

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Theorem C.2.3).
This shows that
\overline{\pi}
is surjective, and so completes
the proof of (
b
).
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

If
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a smooth, normal subgroup of
\Gamma
, then

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.

Lemma 7.3
.

If
({\widetilde{B}}_{0},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is preserved by
\Gamma
, then

{\{{\tilde{g}}\in{\widetilde{G}}(k)\,|\,\mathopen{}{\tilde{g}}{\widetilde{T}}_{0}\in({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)\}}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)}

is surjective.
If the map

{N_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)}\longrightarrow{W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)}

is surjective, then even

{{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k)}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)}

is surjective.

Proof.
Let
{\widetilde{B}}_{1}
be the Borel subgroup opposite to
{\widetilde{B}}_{0}

with respect to
{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
, and
{\widetilde{U}}_{1}
its unipotent radical.
Then
{\widetilde{B}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is the Borel subgroup opposite to

{\widetilde{B}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
with respect to
{\widetilde{T}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
.
This characterizes
{\widetilde{B}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
uniquely, so

{\widetilde{B}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
, and hence
its unipotent radical
{\widetilde{U}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
, is
preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Since
\Gamma
is smooth, we have that

\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is Zariski dense in
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, so

{\widetilde{U}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is preserved by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and hence

{\widetilde{U}}_{1}
is preserved by
\Gamma
.
Similarly, the unipotent radical
{\widetilde{U}}_{0}
of
{\widetilde{B}}_{0}
itself is
preserved by
\Gamma
.

By
[
springer:lag
]
*Corollary 15.1.4,
there exists an element
{\tilde{g}}\in{\widetilde{G}}(k)
whose image in

({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0})(k)

lies in

({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
.
The double coset
{\widetilde{U}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}{\tilde{g}}{\widetilde{B}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}

is preserved by
\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and
equals
{\widetilde{U}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}w{\widetilde{B}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}

for a unique element
w
of

W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Uniqueness implies that

w
belongs to

W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}=W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gal(k)}=W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
.
Note that the maximal split torus
{\widetilde{S}}_{0}
in
{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
is
maximal split in
{\widetilde{G}}

(because
{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
is contained in
a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
).
Lemma
2.2.3
gives that

w
belongs to
W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{S}}_{0})(k)
, and

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition C.2.10
gives that
w
has a representative
n
in

N_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}}_{0})(k)
, which equals

N_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{T}}_{0})(k)
because

{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{S}}_{0})
.
If
{N_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)}\longrightarrow{W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)}

is surjective, then, of course, we can choose

n\in N_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
.

Put
{\widetilde{U}}_{0\,w}={\widetilde{U}}_{0}\cap\Int(w){\widetilde{U}}_{1}
.
Then
{\widetilde{U}}_{0\,w}
is preserved by
\Gamma
, and
the restriction to
{\widetilde{U}}_{0\,w}n
of the quotient map

{{\widetilde{U}}_{0}w{\widetilde{B}}_{0}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{U}}_{0}w{\widetilde{B}}_{0}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0}}

is an isomorphism of schemes

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Theorem 21.80(b).
If
n
belongs to
N_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
, then
the isomorphism is
\Gamma
-equivariant.
Otherwise, it only becomes
\Gamma
-equivariant after
factoring through the projection to
{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
.
Applying the inverse of this isomorphism to
{\tilde{g}}{\widetilde{B}}_{0}
yields
an element of
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
in general, and even
an element of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k)
if

n
belongs to
N_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
.
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

If
({\widetilde{B}}_{0},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is preserved by
\Gamma
, and

{\widetilde{U}}_{0}
is the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{B}}_{0}
, then

{{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k)}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{U}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)}

is surjective.

Proof.
Since the multiplication map

{{\widetilde{T}}_{0}\ltimes{\widetilde{U}}_{0}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{B}}_{0}}
is an isomorphism,
it follows from Lemma
7.3
that,
given a coset in
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{U}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
,
we may choose a representative

{\tilde{g}}\in{\widetilde{G}}(k)
such that

{\tilde{g}}{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
belongs to
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{T}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
.
Then, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, we have that

{\tilde{g}}^{-1}\gamma({\tilde{g}})
belongs to

{\widetilde{U}}_{0}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})\cap{\widetilde{T}}_{0}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, which is trivial.
That is, as an element of
{\widetilde{G}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, we have that

{\tilde{g}}
is fixed by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, hence,
since
\Gamma
is smooth, by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
Thus
{\tilde{g}}
belongs to

{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})={\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Since
{\tilde{g}}
already belongs to
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
, it
belongs to
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}(k)
.
∎

The statement about the existence of

\Gamma
-stable Levi components of

\Gamma
-stable parabolics in
Proposition
7.5
(
b
)
is
closely related to complete reducibility, in the sense of

[
serre:cr
]
*§3.2.1.
In particular, it says that every
quasisemisimple action is completely reducible.

An easy variant of
Lemma
6.2
shows that,
in the notation of Proposition
7.5
,

{\widetilde{P}}\cap G
is a parabolic subgroup of
G
, and
a bit more work shows that

{\widetilde{U}}^{\Gamma}(k)
is the group of
k
-rational points of
the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{P}}\cap G
; but
we do not need this.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
{\widetilde{P}}
be a parabolic subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}

that is preserved by
\Gamma
.

(a)

There is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{P}}
that is preserved by
\Gamma
.

(b)

For all Levi components
{\widetilde{M}}
of
{\widetilde{P}}
that
are preserved by
\Gamma
,
we have that

({\widetilde{M}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.
Such Levi components exist, and they are all

{\widetilde{U}}^{\Gamma}(k)
-conjugate, where

{\widetilde{U}}
is the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{P}}
.

Proof.
As observed in the proof of Corollary
7.4
,
since
\Gamma
is smooth,
a point of
{\widetilde{G}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that is fixed by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})

belongs to

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Similarly,
a closed subscheme
{\widetilde{X}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
such that

{\widetilde{X}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})

actually has the property that

{\widetilde{X}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is preserved by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and so

{\widetilde{X}}
is preserved by
\Gamma
.

Let
({\widetilde{B}}_{0},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})
be a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}

that is preserved by
\Gamma
.
There is
a minimal
\Gamma
-stable, parabolic subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}

that is contained in
{\widetilde{P}}

(since
{\widetilde{P}}
is Noetherian).
If we write
{\widetilde{U}}
for the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{B}}
, then

{\widetilde{U}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence
preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, so

{\widetilde{U}}
is preserved by
\Gamma
.
Thus
({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{B}}_{0}){\widetilde{U}}
is
a parabolic subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}

[
borel-tits:reductive-groups
]
*Proposition 4.4(b)
that is contained in
{\widetilde{B}}
and preserved by
\Gamma
,
hence equals
{\widetilde{B}}
.
If we write
{\widetilde{U}}_{0}
for the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{B}}_{0}
, then

({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{U}}_{0}){\widetilde{U}}
is a normal, unipotent subgroup of

({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{B}}_{0}){\widetilde{U}}={\widetilde{B}}
, and

{\widetilde{B}}/(({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{U}}_{0}){\widetilde{U}})=(({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{B}}_{0}){\widetilde{U}})/(({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{U}}_{0}){\widetilde{U}})\cong({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{B}}_{0})/({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{U}}_{0})

embeds into

{\widetilde{B}}_{0}/{\widetilde{U}}_{0}\cong{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
, hence is
of multiplicative type.
Thus
{\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is trigonalizable

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Theorem 16.6,
hence solvable

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Theorem 16.21,
so that
{\widetilde{B}}
is a Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.

There is a unique element
{\tilde{g}}{\widetilde{B}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}\in({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})

such that

\Int({\tilde{g}}){\widetilde{B}}_{0\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
equals
{\widetilde{B}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
By uniqueness, it belongs to

({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0})({k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gal(k)\ltimes\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}=({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{B}}_{0})^{\Gamma}(k)
.
By Lemma
7.3
, we may adjust the choice of
representative of the coset to
an element
{\tilde{g}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
such that

{\tilde{g}}{\widetilde{T}}_{0}
is
\Gamma
-fixed.
Then

{\widetilde{T}}\mathrel{:=}\Int({\tilde{g}}){\widetilde{T}}_{0}

is a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is preserved by
\Gamma
and
contained in

\Int({\tilde{g}}){\widetilde{B}}_{0}={\widetilde{B}}
.
This shows (
a
).

The Levi component
{\widetilde{M}}
of
{\widetilde{P}}
that
contains
{\widetilde{T}}
has the property that

{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is the (unique) Levi component of
{\widetilde{P}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
that
contains
{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence is preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
; so

{\widetilde{M}}
is preserved by
\Gamma
.
This shows, in particular, that
such Levi components of
{\widetilde{P}}

exist.

Since
({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{M}},{\widetilde{T}})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{M}}

that is preserved by
\Gamma
,
we have shown that
({\widetilde{M}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple,
but this only handles the particular choice of
Levi component arising as above.
If
{\widetilde{M}}_{1}
is another such Levi component of
{\widetilde{P}}
, then

{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and
{\widetilde{M}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
are
Levi components of
{\widetilde{P}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
that are
preserved by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
By

[
borel:linear
]
*Proposition 11.23(ii),
there is a unique
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-rational point
u
in
the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{P}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, which equals
{\widetilde{U}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,
such that

\Int(u){\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
equals
{\widetilde{M}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and
{\widetilde{M}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
are
preserved by
\Gal(k)
,
we have that
u
is fixed by
\Gal(k)
, i.e.,
belongs to
{\widetilde{U}}(k)
.
Since
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
and
{\widetilde{M}}_{1\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
are
preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
so is
u

(viewed as a point of
{\widetilde{U}}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
)
so that
u\in{\widetilde{U}}^{\Gamma}({k^{\textup{{s}}}})\cap{\widetilde{U}}(k)={\widetilde{U}}^{\Gamma}(k)
.
Thus the quasisemisimplicity of
({\widetilde{M}}_{1},\Gamma)
is witnessed by
the Borel–torus pair
\Int(u)({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{M}},{\widetilde{T}})
.
∎

Theorem 0
.

The functorial map from the spherical building
\mathscr{S}(G)
of
G

to the spherical building
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
of
{\widetilde{G}}

identifies
\mathscr{S}(G)
with

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.

Proof.
Since

\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
acts trivially on
\mathscr{S}(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, we have by
functoriality that the image of the composition

\textstyle{\mathscr{S}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})}

lies in
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.
Since the diagram

\textstyle{\mathscr{S}(G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes, the image of

\mathscr{S}(G)
in
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
actually lies in

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.

Conversely, suppose that
b_{+}
belongs to

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.
Our argument is similar to that of
Lemma
4.4.4
.
With

{\widetilde{P}}^{+}
the parabolic subgroup
P_{\widetilde{G}}(b_{+})
of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
we have that

\gamma{\widetilde{P}}^{+}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=\gamma P_{\widetilde{G}}(b_{+})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

equals

P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}(\gamma b_{{+}\,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})=P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}(b_{{+}\,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})={\widetilde{P}}^{+}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
.
In particular,
{\widetilde{P}}^{+}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
is preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, hence
by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
Proposition
7.5
(
b
)
gives that there is a Levi component
{\widetilde{M}}
of
{\widetilde{P}}^{+}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
Let
{\widetilde{P}}^{-}
be the parabolic subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
that is
opposite to
{\widetilde{P}}^{+}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and satisfies

{\widetilde{P}}^{+}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}\cap{\widetilde{P}}^{-}={\widetilde{M}}
; and then let

b_{-}
be the point of
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that is
opposite to
b_{+}
and
satisfies
P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}(b_{-})={\widetilde{P}}^{-}
.
This condition determines
b_{-}
uniquely, so that it is
preserved by
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, and hence
\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
Since
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}=G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
,
Lemma
4.4.3
gives that

b_{{+}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
belongs to

\mathscr{S}(({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})=\mathscr{S}(G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Then two applications of Lemma
4.4.4
give
first that

b_{{+}\,{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}
is fixed by
\Gal(k)

(by regarding it as an element of
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
), and
then that

b_{+}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(G)
.
∎

As in §
6
,
for the remainder of §
7
, fix
a maximal split torus
S
in
G
, and let

T
and
{\widetilde{T}}
be the maximal split tori in
G
and
{\widetilde{G}}

containing
S
,
and
{\widetilde{S}}
the maximal split torus in
{\widetilde{T}}
.

See
[
adler-lansky-spice:actions3
]
*Corollary

for a sharper version of
Proposition
7.6
when

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is pinned.

Proposition 7.6
.

(a)

For every
a\in\Phi(G,S)
, we have that

\Lie(G)_{a}
equals
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma}_{a}
.

(b)

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)

is a sub-root system of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
that
contains
\Phi(G,S)
.
If
p
is odd, then

\Phi(G,S)
equals
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
If
p
equals
2
, then

\Phi(G,S)
is the set of
roots in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
that
are either non-multipliable (in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
) or
split for
(\Psi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}),\Gal(k))
.

Note
.

Recall that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)

means

\Phi(\Lie({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}),S)=\big\{a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)\,\big|\,\mathopen{}\Lie({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{a}\neq\left\{0\right\}\big\}
.
Since
\Lie({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{a}
equals
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma}_{a}
,
we may also describe
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
as
the set of weights of
S
on
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})
such that
the corresponding weight space admits
nonzero
\Gamma
-fixed vectors.

Proposition
7.6
(
a
)
can be viewed
as saying that, if smoothing does not totally eliminate the
a
-weight space, then it leaves it unchanged.

Proof.
For (
a
), note that the set

\Phi(\Lie(G)_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

of weights of
T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
on
\Lie(G)_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
that
‘extend’
a
is preserved by
\Gal(k)
; so, by
Proposition
5.2
(
b
),
we have that
\Phi(\Lie(G)_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
contains
all ‘extensions’ of
a
to
T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
in

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
That is,

\Phi(\Lie(G)_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
contains

\Phi(\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Since the reverse containment is obvious, we have equality.
Since

\Lie(G)_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
equals

\bigoplus\Lie(G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})_{\alpha}
and

\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}^{\Gamma}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
equals

\bigoplus\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}}_{\alpha}
,
the sums over all

\alpha
in

\Phi(\Lie(G)_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})=\Phi(\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
,
for the purposes of proving (
a
), we may, and do, assume
upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, hence
S
by
T
, and

a
by an ‘extension’
\alpha
, that

k
is separably closed.
Then Corollary
6.10
(
a
)
gives that
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{\alpha}^{\Gamma}
is one-dimensional.
Since
\Lie(G)_{a}
is certainly one-dimensional and contained in
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}^{\Gamma}
,
we have shown
(
a
).

We now turn to (
b
).
This has two claims: that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
is a root system in
the subspace of
V(S)
that it spans, and that

\Phi(G,S)
is a certan subset of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
We prove the latter claim first.

Since
C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
equals
{\widetilde{T}}
by
Proposition
6.5
(
b
),
hence also equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}(T)
,
we have that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
, respectively

\Phi(G,S)
, is the set of ‘restrictions’ of elements of

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, respectively

\Phi(G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
We now apply Lemma
6.20

in the case where
k
is
k^{\textup{{s}}}
and
S
is
T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
.
If
p
is odd, we have that

\Phi(G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
equals

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
, hence that

\Phi(G,S)
equals

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
If
p
equals
2
, then
we have that

\Phi(G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
contains at least the roots in

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
that
are not multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
;
i.e.,
by Proposition
6.21
(
3
),
the non-multipliable elements of

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
.
Since
\Phi(G_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is reduced, it is
precisely the set of such roots.
The set of ‘restrictions’ of such roots
to
S
certainly contains all roots in

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
that are not
multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
.
On the other hand, a root
a
in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
that

is
multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},S)
is
the restriction of a root in
\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

that is
not
multipliable in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},T_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})

if and only if
a
is split. This proves the second part of (
b
).

To show that
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
is a root system,
we must show that, for every

a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
, there is
a cocharacter
a^{\vee}
of
S
such that

\left\langle a^{\vee},a\right\rangle
equals
2
and
the reflection corresponding to
(a,a^{\vee})

preserves
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
Since
the reflections corresponding to

(2a,\frac{1}{2}a^{\vee})
and

(a,a^{\vee})
are the same,
we may, and do, assume that

a
belongs to
\Phi(G,S)

(by replacing
a
by
2a
, if
p
equals
2
and

a
is multipliable).
Then we may take
a^{\vee}
to be the
coroot in
\Phi^{\vee}(G,S)
corresponding to
a
.
Since every element of
W(G,S)(k)
has
a representative in
N_{G}(S)(k)
, we have that
there is some
n\in N_{G}(S)(k)
whose action on
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(S)
is
the reflection corresponding to
(a,a^{\vee})

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Theorem C.2.15.
In particular, since
n
preserves
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
,
the reflection preserves
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
.
This completes the proof of (
b
), and hence
of the result.
∎

Corollary 7.7
.

{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
equals

{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi^{\vee}(G,S)
, and
the natural map

{W(G,S)}\longrightarrow{W(\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S))}

is an isomorphism.

Corollary
7.8
is our main tool for
establishing smoothness or near-smoothness,
in the sense of
Theorem
A
(
1
), of
fixed-point groups.

Corollary 7.8
.

Suppose that
p
is odd or
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
is reduced.
If
{\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth, then
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth.

Proof.
We have that
\Lie({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})
is the sum of the
0
-weight space

\Lie({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{S}=\Lie({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})
for
S
and
the nonzero-weight spaces for
S
.
Since
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},S)
equals
\Phi(G,S)

by Proposition
7.6
(
b
),
it follows from Proposition
7.6
(
a
)
that each weight space in
\Lie({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})
for a nonzero weight is
contained in
\Lie(G)
.
Thus, since

\Lie({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})=\Lie(({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ})
equals

\Lie(({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})=\Lie(T)
,
we have that
\Lie({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})
is contained in, hence equals,

\Lie(G)=\Lie(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
;
so
[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Proposition 10.15
gives that
G
equals
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
.
It follows that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
, and hence
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
, is smooth.
∎

Theorem 0
.

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
equals

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
unless

p
equals
2
and
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is exceptional.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume, upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.
Suppose that
p
does not equal
2
, or

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is not exceptional.

Since

{\Lie({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}\longrightarrow{\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})^{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
and,
by Corollary
4.1.2
, also

{\Lie(G)_{\alpha}}\longrightarrow{\Lie(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{\alpha}}

is an isomorphism for every nonzero
\alpha\in{\mathbf{X}}^{*}(T)
,
we have that

{\Phi(G,T)}\longrightarrow{\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ},T/(Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap T))}

and

{\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)}\longrightarrow{\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma},T/(Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap T))}

are bijections.
Thus,

p
does not equal
2
, or

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}},\Gamma)
is not exceptional.

By Corollary
4.1.3
,
we may, and do, thus assume, upon replacing
{\widetilde{G}}
by
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
, that

{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint, at which point the conclusion becomes that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smooth.

Suppose first that
p
does not equal
2
or

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)
is reduced.
Remark
6.8
gives that
{\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth, so
Corollary
7.8
gives that

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth.

Thus we may, and do, suppose that
p
equals
2
and

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)
is not reduced.
Let
\alpha
be a multipliable element of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)
.
Then Proposition
7.6
(
b
)
gives that
2\alpha
belongs to
\Phi(G,T)
.
That is,
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is exceptional, which is
a contradiction.
∎

It is easy, regardless of (positive) characteristic, for
passage to fixed points to create non-smoothness, but
this non-smoothness should be thought of as
coming from the failure of smoothness for
an action on a torus.
Remark
6.8
thus suggests that
it should be easier for

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma}
than for

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}

to be smooth.
Lemma
7.9
formalizes this idea
for use in the proof of
Lemma
10.1.4
.

Lemma 7.9
.

If
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth and
{\widetilde{N}}
is a

\Gamma
-stable, normal subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
such that

({\widetilde{T}}/{\widetilde{N}}\cap{\widetilde{T}})^{\Gamma}
is smooth, then

({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}})^{\Gamma}
is smooth.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume, upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.

Since
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth, we have that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
equals
G
, so that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is reductive and

T
is a maximal torus in
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
.
These conditions together imply that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)
is reduced

[
borel:linear
]
*Theorem 14.8.

We now reason by contradiction.
Suppose that
({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}})^{\Gamma}
is not smooth.
Corollary
7.8
gives that

p
equals
2
and
\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}})^{\Gamma},T)
is not reduced.
Let
\alpha
be a multipliable element of

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}})^{\Gamma},T)
, and let

{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime}
be an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}}
such that

\alpha
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime},T)
.
Remark
6.11
gives that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime},T)
is an irreducible component of

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}},T)
, and so also contains

2\alpha
; so
Proposition
6.21
(
3
)
gives that

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime})^{\Gamma},T)
contains
2\alpha
.
Remark
6.12
(
a
)
gives that
there is a positive integer
n
such that
({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime})_{\textup{{ad}}}
,
and hence
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime}
, is
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
, and that
there is an element of
\Gamma(k)
that preserves
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime}
but
does not act on it by an inner automorphism.
Write
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
for an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}

whose image in
{\widetilde{G}}/{\widetilde{N}}
is
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime}
.
Then
\ker({{\widetilde{G}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime}})
is a subquotient of

\ker({({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime})_{\textup{{sc}}}}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}_{1})_{\textup{{ad}}}})=\mu_{2n+1}
.
Since
p
equals
2
, we have that
\mu_{2n+1}
, and hence

\ker({{\widetilde{G}}_{1}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime}})
, is étale.
It follows that the (obviously)
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism

{\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{1})}\longrightarrow{\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime})}
is an isomorphism, so

\alpha,2\alpha\in\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\prime})^{\Gamma},T)
also belong to

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\Gamma},T)
, hence to

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)
.
This is a contradiction of the fact that

\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma},T)
is reduced.
∎

8.
Quasisemisimple outer involutions of special linear groups

In this section,
we give an explicit description of an important example that is
already implicit in
the proof of
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 8.2, specifically

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*pp. 53–54, (2'b).
Our explicit understanding is necessary for the proof of
Theorem
A
(
0
).
We will handle another specific example in
§
10.2
.

We continue to work with
the field
k
of
characteristic exponent
p
from
§
7
.

Let

X
be a nonzero, finite-dimensional
k
-vector space,
and put

{\widetilde{G}}=\GL(X)
.
Let

E/k
be a field extension, and

\gamma
an involution of
{\widetilde{G}}
such that

\gamma
acts by inversion on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
, and

\gamma_{E}
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{E}
.
We do
not
yet assume that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}
, although
see Theorem
B
(
2
) for
conditions under which we can conclude this.
For notational convenience, we put
n=\dim(X)-1
.
Remember that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
means
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})^{\gamma}
, not

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{der}}}
, when they differ; and

{\widetilde{G}}_{E}^{\gamma_{E}}
means

({\widetilde{G}}_{E})^{\gamma_{E}}
.

We are most interested in the cases where

p
equals
2
and
n
is even, but we do not require this.

If
b
is a bilinear form on
X
,
then we denote by
q_{b}
the quadratic form

{x}\longmapsto{b(x,x)}
on
X
.
If
p
equals
2
, then

q_{b}
is a linear map

{X}\longrightarrow{{}_{f}k}
, where

{f}\colon{k}\longrightarrow{k}
is the Frobenius automorphism

{x}\longmapsto{x^{2}}
and

{}_{f}k
is the restriction of scalars of

k
along
f
, as in

[
demazure-gabriel:groupes-algebriques
]
*
Ch. II, §7, 1.1.

Lemma
8.1
will help us deal with
the obstruction to smoothability of
the fixed-point group in
Proposition
8.9
.
The statement involves a lot of notation.
It may be informally, but perhaps more clearly, summarized as follows:
the subgroup of a symplectic group fixing a given subspace of
the defining representation is
smooth and connected, and
its maximal pseudo-reductive quotient is
a symplectic group, hence reductive.

Lemma 8.1
.

Suppose that
b
is a nondegenerate, alternating form on
X
.
We denote
b
-orthogonal spaces by
(\cdot)^{\perp}
.
Let
X^{\prime\prime}
be a subspace of
X
, and put

X^{\prime}=X^{\prime\prime}+X^{\prime\prime\,\perp}
.
Write

G^{\prime\prime}
for
the subgroup of
\Sp(X,b)
that fixes
X^{\prime\prime}
pointwise,

U^{\prime}
and
U^{\prime\prime}
for the subgroups
of
G^{\prime\prime}
that fix

X^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp}
pointwise,
and

b^{\prime}
for the alternating form on
X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp}

induced by
b
.
The form
b
puts

X^{\prime\,\perp}
and
X/X^{\prime}
in duality, so that there is
a duality involution on the space

\Hom(X/X^{\prime},X^{\prime\,\perp})
of

k
-linear homomorphisms.
Write
\Skew(X/X^{\prime},X^{\prime\,\perp})
for the space of
skew homomorphisms
(i.e., homomorphisms negated by the duality involution).

(a)

The subspaces

X^{\prime\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp}
and
X^{\prime\prime\,\perp}/X^{\prime\,\perp}
of

X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp}
are complementary and
nondegenerate for
b^{\prime}
.

(b)

The group
U^{\prime}
is
the vector group associated to

\Skew(X/X^{\prime},X^{\prime\,\perp})
.
The group
U^{\prime\prime}
is an extension by
U^{\prime}
of
the vector group associated to

\Hom(X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\prime},X^{\prime\,\perp})
.

(c)

The natural map

{G^{\prime\prime}}\longrightarrow{\Sp(X^{\prime\prime\,\perp}/X^{\prime\,\perp},b^{\prime})}

is a quotient map with kernel

U^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.
(
a
) is clear, and implies that

{\Fix_{\Sp(X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp},b^{\prime})}(X^{\prime\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp})}\longrightarrow{\Sp(X^{\prime\prime\,\perp}/X^{\prime\,\perp},b^{\prime})}

is an isomorphism.

Since
b^{\prime}
puts

X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\prime}
and

X^{\prime\prime\,\perp}/X^{\prime\,\perp}
in duality,
the kernel of the natural map

{G^{\prime\prime}}\longrightarrow{\Sp(X^{\prime\prime\,\perp}/X^{\prime\,\perp},b^{\prime})}

is

\Fix_{\Sp(X,b)}(X^{\prime\prime},X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp})=U^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus (
c
) will follow once we show that

{G^{\prime\prime}/U^{\prime\prime}}\longrightarrow{\Fix_{\Sp(X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp},b^{\prime})}(X^{\prime\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp})}

is surjective.

Choose a complement
Y^{\prime\prime}
to
X^{\prime\,\perp}
in
X^{\prime\prime}
, and
enlarge it to a complement
Y^{\prime}
to
X^{\prime\,\perp}
in
X^{\prime}
.
Since
b
is nondegenerate on
Y^{\prime}
, we have that

X
equals
Y^{\prime}\oplus Y^{\prime\,\perp}
, and hence that
the natural map

{Y^{\prime\,\perp}/X^{\prime\,\perp}}\longrightarrow{X/X^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism.
Since
b
puts
X/X^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime\,\perp}
in duality, it also puts

Y^{\prime\,\perp}/X^{\prime\,\perp}
and
X^{\prime\,\perp}
in duality.
In particular,
X^{\prime\,\perp}
is a maximal
totally isotropic subspace of
Y^{\prime\,\perp}
.
Let
Y
be a complementary (necessarily totally isotropic) subspace.
In addition to the duality involution on

\Hom(X/X^{\prime},X^{\prime\,\perp})\cong\Hom(Y,X^{\prime\,\perp})
mentioned
in the statement, since
Y^{\prime}
is self-dual, we have a duality isomorphism
(\cdot)^{*}
of

\Hom(Y,Y^{\prime})
with
\Hom(Y^{\prime},X^{\prime\,\perp})
.
Choose a polarization of
Y^{\prime}
, i.e., a pair

(Y^{\prime\,{+}},Y^{\prime\,{-}})
of complementary totally isotropic subspaces.
These furnish maps

{Y^{\prime}}\longrightarrow{Y^{\prime\,{\pm}}}
, hence

{(\cdot)^{\pm}}\colon{\Hom(Y,Y^{\prime})}\longrightarrow{\Hom(Y,Y^{\prime\,{\pm}})}
.
Write
\underline{\Skew}(Y,X^{\prime\,\perp})
and
\underline{\Hom}(Y,Y^{\prime})
for
the vector groups associated to

\Skew(Y,X^{\prime\,\perp})
and
\Hom(Y,Y^{\prime})
.
Write
P^{\prime}
for the parabolic subgroup of
\Sp(X,b)
associated to
the self-dual flag

0\subseteq X^{\prime\,\perp}\subseteq X^{\prime}\subseteq X
.
Note that
U^{\prime}
is a normal subgroup of
P^{\prime}
.
We have an isomorphism of schemes,
not
of group schemes,
from

\Sp(Y^{\prime},b^{\prime})\times\underline{\Hom}(Y,Y^{\prime})\times\underline{\Skew}(Y,X^{\prime\,\perp})
onto
the subgroup of
P^{\prime}
that fixes
X^{\prime\,\perp}
pointwise, given by

{(g,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\,\perp})}\longmapsto{\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&g\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\xi^{\prime\,{+}\,{*}}\\
&1&\xi^{\prime\,{+}}\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\xi^{\prime\,{-}\,{*}}\\
&1&\xi^{\prime\,{-}}\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&&\xi^{\prime\,\perp}\\
&1\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}},

where we use block-matrix notation organized as

\bordermatrix{&X^{\prime\,\perp}&Y^{\prime}&Y\cr X^{\prime\,\perp}&*&*&*\cr Y^{\prime}&*&*&*\cr Y&*&*&*}.

Concretely, the embedding sends

(g,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\,\perp})\in\Sp(Y^{\prime},b^{\prime})\times\underline{\Hom}(Y,Y^{\prime})\times\underline{\Skew}(Y,X^{\prime\,\perp})
to
the symplectomorphism

{x^{\prime\,\perp}+y^{\prime}+y}\longmapsto{(x^{\prime\,\perp}-\xi^{\prime\,{*}}(y^{\prime})+(\xi^{\prime\,\perp}-\xi^{\prime\,{+}\,{*}}\xi^{\prime\,{-}})(y))+g(y^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime}(y))+y}

for all
x^{\prime\,\perp}\in X^{\prime\,\perp}
,
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
, and
y\in Y
.
Although this map is not a morphism of group schemes, we have that

•

the restriction of our map to

\underline{\Skew}(Y,X^{\prime\,\perp})
is
an isomorphism of group schemes onto
U^{\prime}
, which
shows part of (
b
);

•

the composition

\textstyle{\Sp(Y^{\prime},b)\times\underline{\Hom}(Y,Y^{\prime})\times\underline{\Skew}(Y,X^{\prime\,\perp})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P^{\prime}/U^{\prime}}

factors uniquely through projection on the first two factors
to give an isomorphism of group schemes from

\Sp(Y^{\prime},b^{\prime})\ltimes\underline{\Hom}(Y,Y^{\prime})
onto
a closed subgroup of
P^{\prime}/U^{\prime}
, and the isomorphism is
independent of the choice of polarization of
Y^{\prime}
.

Now
U^{\prime\prime}
is the inflation to
P^{\prime}
of
the image in
P^{\prime}/U^{\prime}

of
the vector subgroup of
\underline{\Hom}(Y,Y^{\prime})
corresponding to

\left\{\xi^{\prime}\in\Hom(Y,Y^{\prime})\,\middle|\,\mathopen{}\text{$\xi^{\prime\,{*}}$ is trivial on $Y^{\prime\prime}$}\right\}
, which
shows (
b
); and

\Fix_{\Sp(X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp},b^{\prime})}(X^{\prime\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp})\cong\Fix_{\Sp(Y^{\prime},b^{\prime})}(Y^{\prime\prime})

maps isomorphically onto
G^{\prime\prime}/U^{\prime\prime}
, giving
a section of the natural map

{G^{\prime\prime}/U^{\prime\prime}}\longrightarrow{\Fix_{\Sp(X^{\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp},b^{\prime})}(X^{\prime\prime}/X^{\prime\,\perp})}
, which
is therefore surjective.
This shows (
c
), and completes the proof.
∎

Notation 8.2
.

Since

•

\gamma
acts by inversion on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
, and

•

\gamma
conjugates the defining representation
of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
to its dual
(either because

n
is greater than
1
and

\gamma
restricts to a nontrivial outer automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, or because

n
is at most
1
,

\gamma
restricts to an inner automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, and
the defining representation of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
is self-dual),

the
\gamma
-conjugate of
X
is isomorphic to the dual representation
X^{*}
.

Write
\gamma_{X}
for a map
{X}\longrightarrow{X^{*}}

that intertwines the
\gamma
-twisted action of
{\widetilde{G}}
on
X

with the natural action of
{\widetilde{G}}
on
X^{*}
,
and

b
for the associated bilinear form

{(x_{1},x_{2})}\longmapsto{\left\langle\gamma_{X}(x_{1}),x_{2}\right\rangle}
.
We will always use the notation
\perp
for
orthogonal spaces with respect to
b
.
That is, if
X^{\prime}
is a subspace of
X
, then

X^{\prime\,\perp}
means

\left\{x\in X\,\middle|\,\mathopen{}\text{$b(x,x^{\prime})=0$ for all $x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}$}\right\}
.

Since
X
and
X^{*}
are irreducible representations of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
the map
\gamma_{X}
,
and hence the bilinear form
b
,
in Notation
8.2

are uniquely determined up to
multiplication by a nonzero scalar.

We have that
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is the full isometry group

\Isom(X,b)
of
b
, and

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
is the group of determinant-
1
isometries.
In particular,
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is contained in the orthogonal group

\Or(X,q_{b})
of
q_{b}
.
Since
\gamma

acts by inversion on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
,
we have that
\det\circ\gamma
equals
-{\det}
as
characters of
{\widetilde{G}}
, so

\det({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})
is contained in
\mu_{2}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

The pairing
b
is symmetric or anti-symmetric.
If
n
is even, then
b
is symmetric.

Proof.
Since
\gamma
is an involution,
we have that the dual map
{\gamma_{X}^{*}}\colon{X}\longrightarrow{X^{*}}

to
\gamma_{X}
also intertwines the
\gamma
-conjugate of
X

with
X^{*}
as representations of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
so
\gamma_{X}^{*}
equals
c\gamma_{X}
for some constant
c
.
That is,

b(x_{1},x_{2})=\left\langle\gamma_{X}(x_{1}),x_{2}\right\rangle

equals

\left\langle\gamma_{X}^{*}(x_{2}),x_{1}\right\rangle=c\left\langle\gamma_{X}(x_{1}),x_{2}\right\rangle=cb(x_{2},x_{1})
.

Since
\gamma_{X}^{**}
equals
\gamma_{X}
,
we have that
c^{2}
equals
1
, so that
b
is symmetric or
anti-symmetric.
Moreover,
\det(\gamma_{X}^{-1}\gamma_{X}^{*})=1
equals
c^{n+1}
,
so that if
n
is even, then
c=c^{n+1}(c^{2})^{-n/2}
equals
1

and hence
b
is symmetric.
∎

Fix a Borel–torus pair
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})

in
{\widetilde{G}}_{E}
that is preserved by
\gamma_{E}
.

Notation 8.4
.

Let
\ell_{0},\dotsc,\ell_{n}
be the weight spaces for

{\widetilde{T}}
in
X\otimes_{k}E
, and

e_{0},\dotsc,e_{n}
the corresponding weights,
numbered so that

\Phi({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
equals
\left\{e_{i}-e_{j}\,\middle|\,\mathopen{}i<j\right\}

and

\gamma(e_{i})
equals
-e_{n-i}

for all
0\leq i\leq n
.
Let
(e_{0}^{\vee},\dotsc,e_{n}^{\vee})
be the ordered basis of

{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}})
dual to
(e_{0},\dotsc,e_{n})
,
so that
{\mathbf{X}}_{*}({\widetilde{T}}\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})
is the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-span of
