\left\{e_{i}^{\vee}-e_{j}^{\vee}\,\middle|\,\mathopen{}0\leq i,j\leq n\right\}
.

Remark 8.5
.

For every
0\leq i\leq n
, we have that

(\gamma_{X})_{E}\,(\ell_{i})
is the (
-e_{n-i}
)-weight
space for
{\widetilde{T}}
in
(X\otimes_{k}E)^{*}
,
hence can be
identified with
\ell_{n-i}^{*}
by restriction.
In particular, we have for every
0\leq i,j\leq n

that
\ell_{i}
is
b_{E}
-orthogonal to
\ell_{j}

unless
i+j
equals
n
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Suppose that
p
equals
2
.

(a)

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
equals

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\\
2i\neq n\end{subarray}}^{n}\ell_{i}
.
The restriction
b_{E}^{\prime}
of
b_{E}
to
\ker(q_{b_{E}})

is a nondegenerate, alternating form.

(b)

If
n
is odd, then
\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
equals
\left\{0\right\}
.
If
n
is even, then
\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
equals
\ell_{n/2}
.
In either case, the restriction
b_{E}^{\prime\prime}
of
b_{E}
to

\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
is
nondegenerate.

Proof.
We may, and do, replace
k
by
E
.

For each
0\leq i\leq n
, write
{x}\longmapsto{x_{i}}

for the
{\widetilde{T}}
-equivariant projection
{X}\longrightarrow{\ell_{i}}
.
For convenience, write
{x}\longmapsto{x_{n/2}}
for the
0
map if

n
is odd.
Remark
8.5
and symmetry
(which follows from Lemma
8.3
since
anti-symmetry is the same as symmetry when

p
equals
2
)
give that

q_{b}(x)=b(x,x)\quad\text{equals}\quad b(x_{n/2},x_{n/2})+\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}2b(x_{i},x_{n-i})=b(x_{n/2},x_{n/2})

for all
x\in X
.
This shows that
\ker(q_{b})

equals
\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\\
2i\neq n\end{subarray}}^{n}\ell_{i}
, and
that
b^{\prime}
is alternating.
Now another application of Remark
8.5
shows that

b^{\prime}
is nondegenerate, giving (
a
); and that

\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}
equals

\left\{0\right\}
if
n
is odd, and equals

\ell_{n/2}
if
n
is even,
hence that

b^{\prime\prime}
is nondegenerate,
giving (
b
).
∎

Corollary 8.7
.

Suppose that
p
equals
2
.
Then
\ker(q_{b_{E}})\otimes_{E}L
equals
\ker(q_{b_{L}})
for
all field extensions
L/E
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Suppose that
p
or
n
is odd.
If
b
is anti-symmetric, then

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
and
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
both equal

\Sp(X,b)
, which is smooth and connected.
If
p
is odd and
b
is symmetric, then

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
equals
\Or(X,q_{b})
, which is smooth, and

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
equals
\SO(X,q_{b})
, which is
the identity component of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
.

Proof.
If
p
is odd, then every anti-symmetric form is alternating;
so, if
b
is anti-symmetric, then it is alternating.
If
p
equals
2
and
n
is odd, then
Lemma
8.6
gives that

b_{E}
, and hence
b
, is alternating.
Thus the group
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
of isometries of
b
is

\Sp(X,b)
, which is smooth, connected, and
contained in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
.

An isometry of
b
is always also an isometry of
q_{b}
.
If
p
is odd and
b
is symmetric, then an
isometry of
q_{b}
is also an
isometry of
b
, so
the group
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
of isometries of
b
is

\Or(X,q_{b})
, which is
smooth
[
conrad:red-gp-sch
]
*Theorem C.1.5; and

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
equals

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}=\Or(X,q_{b})\cap\SL(X)
,
which equals
\SO(X,q_{b})
and
is the identity component of
\Or(X,q_{b})
by

[
conrad:red-gp-sch
]
*Theorem C.2.11 and Corollary C.3.2.
∎

Proposition 8.9
.

Suppose that
p
equals
2
.

(a)

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
, and
is the subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
fixing
\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}
pointwise.

(b)

Write
b_{E}^{\prime}
for the restriction of
b_{E}
to

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
.
Extension trivially across
\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
furnishes
an isomorphism onto
(({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E}
from
the subgroup of

\Sp(\ker(q_{b_{E}}),b_{E}^{\prime})
that fixes

\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E)
pointwise.

Note
.

Lemma
8.6
gives that

b_{E}^{\prime}
is a
nondegenerate, alternating form on
\ker(q_{b_{E}})
, so that
it makes sense to speak of
\Sp(\ker(q_{b_{E}}),b_{E}^{\prime})
; and that

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
and
\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
are
complementary subspaces of
X\otimes_{k}E
, so that
the extension map in (
b
) is
well defined.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume, upon replacing

k
and
E
by their separable closures, that they are
separably closed.
Put
X^{\prime}=\ker(q_{b})
and
X^{\prime\prime}=\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}
.

We make a few observations about the extension map of
(
b
).
Remember that

{\widetilde{G}}_{E}^{\gamma_{E}}=({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{E}
equals

\Isom(X,b)_{E}=\Isom(X\otimes_{k}E,b_{E})
.
Since

X^{\prime}\otimes_{k}E=\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E
is
contained in
\ker(q_{b_{E}})
,
we have that

X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E=\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E=(\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E)^{\perp}

contains

\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
.
We observe two consequences.
First,

X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E
is the direct sum of

\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E)
and

\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
, so that the extension map

{\Fix_{\Sp(\ker(q_{b_{E}}),b_{E}^{\prime})}(\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E))}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
has image in

\Isom(X\otimes_{k}E,b_{E})\cap\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}_{E}}(X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E)=\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}_{E}^{\gamma_{E}}}(X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E)=(\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(X^{\prime\prime}))_{E}
.
Second, there is a restriction map

{(\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(X^{\prime\prime}))_{E}}\longrightarrow{\Fix_{\GL(\ker(q_{b_{E}}))}(\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E))}
,
and its image lies in

\Isom(\ker(q_{b_{E}}),b_{E}^{\prime})=\Sp(\ker(q_{b_{E}}),b_{E}^{\prime})
.
These maps are mutually inverse, so that the extension map of
(
b
) is
an isomorphism of

\Fix_{\Sp(\ker(q_{b_{E}}),b_{E}^{\prime})}(\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(X^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{k}E))

onto

(\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(X^{\prime\prime}))_{E}
.
Thus Lemma
8.1
(
b
,
c
)
shows that

(\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(X^{\prime\prime}))_{E}
is smooth, hence that

\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(X^{\prime\prime})
is smooth, and so contained in

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
It remains to show that
({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}

fixes
X^{\prime\prime}
pointwise, hence equals
(\Fix_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(X^{\prime\prime}))_{E}

(completing the proof of (
b
) and part of
(
a
)), and that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}

(proving the other part of (
b
)).

Suppose first that
E
equals
k
,
hence that
\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Lemma
8.6
gives that

b^{\prime}
is a nondegenerate, alternating form on
X^{\prime}
, so

X^{\prime}\cap X^{\prime\prime}
is trivial.
Fix
g\in{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}(k)
.
We have that
\det(g)
belongs to
\mu_{2}(k)=\left\{1\right\}
,
i.e.,
g
belongs to
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}(k)
.
Since
g
fixes
b
, it also fixes
q_{b}
, and hence
preserves
\ker(q_{b})=X^{\prime}
.
Thus
g
also preserves
the
b
-orthogonal space
X^{\prime\prime}
of
X^{\prime}
; and
the restriction of
g
to
X^{\prime}
preserves
b^{\prime}
, hence
belongs to
\Sp(X^{\prime},b^{\prime})(k)
.
By Lemma
8.6
(
b
),
if
n
is odd, then
X^{\prime\prime}
is trivial, so we are done;
whereas, if
n
is even, then
X^{\prime\prime}
is
1
-dimensional,
so that
g
acts on it by a scalar.
In the latter case,
since
g
has determinant
1
,
and the restriction of
g
to
X^{\prime}
also has determinant
1

(because it belongs to
\Sp(X^{\prime},b^{\prime})(k)
),
the scalar by which
g
acts on
X^{\prime\prime}
is also
1
, so that

g
fixes
X^{\prime\prime}
pointwise.
Thus
({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
, which is
the Zariski closure of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}(k)

(because
k
is separably closed),
is contained in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, and fixes
X^{\prime\prime}
.
As observed, this proves the result,
under the assumption that
E
equals
k
.

Now drop the assumption that
E
equals
k

(but keep the assumption that
k
is separably closed).
By the special case of (
a
) that
we have already handled, we have
for every

g\in{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}(k)\subseteq{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}(E)=({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}(E)
that

g
belongs to

({\widetilde{G}}_{E})_{\textup{{der}}}(E)={\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}(E)
, hence to

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}(k)
; and that

g
fixes
\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
pointwise, so that
the fixed-point subspace
\ker(g-1)
of
g
on
X
satisfies
the containment

\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}\subseteq\ker(g-1)\otimes_{k}E
,
from which we deduce successively the containments

\displaystyle\ker(g-1)^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E=(\ker(g-1)\otimes_{k}E)^{\perp}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ker(q_{b_{E}}),

then

\displaystyle\ker(g-1)^{\perp}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap X=\ker(q_{b}),

and finally

\displaystyle X^{\prime\prime}=\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ker(g-1).

That is,

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}(k)
is contained in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}(k)
, and
every element of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}(k)
fixes
X^{\prime\prime}
pointwise; so
the Zariski closure
({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}(k)

is contained in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, hence
equals
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
, and
fixes
X^{\prime\prime}
pointwise.
This completes the proof of the result.
∎

Corollary 8.10
.

Suppose that
p
equals
2
.
The form
b^{\prime}
on
\ker(q_{b})/(\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})

induced by
b

is nondegenerate and alternating, and the
natural map

{({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}=({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}}\longrightarrow{\Sp(\ker(q_{b})/(\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}),b^{\prime})}

is a quotient whose kernel is an extension of
the vector group associated to

\Hom((\ker(q_{b})+\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})/{\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}},\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})

by the vector group associated to the skew-symmetric elements of

\Hom(X/(\ker(q_{b})+\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}),\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}),

i.e., those negated
by the duality involution coming from
b
.

Note
.

That the map in the statement exists follows from
Proposition
8.9
(
a
).

Since
p
equals
2
,
requiring that a homomorphism be
skew-symmetric (i.e., negated by duality)
is the same as
requiring that it be
symmetric (i.e., fixed by duality).

Proof.
Since
E
is faithfully flat over
k
,
the statement may be checked after base change to
E
.
Since

X\otimes_{k}E
is the direct sum of

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
and
\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
,
also

\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E
is the direct sum of

\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E)
and

\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
, so
the natural embeddings

\ker(q_{b_{E}})/\bigl(\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E+(\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E))\bigr)\longrightarrow{}\\
\bigl(X/(\ker(q_{b})+\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})\bigr)\otimes_{k}E

and

\bigl(\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E+(\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E))\bigr)/(\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E))\longrightarrow{}\\
\bigl((\ker(q_{b})+\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})/{\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}}\bigr)\otimes_{k}E

are isomorphisms.
Thus, by Proposition
8.9
(
b
),
the claim follows from
Lemma
8.1

with
\ker(q_{b_{E}})

playing the role of
X
;

\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E)
that of
X^{\prime\prime}
;
and

\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E+(\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E)

that of
X^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 8.11
.

Suppose that
p
equals
2
.
The following statements are equivalent.

(a)

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is smoothable.

(b)

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
is smoothable.

(c)

\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E
equals
\ker(q_{b_{E}})
.

Proof.
If either of
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
or
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
is smoothable,
so that

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}

or

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals
(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
,
then, since Proposition
8.9
gives that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
and

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals
(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
,
we have that both
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
and
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
are smoothable.
Thus (
a
) and (
b
)
are equivalent.

Since

\gamma_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}
,
Lemma
8.6
gives that

\ker(q_{b_{E}})\cap\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
and

\ker(q_{b_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}})\cap\ker(q_{b_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}})^{\perp}
are
both trivial, and
Corollary
8.7
gives that

\ker(q_{b_{E}})\otimes_{E}E^{\textup{{a}}}
equals

\ker(q_{b_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}})
, so
Proposition
8.9
(
b
)
gives that

(({\widetilde{G}}_{E}^{\gamma_{E}})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}
and

({\widetilde{G}}_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
are
both the image in
{\widetilde{G}}_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}
of

\Sp(\ker(q_{b_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}}),b_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\prime})
.
In particular, they are equal.

Since smoothing commutes with base change from
an algebraically closed field, we have that

(({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
By definition, (
a
) means that

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
,
which is equivalent to the equality of

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}=((({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}

and

(({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}=({\widetilde{G}}_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
Since
(({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}
is obviously
the base change to
E^{\textup{{a}}}
of

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E}
, and
we have observed that

({\widetilde{G}}_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{E^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is
the base change to
E^{\textup{{a}}}
of

({\widetilde{G}}_{E}^{\gamma_{E}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
, we have that
(
a
) is equivalent to the equality of

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{E}

and

({\widetilde{G}}_{E}^{\gamma_{E}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
By Proposition
8.9
(
a
), this
is equivalent to the equality of

(\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E)^{\perp}=\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}\otimes_{k}E

with

\ker(q_{b_{E}})^{\perp}
, which is equivalent to
statement (
c
).
∎

Examples
8.12
and

8.13

show different reasons why
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is not always smoothable.
In Example
8.12
, it is because

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is not reductive.

Example 8.12

(Alex Bauman and Sean Cotner)
.

Suppose that
p
equals
2
and
k
is imperfect.
Let
t
be an element of
k^{\times}\smallsetminus(k^{\times})^{2}
, and let

\gamma
be the automorphism

{{\tilde{g}}}\longmapsto{\Int\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t\\
&1\\
&&t\end{smallmatrix}\right){\tilde{g}}^{-\mathsf{T}}}

of
{\widetilde{G}}:=\GL_{3}
.
Since
\gamma
is an involution,
Lemma
10.1.1
below
guarantees that it acts quasisemisimply on

\SL_{3,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
, hence on
\GL_{3,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
.
Concretely, if we put
E=k(\sqrt{t})
, then

\gamma_{E}
preserves the opposite Borel subgroups of

\GL_{3,E}

corresponding to the flags

\displaystyle 0\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t}\\
1\\
\sqrt{t}\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq E^{3}

and

\displaystyle 0\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t}\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t}\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t}\\
1\\
\sqrt{t}\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq E^{3},

hence their common maximal torus.
The first Borel subgroup descends to
the Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
of
\GL_{3}
corresponding to the flag

0\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{k}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{k}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq k^{3},

but the second is not defined over
k
.

We have that

q_{b}(x)
equals
t^{-1}x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+t^{-1}x_{2}^{2}
for all

x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in k^{3}
, so that

\ker(q_{b})
equals

\operatorname{Span}\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
,
but

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
equals

\operatorname{Span}\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sqrt{t}\\
1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
.
Using Proposition
8.9
, we have that

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E}^{\gamma_{E}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is
the extension trivially across

\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sqrt{t}\\
1\\
\sqrt{t}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}

of the symplectic group on

\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sqrt{t}\\
1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
; but

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is
the subgroup of
\Isom(k^{3},b)
that fixes

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
which is the additive group

\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}c+1&0&c\\
0&1&0\\
c&0&c+1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
.
Since
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is not reductive,
we have
(by Theorem
A
(
2
))
that
\gamma
does not act quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
.
In particular, there is no Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that is
opposite to
{\widetilde{B}}
and
preserved by
\gamma
.

In Example
8.13
,
the fixed-point group
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is reductive, but
“too small”.
This explains the need for the largeness condition that
a certain centralizer be of multiplicative type in
Theorem
B
(
2
)(
2c
).

Example 8.13
.

A slight modification of Example
8.12

shows that
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
can fail to be smoothable even if

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is reductive.
Namely, continue to suppose that

p
equals
2
and

k
is imperfect, but now choose

t_{0},t_{2}\in k
such that

\left\{1,\sqrt{t_{0}},\sqrt{t_{2}}\right\}
is linearly independent over
k^{2}
, and
consider the automorphism

{{\tilde{g}}}\longmapsto{\Int\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t_{0}\\
&1\\
&&t_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right){\tilde{g}}^{-\mathsf{T}}}

of
\GL_{3}
.
Put
E=k(\sqrt{t_{0}},\sqrt{t_{2}})
.
This time,
\gamma_{E}

preserves the opposite Borel subgroups corresponding to the flags

\displaystyle 0\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t_{0}}\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t_{0}}\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t_{0}}\\
1\\
\sqrt{t_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq E^{3}

and

\displaystyle 0\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
\sqrt{t_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
\sqrt{t_{2}}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{t_{0}}\\
1\\
\sqrt{t_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subseteq E^{3},

hence their common maximal torus.

Now

q_{b}(x)
equals
t_{0}^{-1}x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{-1}x_{2}^{2}
for all

x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in k^{3}
, so

\ker(q_{b})
is trivial, but

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
equals

\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sqrt{t_{0}}\\
1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\\
\sqrt{t_{2}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}
.
We have that

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E}^{\gamma_{E}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is the extension trivially across

\operatorname{Span}_{E}\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\sqrt{t_{0}}\\
1\\
\sqrt{t_{2}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}

of the symplectic group on

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
; but

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is trivial.
In particular,
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is reductive.
Nonetheless,

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
does not equal

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
, so

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}=({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})^{\circ}
is not smoothable.
Thus, Theorem
A
(
0
) gives that

\gamma
does not act quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
.

Corollary 8.14

(to Lemma
8.8
and Proposition
8.9
)
.

The following statements are equivalent.

(a)

\gamma_{E}
is an exceptional automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{der}\,E}
.

(b)

p
equals
2
and
n
is even.

(c)

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
is not smooth.

Proof.
Since
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
is smooth if and only if

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{E}^{\gamma_{E}}=({\widetilde{G}}_{E})_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma_{E}}
is,
we may, and do, replace
k
by
E
.
The equivalence of
(
b
) with
(
c
) is
Lemma
8.8
and Proposition
8.9
.

Put
T=(({\widetilde{T}}\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
By Lemma
5.12
,
the quotient root system
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}},T)
is of type

\mathsf{C}_{(n+1)/2}
if
n
is odd, and of type

\mathsf{BC}_{n/2}
if
n
is even.
In particular, if
n
is odd, then

\Phi({\widetilde{G}},T)
is reduced, so that

\gamma
is not exceptional.
If
n
is even, then another application of
Lemma
8.8
and Proposition
8.9

gives that

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ},T)
is the set

\mathsf{C}_{n/2}
, respectively
\mathsf{B}_{n/2}
, of
non-multipliable, respectively non-divisible, roots in

\mathsf{BC}_{n/2}
, according as

p
equals or does not equal
2
.
Thus, if
p
does not equal
2
, then (
a
)
does not hold.
Conversely, suppose that
p
equals
2
.
Using Notation
8.4
, we have that

\gamma(e_{0}-e_{n/2})
equals
e_{n/2}-e_{n}
,
so that they have
the same restriction
a
to
T
, and

2a
belongs to
\mathsf{C}_{n/2}=\Phi(G,T)
; but
the image of

{\gamma+1}\colon{\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{e_{0}-e_{n/2}}}\longrightarrow{\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}}

is nonzero and pointwise fixed by
\gamma
, so

a
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma},T)
.
Thus, if
p
equals
2
, then
(
a
) holds.
∎

Proposition
8.15
is a special case of
Theorem
A
(
0
)
that is needed in the proof of the latter.
It isolates the obstruction to upgrading
“smoothable” to “smooth” in that result.

Proposition 8.15
.

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth
k
-group acting on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
,
and suppose that

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{der}\,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is quasisemisimple.

(a)

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smooth and reductive unless

p
equals
2
and

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{der}\,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is exceptional.

(b)

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is an extension of
a reductive group by
a split unipotent group.

(c)

If
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is reductive and

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
is of multiplicative type, then

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume, upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.

Note that, if

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smooth and reductive, then
the result is satisfied.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the subgroup of
\Gamma
that
acts on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
by inner automorphisms.
In particular, it acts trivially on

Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})=Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, so its action
may be extended trivially across
Z({\widetilde{G}})
to

Z({\widetilde{G}})\cdot{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}={\widetilde{G}}
.
Put
{\widetilde{M}}=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\circ}
.

We have that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
acts by
inner automorphisms of

{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
that preserve
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
, so that
the action factors through

{{\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Inn}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})}

to give a map

{\Gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.
Lemma
4.2.5
gives that

{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})^{\circ}
is
a Levi subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, hence reductive.
In particular,
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, hence
{\widetilde{M}}
, is smooth and reductive; so,
if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is all of
\Gamma
, then we are done.

Thus, we may, and do, assume that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is not all of
\Gamma
.
Since
\Gamma(k)
is Zariski dense in
\Gamma
,
there is some

\gamma\in\Gamma(k)
that acts on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
by
an outer automorphism, hence by inversion on

Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})=Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
.
We may thus extend
\gamma
to an automorphism of

Z({\widetilde{G}})\cdot{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}={\widetilde{G}}
that acts
by inversion on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
.

Since

(\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}/\Gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

embeds into

\underline{\Aut}({\widetilde{G}})/\underline{\Inn}({\widetilde{G}})=\underline{\Out}({\widetilde{G}})
, which
is trivial if
n
is at most
1
and
has order
2
otherwise, we have that
the image of
\gamma
generates
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
.
In particular, we may extend
the action of all of
\Gamma
to
{\widetilde{G}}
so that

\Gamma^{\prime}
acts trivially on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
and

\gamma
acts by inversion on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
.
Further,

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}={\widetilde{M}}^{\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}}

equals

{\widetilde{M}}^{\gamma}
.
Since
\gamma
acts by inversion on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
, we have that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is contained in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, hence
equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
; and similarly that

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
equals
(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

Since
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a Levi subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=\GL(X\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
,
it is the product of general linear groups
corresponding to the weight spaces in
X\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
for the
maximal central torus in
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
These factors are permuted by
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime})({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, and
at most one of them is preserved by
\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
(Concretely, if we form
the Dynkin diagram of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
with respect to
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
, then
there is a
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
-equivariant bijection between
factors of
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
and connected components of the associated
subdiagram of the Dynkin diagram.)

Since
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is reductive, so is
{\widetilde{M}}
.
If
{\widetilde{A}}
is the maximal central torus in
{\widetilde{M}}
, then

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Lemma C.4.4 gives that

{\widetilde{A}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is the maximal central torus in
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a Levi subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, we have that

{\widetilde{M}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals
C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}({\widetilde{A}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})=C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{A}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
,
so that
{\widetilde{M}}
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\widetilde{A}})
and hence is
a Levi subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Again, it is the product of general linear groups
corresponding to the weight spaces in
X
for
{\widetilde{A}}
.
Since the weight spaces in
X\otimes_{k}{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
for
{\widetilde{A}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
are just
the base changes to
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
of
the weight spaces in
X
for
{\widetilde{A}}
,
it follows that at most one of them is preserved by
\gamma
.

If no weight space is preserved by
\gamma
, then

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}={\widetilde{M}}^{\gamma}
is
a product of general linear groups,
one for each
\gamma
-orbit of weight spaces.
In particular,

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}={\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth and reductive.
Thus

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})^{\circ}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is
also smooth, and so equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, which
we have already observed is
contained in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{der}\,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
; so

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is contained in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
, hence
equals
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
, which is therefore also
smooth and reductive.
Again, in this case, we are done.

Thus we may, and do, assume that
some weight space

Y
is preserved by
\gamma
.
Note that
\gamma
is an involution of
\GL(Y)
that
acts by inversion on
Z(\GL(Y))
.
Since
\SL(X)^{\Gamma}
is
a direct product of a smooth group
(a product of general linear groups) with

\SL(Y)^{\gamma}
,
we may, and do, replace

X
by
Y
, and
\Gamma
by
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
.
Since now
\gamma
is an involution of
{\widetilde{G}}
that
acts by inversion on
Z({\widetilde{G}})
, we may apply
the results of this section.

If
p
or
n
is odd, then
Lemma
8.8
gives
that
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
is connected,
hence equals
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})^{\circ}
, and
is smooth and reductive,
so we are done.
Thus we may, and do, finally suppose that

p
equals
2
and
n
is even.
Then Corollary
8.14
gives that

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{der}\,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}},\left\langle\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}\right\rangle)
is exceptional, so that
(
a
) is vacuously true; and
(
b
) in this case follows from
Corollary
8.10
.

Finally, suppose that
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is reductive and

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
is of multiplicative type.
We now apply Corollary
8.10
several more times.
First, we observe that reductivity implies that

\Hom((\ker(q_{b})+\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})/{\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}},\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})

is trivial.
Second, if
\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}
is nontrivial, then
its
b
-orthogonal space

\ker(q_{b})+\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}
is a proper subspace of
X
; so

\Hom(X/(\ker(q_{b})+\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}),\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})

is nontrivial, and hence
has a nonzero vector negated by the duality involution
(since
p
equals
2
).
This contradicts reductivity by Corollary
8.10
,
so in fact

\ker(q_{b})\cap\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}
is trivial.
Third and finally,
another application of Corollary
8.10
gives that
the restriction map

{({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}}\longrightarrow{\Sp(\ker(q_{b}),b^{\prime})}

is an isomorphism.
Thus the factor
\SL(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})
of
the subgroup
\SL(\ker(q_{b}))\times\SL(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})

of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}

centralizes
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Since
C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
, and hence

\SL(\ker(q_{b})^{\perp})
, is of multiplicative type, we have that

\ker(q_{b})^{\perp}
is at most one dimensional, so that

\ker(q_{b})
is at least
n
dimensional.
Therefore the subspace
\ker(q_{b})\otimes_{k}E
of
\ker(q_{b_{E}})

is at least
n
dimensional; but

\ker(q_{b_{E}})
is
n
dimensional, by
Lemma
8.6
(
a
), so they are equal.
Then Corollary
8.11
gives that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}^{\gamma}
is smoothable.
This shows (
c
).
∎

9.
Proof of Theorem
B

As in Notation
3.1
, we
let
k
be a field,

{\widetilde{G}}
a connected, reductive
k
-group, and

\Gamma
a smooth
k
-group acting on
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
put
G=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
We do not require the particular choice

{\widetilde{G}}=\GL_{n+1}
of §
8
.

Theorem 0
.

Suppose that
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is quasisemisimple.

(1)

G
is an extension of
a reductive group by
a split unipotent group.

(2)

The following statements are equivalent.

(a)

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is quasisemisimple.

(b)

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable.

(c)

G
is reductive, and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(G)
is of multiplicative type.

(d)

There is a torus
T
in
G
such that

T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a maximal torus in
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

(e)

There are a

\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
-stable maximal torus
{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, and a

\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
-stable Borel subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
containing

{\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.

Proof of
Theorem
B
(
1
) and
Theorem
B
(
2
)(
2c

\implies

2b
).

Suppose that we have proven the result for
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.
Write
R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
for
the unipotent radical of
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
which is split; and

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\textup{red}}
for the quotient of

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
by its unipotent radical,
which is reductive.
Then we have by Corollary
4.1.2
that
the natural map

{({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}
is a quotient,
obviously with kernel
Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
The pre-image
S
of
R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
is
an extension of
R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
by

Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Since
R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is split, we have by

[
SGA-3.3
]
*Exposé XVII, Théorème 6.1.1(A)(ii)
that

S
is a trivial extension, i.e., is isomorphic to

(Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})\times R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
.
In particular, we may view
R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
as
a subgroup of
G
.
Then
G/R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
is an extension

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\textup{red}}
by
Z({\widetilde{G}})\cap({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, hence
is reductive.
Finally, Corollary
4.1.4
shows that
(
2
)(
2b
)
is unchanged if we replace
{\widetilde{G}}
by
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.

Thus we may, and do, assume, upon
replacing
{\widetilde{G}}
by
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
, that

{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint.
Since a unipotent group is split if and only if
it becomes so after separable base change, and since
formation of the unipotent radical commutes with
separable base change, we may, and do, assume,
upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.
Then, by Remark
4.3.7
, we may, and do, assume,
upon replacing
{\widetilde{G}}
by
an almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
and

\Gamma
by
\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})
, that

{\widetilde{G}}
is almost simple
(hence simple, because it is adjoint).

Unless
p
equals
2
and

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is exceptional,
Theorem
A
(
1
,
2
)
gives that

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})^{\circ}
is
smooth and reductive, so that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is also smooth and reductive.
In particular,

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
equals
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=G
, which
is therefore itself reductive; and
(
2
)(
2b
) holds
(hence is certainly implied by
(
2
)(
2c
)).

Thus we may, and do, assume for the remainder of the proof that

p
equals
2
and

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is exceptional.
Remark
6.12
(
a
)
gives that
{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
for
some positive integer
n
.
We shall use twice the consequence of
Corollary
4.1.2
that

G=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is (isomorphic to)
the quotient of
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
by

Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})\cap({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

We begin by proving
(
1
).
Proposition
8.15
(
b
)
gives that
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is
an extension of a reductive group

(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\textup{red}}

by a split unipotent group
R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
.
Since the multiplicative-type group

Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})\cap({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
necessarily intersects
the (split) unipotent group
R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}

trivially, we have that

G
is an extension by

R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
of
a group that is a quotient of
(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\textup{red}}
, and so
reductive.
This shows (
1
).

Next we show that
(
2
)(
2c
) implies
(
2
)(
2b
),
beginning by assuming that
(
2
)(
2c
) holds,
i.e., that
G
is reductive and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(G)
is of multiplicative type.
Then, first,

R_{\textup{u}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
is trivial, so

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
equals
(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\textup{red}}
, and hence is
reductive.
Second, the restriction to

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
of
the natural quotient map
{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}}
has image in the multiplicative-type group
C_{\widetilde{G}}(G)
.
Thus

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
is
a central extension of a multiplicative-type group by
the multiplicative-type group
Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})
, hence is
itself multiplicative

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 12.22.
Then
Proposition
8.15
(
c
)
gives that
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
, hence,
by Corollary
4.1.4
, also

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
, is smoothable.
That is, (
2
)(
2b
)
holds.
∎

Proof of
Theorem
B
(
2
)(
2a

\iff

2b

\iff

2d

\iff

2e

\implies

2c
).
We may, and do, assume, upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.

First assume (
2a
).
Then (
2e
) is obvious;
Theorem
A
(
0
) gives
(
2b
); and
Theorem
A
(
2
) and
Proposition
6.5
(
b
) give
(
2c
).

Remark
2.2.2
shows that
(
2b
) implies that

G_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Then

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Lemma C.4.4 gives
(
2d
).

Assuming (
2d
), we have by
Proposition
6.5
(
b
) that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(T)_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}(T_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is
a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, so

C_{\widetilde{G}}(T)
is a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Then
Lemma
6.3
gives that

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})
is quasisemisimple, which is
(
2a
).

Finally, assume (
2e
).
Proposition
6.5
(
a
) gives that

({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is
a maximal torus in
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Since all subgroups of tori are smoothable, we have by
Remark
2.2.2
that

(({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals
({\widetilde{T}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, hence is
a maximal torus in
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
,
giving (
2d
).
∎

Corollary 9.1
.

Suppose that
k
is separably closed.
If
\gamma
is a semisimple automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
in the sense of
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*§7, p. 51,
then
\gamma
is a quasisemisimple automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}
.

Proof.
We have by
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 7.5 that

\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a quasisemisimple automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is smooth, by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2),
the result follows from
Theorem
B
(
2
).
∎

Theorem
A
is nearly subsumed by
Theorem
B
, except that
the latter has nothing to say about
spherical buildings.
Conjecture
9.2
describes
an analogue of
Theorem
A
(
3
) in
the setting of
Theorem
B
.

It is not hard to prove
the existence of the set
\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}}
and
the map
i
as in Conjecture
9.2
,
but we do not do it here.
Once existence is proven,
uniqueness is obvious, and it is clear that

i(\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}})
lies in

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
.
Determining whether
i
is surjective will be
the subject of future work.

Conjecture 9.2
.

Suppose that
({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}},\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is quasisemisimple.
Write
G^{\textup{red}}
for the maximal
pseudo-reductive quotient of
G
, which is
reductive by
Theorem
B
(
1
).
There are a unique
subset
\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}}
of
\mathscr{S}(G^{\textup{red}})
and
map
{i}\colon{\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})}
with
the following properties.
For every (split) torus
S
in
G^{\textup{red}}
,
the subset

\mathscr{S}(S)\cap\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}}
of

\mathscr{S}(G^{\textup{red}})
contains precisely the rays through
elements
\lambda\in{\mathbf{X}}_{*}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus\left\{0\right\}
such that

P_{G}(\lambda)
contains the unipotent radical of
G
; and
the diagram

\textstyle{\mathscr{S}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}(S)\cap\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}

commutes.
Then
i
is a bijection from

\mathscr{S}(G)^{\textup{red}}
onto
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{s}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{s}}}})}
.

Corollary
9.3

is quite close to

[
lemaire:twisted-characters
]
*Théorème 4.6.
A special case of this latter result is proven by a different method in

[
adler-lansky:lifting1
]
*Lemma A.1.
Note that it provides a practical way to verify
Theorem
B
(
2
)(
2a
), hence
the equivalent conditions of
Theorem
B
(
2
).

Corollary 9.3
.

If
G
contains a split torus
S
such that

S_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a maximal torus in

({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\Gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, then

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is quasisemisimple.

Proof.
Proposition
6.5
(
b
)
gives that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}(S_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is
a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, so

C_{\widetilde{G}}(S)
is a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
.
The result follows from Lemma
6.3
.
∎

10.
Proof of Theorem
C

In this section,

k
is any field, and

{\widetilde{G}}
is a connected, reductive
k
-group.
We let
p
be
1
or a prime number, and assume that

k
has characteristic exponent
p
or
1
.

We allow the possibility of characteristic exponent
1
to
handle valued fields of mixed characteristic
p
in

[
adler-lansky-spice:actions3
]
; but, for
our applications in this paper,
we are most interested in
the case where
p
is a prime, and

k
has characteristic exponent
p
.

Beginning with §
10.3
, we will
impose the full hypotheses of
Theorem
C
, and
assume that

k
has characteristic exponent
p
; but
we do not do so yet.

10.1.
Unipotent, or topologically unipotent, automorphisms

In this subsection, we are mostly interested in
order-
p
automorphisms that are
assumed to be quasisemisimple.
We begin, however, with a family of automorphisms for which
quasisemisimplicity is automatic.

Specifically,
Lemma
10.1.1
shows that
involutions on groups of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
in
characteristic
2
,
which, for many purposes, are the
hardest
case to handle
(see, for example, Proposition
6.22
),
are actually
easier
to handle in one respect:
that they are all quasisemisimple.
This should be surprising; see
Remark
10.1.2
on its rarity.

Lemma 10.1.1
.

Suppose that
k
is algebraically closed.
If

{\widetilde{G}}
is an almost-simple group of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n}

for some positive integer
n
,
and

\gamma
is an outer involution of
{\widetilde{G}}
, then

\gamma
is quasisemisimple.

Proof.
By
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 7.5,
the result holds if

p
is not
2
or

k
has characteristic exponent
1
.
Thus we may, and do, assume that

p
equals
2
and

k
has characteristic exponent
p=2
.
By
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 7.2, there is
a Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
that is
preserved by
\gamma
.
Let
{\widetilde{T}}
be a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{B}}
.
By, for example,
[
springer:corvallis
]
*Proposition 2.13,
there is a quasisemisimple involution
\gamma_{0}
of

{\widetilde{G}}
that preserves
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
and
has the same image in the outer-automorphism group as
\gamma
.
We claim that
\gamma
is conjugate to
\gamma_{0}
in

\Aut({\widetilde{G}})
(indeed, in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
).
Since this claim is unaffected if we replace
\gamma
by
a
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
-conjugate, we do so freely.

Write
{\widetilde{B}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
and
{\widetilde{T}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
for
the images of
{\widetilde{B}}
and
{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.
Since
\gamma\gamma_{0}^{-1}
is inner and preserves
{\widetilde{B}}
,
it belongs to
{\widetilde{B}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
.
Write
{\widetilde{b}}_{0}=\gamma\gamma_{0}^{-1}
.
Since
{\widetilde{b}}_{0}\gamma_{0}=\gamma
is an involution,
we have that

{\widetilde{b}}_{0}\cdot\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{b}}_{0})
is trivial.
Let
{\widetilde{U}}
be the unipotent radical of
{\widetilde{B}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
and
write
{\widetilde{b}}_{0}={\widetilde{t}}_{0}{\tilde{u}}_{0}
, with

{\widetilde{t}}_{0}\in{\widetilde{T}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
and
{\tilde{u}}_{0}\in{\widetilde{U}}(k)
.
By
[
digne-michel:quass
]
*Lemma 1.2(iii),
we may write
{\widetilde{t}}_{0}
as
{\widetilde{t}}_{+}{\widetilde{t}}_{1}\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{t}}_{1})^{-1}
, where

{\widetilde{t}}_{1}
belongs to
{\widetilde{T}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
and

{\widetilde{t}}_{+}
is a
k
-rational point of
the maximal subtorus of
{\widetilde{T}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
on which

\gamma_{0}
acts trivially.
Then

{\widetilde{t}}_{+}^{2}={\widetilde{t}}_{0}\cdot\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{t}}_{0})

equals

{\widetilde{b}}_{0}\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{b}}_{0})\cdot\Int(\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{b}}_{0}))^{-1}{\tilde{u}}_{0}^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{0}({\tilde{u}}_{0})^{-1}={\tilde{u}}_{1}\gamma_{0}({\tilde{u}}_{0})^{-1}
.
Since
\Int(\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{b}}_{1}))^{-1}{\tilde{u}}_{0}^{-1}

belongs to
{\widetilde{U}}(k)
,
{\widetilde{t}}_{+}^{2}
belongs to

{\widetilde{T}}(k)\cap{\widetilde{U}}(k)
, hence is trivial.
Since
p
equals
2
, this implies that

{\widetilde{t}}_{+}
is trivial.
Then

{\widetilde{t}}_{1}^{-1}\cdot\gamma\cdot{\widetilde{t}}_{1}={\widetilde{t}}_{1}^{-1}\cdot{\widetilde{b}}_{0}\cdot\gamma_{0}\cdot{\widetilde{t}}_{1}
equals

{\tilde{u}}_{1}\cdot\gamma_{0}
, where

{\tilde{u}}_{1}\mathrel{:=}\Int(\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{t}}_{1}))^{-1}{\tilde{u}}_{0}\in{\widetilde{U}}(k)
.
We may, and do, replace
\gamma
by

{\widetilde{t}}_{1}^{-1}\cdot\gamma\cdot{\widetilde{t}}_{1}
.

For each positive integer
h
,
write
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}
for the subgroup of
{\widetilde{U}}

generated by root subgroups corresponding to
roots in
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})
of height at least
h
.
Thus, each
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}
is preserved by
\gamma_{0}
.

We now proceed by induction on
h
.
Fix a positive integer
h
, and
suppose that we have arranged, after
replacing
\gamma
by
a
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
-conjugate if needed, that
there is an element
{\tilde{u}}_{h}
of
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}(k)

such that

\gamma
equals
{\tilde{u}}_{h}\cdot\gamma_{0}
.
(The above element
u_{1}
satisfies this condition when
h=1
.)
We prove the existence of an analogous element
{\tilde{u}}_{h+1}\in{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1}(k)
.
Note that, since

\gamma^{2}
is trivial, so is

{\tilde{u}}_{h}\cdot\gamma_{0}({\tilde{u}}_{h})
;
i.e.,
{\tilde{u}}_{h}
is inverted by
\gamma_{0}
.

We now make a number of computations backed up by
the Chevalley commutation relations

[
adler:thesis
]
*Proposition 1.2.3.
We have that
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1}
is normal in
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}
.
The unique
{\widetilde{T}}
-equivariant linear structures on
the various root groups for
{\widetilde{T}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Lemma 2.3.8
piece together to a

{\widetilde{T}}
-equivariant linear structure on

{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}/{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1}
.
Let us denote this structure by
\exp_{h}
.
Uniqueness of the structures on the individual root groups
implies that
\exp_{h}
is
\gamma_{0}
-equivariant.

There are linearly disjoint,
sub-
{\widetilde{T}}
-representations
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{\pm}_{h}
of

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h})
such that

\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{+}_{h})
equals

{\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{-}_{h}
and

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h})/({\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{+}_{h}+{\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{-}_{h}+\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1}))

is trivial or one dimensional, according as

h
is even or odd.
The subspaces
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{\pm}_{h}
are not uniquely determined, but
we only need their existence.
If
h
is even, then there is
a unique root
{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
of height
h
that is
pre-divisible, in the sense of
Remark
5.14
, and it is
the weight of
{\widetilde{T}}
on

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h})/({\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{+}_{h}+{\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{-}_{h}+\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1}))
.
(Specifically, in the Bourbaki numbering

[
bourbaki:lie-gp+lie-alg_4-6
]
*Chapter VI, Plate I,
except that we write
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
in place of just
\alpha
,
we have that

{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
equals

{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{n-h/2+1}+\dotsb+{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{n+h/2}
.)
For convenience, we put
{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}=0
if
h
is odd.

Choose
{\widetilde{X}}^{\pm}_{h}\in{\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}}^{\pm}_{h}
such that

\exp_{h}^{-1}({\tilde{u}}_{h})-({\widetilde{X}}^{+}_{h}+{\widetilde{X}}^{-}_{h})

belongs to the
{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
-weight space for
{\widetilde{T}}
in

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}/{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1})

(hence is trivial if
h
is odd).
Since
{\tilde{u}}_{h}
is inverted by
\gamma_{0}
,

{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
is fixed by
\gamma_{0}
, and

\exp_{h}
is
\gamma_{0}
-equivariant, we have that

{\widetilde{X}}^{-}_{h}
equals
-\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{X}}^{+}_{h})
.
(Of course the minus sign has no effect, but
we include it to be suggestive.)
Write
{\tilde{v}}^{+}_{h}
for any element of
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}(k)

such that

{\widetilde{X}}^{+}_{h}
belongs to the coset
\exp_{h}^{-1}({\tilde{v}}^{+}_{h})
.
Then

\exp_{h}^{-1}\bigl(({\tilde{v}}^{+}_{h})^{-1}\cdot{\tilde{u}}_{h}\cdot\gamma_{0}({\tilde{v}}^{+}_{h}))\bigr)

belongs to the
{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
-weight space in

\Lie({\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}/{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1})
.
Thus we may, and do, assume, upon
replacing
\gamma
by
({\tilde{v}}^{+}_{h})^{-1}\gamma{\tilde{v}}^{+}_{h}
, that

\exp_{h}^{-1}({\tilde{u}}_{h})
belongs to
the
{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
-weight space.

In particular, if
h
is odd, then

\exp_{h}^{-1}({\tilde{u}}_{h})
is trivial, so
we may put
{\tilde{u}}_{h+1}={\tilde{u}}_{h}
.
Thus we may, and do, assume that
h
is even.
Let
{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h}
be the vector in

\exp_{h}^{-1}({\tilde{u}}_{h})
that belongs to
the
{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
-weight space.
Note that, since
{\tilde{u}}_{h}
is inverted by
\gamma_{0}
,
it follows that

{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h}
is negated, and hence fixed, by
\gamma_{0}
.
(Actually this does not need any special condition on
{\tilde{u}}_{h}
,
since it is not hard to show that
\gamma_{0}
acts trivially on

\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}}
.)
By Remark
5.14
(
a
),
the restriction of
{\widetilde{\beta}}_{h}
to
({\widetilde{T}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is
divisible, hence may be written as

2a
for some root
a\in\Phi({\widetilde{G}},({\widetilde{T}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
.
By Proposition
6.21
(
3
)
(applied to
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\ker(a))^{\circ}
),
there is a unique element

{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h/2}
in
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{a}^{\gamma_{0}}
such that

({\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h/2})^{[2]}
equals
{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h}
.
Concretely, by
Proposition
6.21
(
2
),
if we let
\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{h/2},{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{h/2}^{\prime}\}

be an exceptional pair for

(\Psi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}}),\left\langle\gamma_{0}\right\rangle(k))
extending
a
,
then
{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h/2}
equals
{\widetilde{X}}_{h/2}+{\widetilde{X}}_{h/2}^{\prime}

for some

{\widetilde{X}}_{h/2}\in\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{h/2}}
and

{\widetilde{X}}_{h/2}^{\prime}\in\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{h/2}^{\prime}}
, and

{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h}
equals
[{\widetilde{X}}_{h/2},{\widetilde{X}}_{h/2}^{\prime}]
.
Since
{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h}
is fixed by
\gamma_{0}
, so is

{\widetilde{X}}^{0}_{h/2}
, so

{\widetilde{X}}_{h/2}^{\prime}
equals
\gamma_{0}({\widetilde{X}}_{h/2})
.
Now write
{\tilde{v}}_{h/2}
for the element of the coset

\exp_{h/2}({\widetilde{X}}_{h/2})
that lies in the

{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{h/2}
-root group, and
put
{\tilde{v}}^{0}_{h/2}={\tilde{v}}_{h/2}\gamma_{0}({\tilde{v}}_{h/2})
.
Then
{\tilde{u}}_{h}
and

[{\tilde{v}}_{h/2},\gamma_{0}({\tilde{v}}_{h/2})]
have
the same image in
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}/{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1}
,
so

({\tilde{v}}^{0}_{h/2})^{-1}\cdot\gamma\cdot{\tilde{v}}^{0}_{h/2}=\gamma_{0}({\tilde{v}}_{h/2})^{-1}{\tilde{v}}_{h/2}^{-1}\cdot{\tilde{u}}_{h}\cdot\gamma_{0}\cdot{\tilde{v}}_{h/2}\gamma_{0}({\tilde{v}}_{h/2})

equals

{\tilde{u}}_{h+1}\gamma_{0}
, where

{\tilde{u}}_{h+1}\mathrel{:=}\gamma_{0}({\tilde{v}}_{h/2})^{-1}{\tilde{v}}_{h/2}^{-1}\cdot{\tilde{u}}_{h}\cdot\gamma_{0}({\tilde{v}}_{h/2}){\tilde{v}}_{h/2}

belongs to
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h+1}(k)
.

Since
{\widetilde{U}}_{\geq h}
is trivial for all
sufficiently large positive integers
n
, eventually
our process of successive replacements will have replaced

\gamma
by
\gamma_{0}
, which is quasisemisimple
by assumption.
∎

Remark 10.1.2
.

The “automatic quasisemisimplicity” property of
Lemma
10.1.1
is
specific to
\mathsf{A}_{2n}
,
in the following sense.
For every other connected Dynkin diagram that admits
an automorphism of order
p
,
there is at least one node fixed by all automorphisms
of the Dynkin diagram;
and, if
{\widetilde{G}}
is a quasisplit
reductive group of that type over
k
,
then there is a quasisemisimple automorphism

\gamma_{0}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
that
acts trivially on the corresponding root subgroup.
If
u
is an element of that root subgroup,
then
\gamma_{0}\Int(u)
is not quasisemisimple.
(This can be shown by combining
Proposition
7.5
(
b
)
with a computation as in the proof of
Lemma
10.1.1
.)
See
[
chernousov-elduque-knus-tignol:d4
]
*§11 for
the case of
\mathsf{D}_{4}
.

For the remainder of
§
10.1
,
let
\gamma
be a
quasisemisimple, outer automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}
such that

\gamma^{p}
is trivial.
(Remember that we call the trivial automorphism outer, so
our results here include the possibility that

\gamma
itself is trivial, though
of course they have little content in that case.)
Remember that

k
has characteristic exponent
1
or
p
.
If
k
has characteristic exponent
p
, then
‘outer’ is redundant; in this case,
every quasisemisimple automorphism
\gamma
of
{\widetilde{G}}

satisfying
\gamma^{p}=1
is already outer.

Recall that notation like

\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
stands for
the
algebraic
group, not the
abstract
group,
generated by
\gamma
.
Since
\gamma
has finite order, there is little distinction;
we have that
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
is the constant group such that

\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k)
is the abstract group generated by
\gamma
.
However, for an element not known to be of finite order,
such as the element
s
of Lemma
10.1.3
,
it is possible that
\left\langle s\right\rangle(k)
is strictly bigger than
the abstract group generated by
s
.

Lemma
10.1.3
does not assume that

k
has characteristic exponent
p
.
If
k

does
have characteristic exponent
p
, then
the semisimplicity of
s
already implies that
the hypothesis of Lemma
10.1.3
is satisfied.
If
k
is a valued field of mixed characteristic
p
and

s
has finite order
(which already implies that it is semisimple), then

\pi_{0}(\left\langle s\right\rangle)(k)
is the abstract group generated by
s
, so
the hypothesis of Lemma
10.1.3
is
equivalent to
s
being topologically semisimple, in
the sense that its order is relatively prime to
the residue characteristic
p
.

Lemma 10.1.3
.

Suppose that
s
is a
semisimple,
\gamma
-fixed element of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
such that
the order of
\pi_{0}(\left\langle s\right\rangle)(k)
is relatively prime to
p
.
Put
Z=((Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
Then there is a maximal torus
T^{\prime}
in

(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
such that
the image of
s
belongs to
T^{\prime}(k)
.

Proof.
Suppose first that
k
is algebraically closed.

Since passing to the maximal subgroup scheme does not
affect the group of
k
-points, we have that

Z(k)=((Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}(k)

equals

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})^{\gamma}(k)=Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ}(k)^{\gamma}=(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}(k)
.
Recall that we do not always have that
the maximal smooth subgroup of
a connected subgroup is
connected;
but, over a perfect field like
k
, we do have that

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is
the maximal reduced subscheme of
the connected subscheme

Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ}
, hence is
itself connected.
(Actually, we do not even need that
k
is perfect here,
since
Z({\widetilde{G}})
is of multiplicative type.)
Thus

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is both smooth and connected, hence
contained in

(Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}})^{\circ}
, which we have agreed to denote by

Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
The reverse containment is automatic
(for any group scheme over any field), so we have equality.
Thus,

Z(k)=(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})_{\textup{{sm}}}(k)^{\gamma}
equals

Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}(k)^{\gamma}
.

Because
k
is algebraically closed, the sequence

\textstyle{Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}(k)\times{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{G}}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

is exact.
Thus, arguing as in the proof of

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Lemma 9.2, we may apply

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*§4.5 to get an exact sequence

\textstyle{Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}(k)^{\gamma}\times{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}(k)^{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{G}}(k)^{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k),{\widetilde{Z}}(k)),}

where we have put

{\widetilde{Z}}=\ker({Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}\times{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}})
.
It is a general fact

[
serre:galois
]
*Ch. I, Proposition 2.4.9
that
H^{1}(\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k),{\widetilde{Z}}(k))
is
annihilated by the order of
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k)
, hence,
in particular, by
p
.
(In this case, we can see this fact concretely by observing that

H^{1}(\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k),{\widetilde{Z}}(k))
may be realized as
a subgroup of the co-invariant quotient
{\widetilde{Z}}(k)_{\gamma}
by
evaluating at
\gamma
;
that
1+\dotsb+\gamma^{p-1}
equals
the
p
-power map on
{\widetilde{Z}}(k)_{\gamma}
; and that
it annihilates the subgroup
H^{1}(\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle(k),{\widetilde{Z}}(k))

by the cocycle condition.)
Thus
s^{p}
lifts to

Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}(k)^{\gamma}\times{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}(k)^{\gamma}=(Z({\widetilde{G}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\gamma}(k)\times{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\gamma}(k)=Z(k)\times({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}(k).

Since
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is connected

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 8.2,
so is its image in
({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
, so
we have that
s^{p}
lifts to

Z(k)\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}(k)
.

Now
\left\langle s\right\rangle/\left\langle s^{p}\right\rangle
is étale,
so
\left\langle s\right\rangle
and
\left\langle s^{p}\right\rangle
have
the same identity component.
Therefore, we may regard
\pi_{0}(\left\langle s^{p}\right\rangle)
as
a subgroup of
\pi_{0}(\left\langle s\right\rangle)
.
We have that the index of
\pi_{0}(\left\langle s^{p}\right\rangle)(k)
in

\pi_{0}(\left\langle s\right\rangle)(k)
divides
p
, hence
equals
1
.
It follows that

\left\langle s^{p}\right\rangle(k)
equals
\left\langle s\right\rangle(k)
, and,
in particular, contains
s
; so

s
belongs to
Z\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
; so
the image of
s
in
{\widetilde{G}}/Z
belongs to the image there of

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, hence to

(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
By
[
borel:linear
]
*Corollary 18.12,
we have that
s
belongs to a maximal torus in

(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

Now drop the assumption that
k
is algebraically closed.
Since groups of multiplicative type, such as

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})^{\gamma}
, are
smoothable, we have that

Z_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=(((Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\circ})^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

((Z({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\circ})^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
.
Since Lemma
6.4
gives that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}/Z
,
it follows from Theorem
A
(
0
) that

(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})^{\circ}
is smoothable, so
Remark
2.2.2
gives that

((({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
equals

(({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}/Z_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\gamma_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Thus the special case that we have already proven shows that
the image of
s_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
in
({\widetilde{G}}/Z)({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
belongs to
a maximal torus in
((({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
We note two consequences.
First, we see that
s_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is a
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
-rational point of
some maximal torus in

C_{((({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}(s_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, hence,
by their conjugacy

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Theorem C.2.3, of
all such maximal tori.
Second, we see that

C_{(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}(s)_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}=C_{((({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}(s_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})
has the same rank as

((({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
,
hence that

C_{(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}(s)
has the same absolute rank as

(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
That is,

C_{(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}(s)
contains a maximal torus
T^{\prime}
in

(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Since
T^{\prime}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
is still maximal in

C_{(({\widetilde{G}}/Z)^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}}(s)_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, we have that

s_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
belongs to
T^{\prime}({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, so

s
belongs to
T^{\prime}(k)
.
∎

Lemma 10.1.4
.

Suppose that

k
has characteristic exponent
p
and

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Let

\Gamma^{\prime}
be a
\gamma
-stable subgroup of
\underline{\Aut}({\widetilde{G}})
such that

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma^{\prime})
is quasisemisimple.
Fix
s^{\prime}\in Z(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\gamma}(k)
and put

{\widetilde{H}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(s^{\prime})^{\circ}
, or
let
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
be
a subspace of
\Lie(Z(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\gamma}
and put

{\widetilde{H}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
Then

{\widetilde{H}}
is reductive,

\gamma
gives a quasisemisimple automorphism of
{\widetilde{H}}
, and

{\widetilde{H}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.

Proof.
Reductivity follows from

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.12
or
Corollary
4.2.2
.

Put

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\Lie(Z(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})^{\gamma}
.
Let us say that we are
in case (I) if we have put

{\widetilde{H}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(s^{\prime})^{\circ}
, and
in case (II) if we have put

{\widetilde{H}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
We now argue in parallel.

Let
T_{H}
be a maximal torus in
H
.
Proposition
6.5
(
b
)
gives that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(T_{H})
is a torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
, so

C_{\widetilde{H}}(T_{H})
is a torus in
{\widetilde{H}}
.
Thus, Lemma
6.3
gives that

({\widetilde{H}},\Gamma^{\prime})
is quasisemisimple.

Lemma
6.4
gives that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
, and
Remark
6.8
and Lemma
7.9

together give that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.
We have by
Proposition
7.1
(
a
) that

G\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})^{\circ}
and

G^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\gamma})^{\circ}
are reductive, and
by Corollary
4.1.2
that

{G}\longrightarrow{G^{\prime}}
is a central quotient.
In case (I), we have by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2) that

C_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(s^{\prime})=C_{\widetilde{G}}(s^{\prime})^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Since
C_{\widetilde{G}}(s^{\prime})/{\widetilde{H}}
is étale, so is

C_{\widetilde{G}}(s^{\prime})^{\gamma}/{\widetilde{H}}^{\gamma}
, so

{\widetilde{H}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Further, since
the characteristic exponent of
k
is
p
, we have by
Lemma
10.1.3
that
there is a maximal torus
T_{G^{\prime}}
in
G^{\prime}
such that

s^{\prime}
belongs to
T_{G^{\prime}}(k)
.
The corresponding torus
T_{G}
in
G
is contained in

C_{\widetilde{G}}(s^{\prime})^{\circ}={\widetilde{H}}
.
In case (II), since

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is a commuting algebra of semisimple elements that
is contained in

\Lie({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})^{\gamma}=\Lie(G^{\prime})
, we have by
Lemma
4.2.1
that
there is a maximal torus
T_{G^{\prime}}
in
G^{\prime}
such that

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is contained in
\Lie(T_{G^{\prime}})
, and by
Corollary
4.2.2
that

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
is smooth.
The torus
T_{G}
in
G
corresponding to
T_{G^{\prime}}
is contained in

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}={\widetilde{H}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}/G
is étale, so is

C_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})/C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
, hence also

C_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})/C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
Since
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})/{\widetilde{H}}
is étale, so is

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\gamma}/{\widetilde{H}}^{\gamma}
.
Since
C_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
equals

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\gamma}
, and

C_{G}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
is smooth, so is

{\widetilde{H}}^{\gamma}
.

We have now shown, in both cases, that

{\widetilde{H}}^{\gamma}
is smooth, and that there is a
maximal torus
T_{G}
in
G
that is contained in
{\widetilde{H}}
.
We have by
Proposition
6.5
(
b
)
that
C_{\widetilde{G}}(T_{G})
, hence also
C_{\widetilde{H}}(T_{G})
, is
a torus; and then by Lemma
6.3
that

\gamma
is a quasisemisimple automorphism of
{\widetilde{H}}
.
∎

The following cohomological remark will
be useful in the proofs of
Propositions
10.1.6
and
10.2.1
.

Remark 10.1.5
.

Suppose that

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is a
quasisemisimple reductive datum, and

({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
is a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
such that

{\widetilde{T}}
, but not necessarily
{\widetilde{B}}
, is preserved by
\Gamma
.
There is a unique function
{\Gamma(k)}\longrightarrow{W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})(k)}
,
which is easily verified to be a coboundary, sending

\gamma\in\Gamma(k)
to
the unique element
w(\gamma)\in W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})(k)
such that

\gamma{\widetilde{B}}
equals
\Int(w(\gamma))^{-1}{\widetilde{B}}
.
Let
({\widetilde{B}}_{0},{\widetilde{T}}_{0})
be
a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that is preserved by
\Gamma
.
Then there is a unique element
w_{0}\in W({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})(k)
such that

{\widetilde{B}}
equals
\Int(w_{0})^{-1}{\widetilde{B}}_{0})
, and it follows that

w(\gamma)
equals
w_{0}^{-1}\gamma(w_{0})
for all

\gamma\in\Gamma(k)
.
That is, the cohomology class of
{\gamma}\longmapsto{w(\gamma)}
is
trivial.

Proposition
10.1.7
is
a statement about the action of
\gamma
on
a certain fixed-point group.
The hardest case there is when

{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{E}_{6}
, and
certain other conditions are satisfied.
We isolate this case as Proposition
10.1.6
.

Proposition 10.1.6
.

Suppose that

{\widetilde{G}}
is split and adjoint of type
\mathsf{E}_{6}

and

\gamma
preserves a
semisimple subgroup
{\widetilde{H}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
of type

3\mathsf{A}_{2}
.
Then
Z({\widetilde{H}})
is étale of order
3
, and

\gamma
acts trivially on
Z({\widetilde{H}})
.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume, upon
replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, that

k
is algebraically closed.

Let
{\widetilde{T}}_{\bullet}
be a split maximal torus in
{\widetilde{H}}
.
Since the ranks of
{\widetilde{H}}
and
{\widetilde{G}}
are equal,
also
{\widetilde{T}}_{\bullet}
is a split maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
.
By Remark
4.2.4
,
for some Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}_{\bullet}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
containing
{\widetilde{T}}_{\bullet}
,
and using the Bourbaki numbering

\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,5}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,6}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,2}}

of
\Delta({\widetilde{B}}_{\bullet},{\widetilde{T}}_{\bullet})
,
we have that the
\Delta({\widetilde{B}}_{\bullet},{\widetilde{T}}_{\bullet})
-highest root

{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,0}
equals

{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,1}+2{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,2}+2{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,3}+3{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,4}+2{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,5}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,6}
,
and
the extended Dynkin diagram looks like

(
*
)

\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{\bullet\,3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The Borel–de Siebenthal subgroups

The conclusion of
Proposition
10.1.7
is
surprising, at least to us.
It boils down to a case-by-case check,
which is feasible because of the short list of possibilities in
Remark
2.1.5
.

Proposition 10.1.7
.

Suppose that

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a
nontrivial,

\gamma
-stable
subgroup of
a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.
Then

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})^{\gamma}
is nontrivial
or

\Lie(Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}))
is nontrivial.

Proof.
We may, and do, assume, upon
replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.

Suppose that

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})^{\gamma}
and

\Lie(Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}))
are trivial.
Thus,

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
is étale.

Let
{\widetilde{T}}
be a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
that
contains
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Note that
C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
is contained in

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}({\widetilde{T}})={\widetilde{T}}/Z({\widetilde{G}})
, hence in

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
, hence in

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
.
Since the reverse containment is obvious,
we have that

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
equals

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
.
We will use this later.
For now,
we have that
\Phi(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ},{\widetilde{T}})
is
an integrally closed subsystem of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})
, and

{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})/{\mathbb{Z}}\Phi(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ},{\widetilde{T}})
is
the character group of the étale, multiplicative-type group

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
, hence is
finite, but has no nontrivial torsion of
order the characteristic exponent of
k
.
By Remark
4.2.4
(
b
),
there are a Borel subgroup
{\widetilde{B}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
containing
{\widetilde{T}}
,
an almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
, and
an element
{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\Delta({\widetilde{B}}\cap{\widetilde{G}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}})
such that
the coefficient
\ell
of
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
in the

\Delta({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
-highest root
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{0}
of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})

is prime, and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
is contained in
the Borel–de Siebenthal subgroup corresponding to

({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}},{\widetilde{\alpha}})

(Definition
4.2.3
).
Note that
\ell
is different from
the characteristic exponent of
k
.
Let
\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}
be the fundamental coweight of

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
corresponding to
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
.
Then

\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}(\mu_{\ell})
,
which is the center of the relevant Borel–de Siebenthal subgroup
of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}

by Remark
4.2.4
(
a
),
is contained in

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
, which, we recall,
equals

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
.

If
\gamma
does not preserve
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
, then
the various conjugates

\gamma^{i}\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}
with
0\leq i<p
are
nontrivial cocharacters of different
almost-simple components of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
, so
their sum

(1+\gamma+\dotsb+\gamma^{p-1})\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}

is a
\gamma
-fixed, faithful cocharacter of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
that
carries
\mu_{\ell}
into

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
.
This is a contradiction.
Thus
\gamma
preserves
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
.

By
Lemmas
6.1
and
6.4
,
the action of
\gamma
on
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}

remains quasisemisimple.
Since the image of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}
contains

\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}(\mu_{\ell})
, it is nontrivial.
Since replacing

{\widetilde{B}}
,
{\widetilde{T}}
, and
\Gamma^{\prime}
by
their images in
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}
, then

{\widetilde{G}}
by
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}
, replaces

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
by a subgroup,
we may, and do, make this replacement, and so assume that

{\widetilde{G}}
is almost simple of adjoint type.
In particular, we now need not distinguish between

{\widetilde{G}}
and
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.
Now put
{\widetilde{H}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}(\mu_{\ell}))^{\circ}
, so that

Z({\widetilde{H}})
equals

\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}(\mu_{\ell})
.

Since
\gamma
has no nontrivial fixed points on

Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
,
hence on
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
in particular it acts nontrivially on
{\widetilde{G}}
.
That is,
p
is prime, and

\gamma
has order
p
.

Suppose first that
p
equals
2
.
Since
\gamma^{2}
acts trivially, but

\gamma
acts without fixed points, on

Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
,
in fact
\gamma
acts on
Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
by

and so preserves

In particular,
\gamma
preserves

\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}(\mu_{\ell})
.
If
\ell
equals
p

(which therefore does not equal the characteristic exponent of
k
),
then, since the restriction of
\gamma
to

\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}(\mu_{\ell}(k))
is

whose

p

actually
\gamma
itself acts trivially on
the nontrivial group

\widetilde{\varpi}^{\vee}(\mu_{\ell}(k))
.
This is a contradiction.

Thus we may, and do, suppose that

p
does not equal
2
, or

\ell
does not equal
p
.
Since
{\widetilde{G}}
is (absolutely) almost simple and
admits an outer automorphism
\gamma
of order

,
by Remark

,
there are now only two possibilities.

First,

equals
2
, then

{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{E}_{6}
,

is of type
3\mathsf{A}_{2}
, and

10.1.6
shows that
