is nontrivial.

Second,
if
p
equals
3
, then

\ell
equals
2
and

{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
.
In the Bourbaki numbering

[
bourbaki:lie-gp+lie-alg_4-6
]
*Chapter VI, Plate IV

(10.1.8)

\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}}

of
\Delta({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
(except with
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
in place of
\alpha
),
we have that

{\widetilde{\alpha}}
equals
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}
, and

\Phi({\widetilde{H}},{\widetilde{T}})
is
the orthogonal direct sum of the
integrally closed root subsystems of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})
spanned by

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}\right\}
,

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}\right\}
,

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}\right\}
, and

\left\{-{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{0}\right\}
, each of which
has type
\mathsf{A}_{1}
.
In particular, since

is semisimple (as

is finite) and equals

C_{\widetilde{G}}(Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}))^{\circ}=C_{\widetilde{H}}(Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}))^{\circ}
,
Lemma

gives that

equals

; thus

equals

,
hence

.
Since

is trivial but

, we have that

acts trivially on

.
This is a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 10.1.9
.

Suppose that
k
is separably closed, that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is a
\gamma
-stable subgroup of
a torus in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
, and that

{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Then

\gamma
is a quasisemisimple automorphism of

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
, and

(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})^{\gamma}
is smooth.

Proof.
If
k
has characteristic exponent
1
, then
every
k
-group is smooth by Cartier’s theorem

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Theorem 3.23; and

\gamma
, being of finite order, is semisimple, hence induces
a quasisemisimple automorphism of any group that
it preserves, such as

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}

(Corollary
9.1
).
Thus we may, and do, assume that

p
is prime, and

k
has characteristic exponent
p
.
In particular,
\gamma
is unipotent.

We reason by induction on
\dim({\widetilde{G}})
.
If the dimension is
0
, then
{\widetilde{G}}
, and hence
the result, is trivial.
Thus we may, and do, assume that

\dim({\widetilde{G}})
is positive, and
that we have proven the result for all smaller-dimensional groups.

The result is obvious if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is trivial, so
we assume that it is not.

Since
C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
is reductive by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.12,
we have that
Z(C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
is
contained in every maximal torus in

C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
Remark
6.8
and
Lemma
7.9
give that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.

By, and with the notation of,
Proposition
10.1.7
,
we have that

Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})^{\gamma}
or

\Lie(Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}))
is nontrivial.

If
Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})^{\gamma}
is nontrivial, then
choose a nontrivial
k
-point
s^{\prime}
, and
put
{\widetilde{H}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(s^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
Since
s^{\prime}
is nontrivial, we have that

{\widetilde{H}}
is a proper subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
If
\Lie(Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})
is nontrivial, then,
since
\gamma
is unipotent, so is

\Lie(Z(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ})^{\gamma}
.
Put
{\widetilde{H}}=C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is nontrivial, we have that

{\widetilde{H}}
is a proper subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.

In either case,

{\widetilde{H}}
is a proper subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that
contains
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
We have by Lemma
10.1.4
that

{\widetilde{H}}
is a connected, reductive subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
, that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{H}}
, and that

{\widetilde{H}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Let us temporarily write
{\widetilde{T}}^{\prime}_{1}
for
a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
that
contains
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and

{\widetilde{T}}_{1}
for the corresponding
maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Then
{\widetilde{T}}_{1}
is centralized by
{\widetilde{T}}^{\prime}_{1}
, hence by
\Gamma^{\prime}
, so

{\widetilde{T}}_{1}
is contained in
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
, hence in
{\widetilde{H}}
.
In particular,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is contained in
{\widetilde{H}}/Z({\widetilde{G}})
; and
the image of
{\widetilde{T}}_{1}
in
{\widetilde{H}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
is
a maximal torus there that contains
the image of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
That is, all the hypotheses remain valid if
we replace
{\widetilde{G}}
by
{\widetilde{H}}
, and

\Gamma^{\prime}
by its image in
{\widetilde{H}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.

Since
{\widetilde{H}}
is a proper subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
we may apply the result inductively to
{\widetilde{H}}
.
Since

C_{\widetilde{H}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
equals
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
,
the conclusion for
{\widetilde{H}}
is the same as that for
{\widetilde{G}}
.
∎

Proposition
10.1.11
relies on
surprisingly deep facts about simple groups whose orders avoid
certain small primes.
We thank the user who pointed out that the Suzuki groups are
the only non-commutative, simple groups of
order relatively prime to
3
in
[
MSE4959294
]
,
and the user who provided the excellent history and
literature survey in
[
MSE4959778
]
.

We shall also need to rely on the following fact.

Remark 10.1.10
.

Suppose that
k
is separably closed,

{\widetilde{G}}
is almost simple of rank at most
4
,
and
{\widetilde{G}}
does not have type

\mathsf{F}_{4}
.
Then there is a homomorphism

{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\PGL_{8}}
with central kernel,
and the group of diagram automorphisms of
{\widetilde{G}}
has
order at most
6
.

Proposition 10.1.11
.

Suppose that

{\widetilde{G}}
is absolutely almost simple, and

\gamma
is nontrivial.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a nontrivial, finite subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
of
order relatively prime to
p
.
Then we have the following:

(a)

\mathcal{H}
is solvable.

(b)

\mathcal{H}

admits a nontrivial,
\gamma
-stable subgroup that is
contained in a torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
.

Proof.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be such a subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
.
Since
\gamma
is nontrivial and

\gamma^{p}
is trivial, we have that
p

is not
1
, hence is prime.
We may, and do, replace
k
by
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
Note that every element of
\mathcal{H}
is semisimple.

If
p
equals
2
, then
(
a
) follows from
the Feit–Thompson odd-order theorem (!)
[
feit-thompson:solvable
]
.
For (
b
),
we may, and do, assume, upon replacing
\mathcal{H}
by
the last nontrivial term in its derived series, that

\mathcal{H}
is nontrivial and commutative.
Of course, every cyclic subgroup of
\mathcal{H}
is
contained in a maximal torus in
{\widetilde{G}}

[
borel:linear
]
*Corollary 11.12.
If
\gamma
has a nontrivial fixed point on

\mathcal{H}
, then
the subgroup generated by that fixed point is
\gamma
-stable.
If not, then

\bar{s}\gamma(\bar{s})
is
\gamma
-fixed, so
trivial, for all

\bar{s}\in\mathcal{H}
; hence

\gamma
acts by inversion on
\mathcal{H}
,
so every cyclic subgroup of
\mathcal{H}
is
\gamma
-stable.
This shows (
b
).

We have handled the case
p=2
.
Since
{\widetilde{G}}
is (absolutely) almost simple and
admits an outer automorphism
\gamma
of order
p
,
by Remark
2.1.5
,
the only other possibility is that

p
equals
3
and
{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
.
We suppose for the remainder of the proof that
we are in this case.

We first show how (
b
)
follows from (
a
).
Thus, we assume for now that

\mathcal{H}
is solvable.
Again, we may assume that
\mathcal{H}
is commutative and that
it contains no nontrivial
\gamma
-fixed points.
Fix a nontrivial element
\bar{s}\in\mathcal{H}
of
prime order
\ell
.
Since

\gamma^{2}(\bar{s})\gamma(\bar{s})\bar{s}

is preserved by
\gamma
, it is trivial; so

\gamma^{2}(\bar{s})
equals

\bar{s}^{-1}\gamma(\bar{s})^{-1}
, whence
the group generated by

\bar{s}
and
\gamma(\bar{s})
is

\gamma
-stable.
We claim that

\gamma(\bar{s})
, which certainly lies in
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s})(k)

(because
\mathcal{H}
is commutative), actually lies in

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s})^{\circ}(k)
.
This will show that

\gamma(\bar{s})
belongs to a torus in
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s})^{\circ}
,
which necessarily also contains
\bar{s}

[
borel:linear
]
*Corollary 11.12, hence that
the group generated by
\bar{s}
and
\gamma(\bar{s})
is
contained in a torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
.

Suppose first that
\ell
is odd.
We have by

[
digne-michel:non-connected
]
*Proposition 1.27 that

\pi_{0}(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s}))(k)
has order dividing
\ell
, and by

[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Theorem 9.1(a) that

\pi_{0}(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s}))(k)
is isomorphic to a subquotient of

\ker({{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}})(k)
.
Since
{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
and

\ker({{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}})
is central in

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}
,
the group of
k
-rational points of the kernel, hence
its subquotient
\pi_{0}(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s}))(k)
, has

2
-power order.
That is,
\pi_{0}(C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s}))(k)
is trivial, so

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s})
is connected.

Thus we may, and do, assume that

\bar{s}
has order
\ell=2
.
We now turn to the more subtle matter of
showing that
\gamma(\bar{s})
still
belongs to
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s})^{\circ}(k)
.

By two applications of
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Lemma 9.16,
there is a unique automorphism

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}
of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}
whose action is intertwined,

via
the quotient
{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}}
,
with the action of
\gamma
on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}}
; and

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}^{3}
is trivial
(because it is intertwined with
\gamma^{3}=1
).
In particular,
\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}
is an
order-
3
, outer automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}
.
With the Bourbaki numbering

[
bourbaki:lie-gp+lie-alg_4-6
]
*Chapter VI, Plate IV
(see (
10.1.8
))
of a system of simple roots of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})

(except with
{\widetilde{\alpha}}
instead of
\alpha
),
we have that

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}
acts as the
3
-cycle

({\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}\ {\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}\ {\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4})
on the Dynkin diagram, and

Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})
is the constant Klein 4-group
whose nontrivial elements are

({\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}^{\vee}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}^{\vee})(-1)
,

({\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}^{\vee}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}^{\vee})(-1)
, and

({\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}^{\vee}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}^{\vee})(-1)
.
In particular, the action of
\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}
on

Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})(k)
has
no nontrivial fixed points.

Let
s
be a lift of
\bar{s}
to
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}(k)
.
Remember that

\gamma^{2}(\bar{s})
equals

\bar{s}^{-1}\gamma(\bar{s})^{-1}=\bar{s}\gamma(\bar{s})
, so the lifts

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}^{2}(s)
of
\gamma^{2}(\bar{s})
and

s\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)
of
\bar{s}\gamma(\bar{s})
are
translates of one another by

\ker({{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}})\subseteq Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})
.
That is, there is some
z\in Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})(k)
such that

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}^{2}(s)
equals
s\gamma(s)z
.
Similarly, since the image

[\bar{s},\gamma(\bar{s})]
of

[s,\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)]
in
\mathcal{H}
is trivial, we have that

[s,\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)]
lies in
Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})(k)
.
Further, we have that

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}[s,\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)]\quad\text{equals}\quad\\
[\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s),\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}^{2}(s)]=[\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s),s\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)z]=[\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s),s]\cdot\Int(s)[\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s),\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)z]=[\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s),s]=[s,\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)]^{-1}.

It follows that
\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}^{2}
, hence also

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}=\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}^{4}
, fixes
[s,\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)]
.
Since
\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}
has no nontrivial fixed points on

Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}})(k)
, it follows that

[s,\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)]
is trivial, i.e., that

\gamma_{\textup{{sc}}}(s)
belongs to
C_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{sc}}}}(s)(k)
; and
so, by
[
steinberg:endomorphisms
]
*Lemma 9.2(a), that
its image

\gamma(\bar{s})
belongs to
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\bar{s})^{\circ}(k)
.
This completes the proof of
(
b
), assuming
(
a
).

Recall that we have reduced to the case that

p
equals
3
and
{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
.
To prove (
a
),
we drop the assumption that
\mathcal{H}
is solvable;
indeed, we assume for the sake of contradiction that it is not.

We may, and do, assume, upon replacing
{\widetilde{G}}
by
a central quotient of a connected, reductive subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
, that

{\widetilde{G}}
has no
nontrivial such subquotient
whose
group of
k
-rational points contains
a finite, non-solvable subgroup of
order relatively prime to
p=3
.
(Although this replacement preserves almost simplicity, it
may destroy the existence of a
nontrivial, order-
p
, quasisemisimple automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}
;
but this is no issue, since we used the existence of
such an automorphism only to force
p=3
.)
Since
\mathcal{H}
is contained in
the extension of its image in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
by
the commutative group
Z({\widetilde{G}})(k)
,
the image of
\mathcal{H}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
is still
a finite, non-solvable subgroup of
order relatively prime to
p=3
; so
our minimality assumption forces
{\widetilde{G}}
to be adjoint.

Now there is a positive integer
n
such that
the Suzuki group
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{:=}{}^{2}\mathsf{B}_{2}(2^{2n+1})

(of order
2^{2(2n+1)}(2^{2(2n+1)}+1)(2^{2n+1}-1)
)
is a composition factor of
\mathcal{H}

[
aschbacher:s3-free-2-fusion
]
*p. 29, Corollary 4

[
toborg-waldecker:3'=>Sz
]
*Theorem 3.1
.
Upon replacing
\mathcal{H}
by
an appropriate subgroup, we may, and do, assume that it is
an extension of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}
by a solvable group
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is nontrivial.
Then the last nontrivial term in the derived series of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}
is
a finite, commutative subgroup
\mathcal{S}
of
{\widetilde{G}}(k)
of
order relatively prime to
p
that
is normalized by
\mathcal{H}
, i.e., for which

\mathcal{H}
is contained in
N_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})(k)
.
Since the constant group associated to

\mathcal{S}
is commutative, and linearly reductive by
Remark
2.2.5
, it is
of multiplicative type by

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Proposition 12.54.
The rigidity of such groups

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 12.37 gives that

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})^{\circ}
, which is
reductive
[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.12 and
obviously connected,
is the identity component of
N_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})
.
Since
N_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})(k)/C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})^{\circ}(k)
may
be identified with a group of diagram automorphisms of

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})^{\circ}
, it has order at most
6

from Remark
10.1.10
.
Since the order of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}
is at least

8^{2}(8^{2}+1)(8-1)
, which is greater than
6
, we have that

\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}
is a composition factor of

\mathcal{H}\cap C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})^{\circ}(k)
.
That is,
\mathcal{H}\cap C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})^{\circ}(k)
is
a finite, non-solvable subgroup of
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\mathcal{S})^{\circ}(k)

consisting of semisimple elements.
By minimality of
{\widetilde{G}}
, we have that

\mathcal{S}
is central in
{\widetilde{G}}
, hence, because

{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint, is trivial.
This is a contradiction.

That is,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is trivial, so
the Suzuki group
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}
is a subgroup of

{\widetilde{G}}
.
We may, and do, replace
\mathcal{H}
by
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}
.
From Remark
10.1.10
,
there is a homomorphism

{\rho}\colon{{\widetilde{G}}}\longrightarrow{\PGL_{8}}

with central kernel.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is now simple and non-commutative,
the intersection of
\mathcal{H}
with

\ker(\rho)(k)
is trivial, so

\rho
restricts to an embedding of

\mathcal{H}
into
\PGL_{8}(k)
.
Write
\mathcal{H}^{+}
for the pre-image of

\mathcal{H}
in
\SL_{8}(k)
.
Then
the characteristic exponent of
k

(which is
1
or
3
)
is relatively prime to the order of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and

\mathcal{H}^{+}
has a nontrivial representation on
k^{8}
.
By Maschke’s theorem
[
isaacs:char-fg
]
*Theorem 1.9,
all representations of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
on
k
-vector spaces are
completely reducible, so
the existence of
a nontrivial representation of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
on
k^{8}
implies
the existence of
an
irreducible
representation of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
on
a
k
-vector space of dimension at most
8
.
By
[
isaacs:char-fg
]
*Theorem 15.13,
there is a
nontrivial, irreducible, complex representation of

\mathcal{H}^{+}
of dimension at most
8
,
hence a
nontrivial, irreducible, complex
projective
representation of

\mathcal{H}
of dimension at most
8
.

If
n
equals
1
, that is, if

\mathcal{H}
is the Suzuki group
{}^{2}\mathsf{B}_{2}(8)
, then

[
atlas-fg
]
*p. 28 shows that the smallest dimension
of a nontrivial, irreducible, complex projective representation of

\mathcal{H}
is
14
,
which is a contradiction.

If
n
is greater than
1
, then

\mathcal{H}
has trivial Schur multiplier

[
alperin-gorenstein:schur
]
*p. 515, Theorem 1, so

\mathcal{H}
has a
nontrivial, irreducible, complex (linear) representation of
dimension at most
8
.
By
[
suzuki:my-groups
]
*§11, p. 127, Theorem 5,
the smallest dimension of a
nontrivial, irreducible, complex representation
of
\mathcal{H}
is

2^{n}(2^{2n+1}-1)
, which is greater than
8
.
Again, this is a contradiction.
This completes the proof of
(
a
).
∎

10.2.
Purely outer, non-cyclic actions on
\mathsf{D}_{4}

We are building to the proof of
Theorem
C
, which concerns the action of
a group of automorphisms each of which individually
preserves a Borel–torus pair, but where the pair might
depend on the automorphism.
Although this is a weakening of the hypothesis of
Theorem
A
, where the automorphisms are
required to preserve a
common
Borel–torus pair,
we will show in §
10.3
that
fine control of the automorphism groups of
absolutely almost-simple groups allows us nearly to
reduce to that case, or the prime-to-
p
case handled by

[
prasad-yu:actions
]
.
Most of the difficulty is caused by the presence of
an outer-automorphism group whose order is divisible by
p
,
which was handled in §
10.1
, and by
a non-cyclic outer-automorphism group.
In this subsection, we handle the latter case.

Remember that
p
is
1
or a prime number, and that

k
has characteristic exponent
p
or
1
.
In this section, we consider only
p=2
.

Proposition 10.2.1
.

Suppose that

k
is separably closed,

p
equals
2
,

({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
is a reductive datum with

{\widetilde{G}}
the adjoint group of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
, and
the map
{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Out}({\widetilde{G}})}
is an isomorphism.
Let
\sigma
and
\tau
be elements of
\Gamma(k)\cong\Out({\widetilde{G}})

of
respective orders
2
and
3
.
Suppose that
\sigma
and
\tau
act quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Then
\sigma
acts quasisemisimply on
({\widetilde{G}}^{\tau})^{\circ}
, and

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\tau})^{\circ})^{\sigma}
is smooth.

Proof.
Let
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
be a Borel–torus pair in
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is preserved by
\tau
.
Since the characteristic exponent of
k
is not
3
,
we have that
\tau
is semisimple,
so Remark
2.2.5
and

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.10(2) give that

{\widetilde{T}}^{\tau}
and
{\widetilde{G}}^{\tau}
are smooth.
Theorem
A
(
2
)
gives that
H\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{G}}^{\tau})^{\circ}
is reductive, while
Proposition
7.1
(
a
)
and
Proposition
6.5
(
a
,
b
)
give that

T\mathrel{:=}({\widetilde{T}}^{\tau})^{\circ}
is
a maximal torus in
H
, and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(T)
equals
{\widetilde{T}}
.

If
k
has characteristic exponent
1
, then

\sigma
is semisimple, so the result follows from
Corollary
9.1
.
Thus we may, and do, assume that

k
has characteristic exponent
2
.

We have that
\Delta({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
is
the union of two
\tau
-orbits of sizes
1
and
3
.
Specifically, if we number
\Delta({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
as
in (
10.1.8
),
then
\tau
fixes
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}
, and
admits
\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1},{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3},{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}\right\}
as
an orbit.
Upon replacing
\tau
by
\tau^{-1}
if necessary, which
does not affect the conclusion, we may, and do, assume that

\tau{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}
equals
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}
, and

\tau{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}
equals
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}
.
If
{\widetilde{X}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}}
is a nonzero element of

\Lie({\widetilde{G}})_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
, then
the
\tau
-orbit of
{\widetilde{X}}_{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}}
is
obviously preserved by
\tau
, and
contains exactly one nonzero root vector for each
root in
\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1},{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3},{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}\right\}
.
Thus, combining it with any nonzero element of
the
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}
-root space gives
a pinning
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, in the sense of

[
SGA-3.3
]
*Exposé XXIII, Définition 1.1

[
kaletha-prasad:bt-theory
]
*Definition 2.9.1
.
This pinning is preserved by
\tau
if and only if

\tau
is pinned, in the sense of

[
SGA-3.3
]
*Exposé XXIII, Définition 1.3.
It determines a section of the natural map from

\Aut({\widetilde{G}})
to
the group of diagram automorphisms of
the based root datum
\Psi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
, hence a
retraction of
\Aut({\widetilde{G}})
onto
the subgroup of automorphisms that
preserve
{\widetilde{B}}
,
{\widetilde{T}}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
.
Write
\sigma_{0}
and
\tau_{0}
for
the images of
\sigma
and
\tau
under
this retraction.
Thus,
\sigma_{0}
and
\tau_{0}
are the pinned automorphisms corresponding
to
\sigma
and
\tau
.

Write
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{4}
for
the common restriction to
T
of

{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}
,
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}
, and
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}
, and

\alpha_{2}
for the restriction of
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}
.
We have that

\Phi(({\widetilde{G}}^{\tau_{0}})^{\circ},T)
equals
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},T)
and is of
type
\mathsf{G}_{2}
, and

\Delta(({\widetilde{B}}^{\tau_{0}})^{\circ},T)

and

\Delta({\widetilde{B}},T)

both equal

\left\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\right\}
.

Suppose first that
\tau
is pinned, so that

\tau
equals
\tau_{0}
and

H
equals
({\widetilde{G}}^{\tau_{0}})^{\circ}
.
We have that

\sigma_{0}
preserves
({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
,
and
\sigma
equals
\sigma_{0}\circ\Int(u)
for some
u\in{\widetilde{G}}(k)
.
Since both
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
normalize
\left\langle\tau\right\rangle
,
they preserve
H
, so

\Int(u)
must as well.

Since
H
is of type
\mathsf{G}_{2}
,
it has no nontrivial outer automorphisms, so
the restriction of
\sigma_{0}
to
H

is a toral inner automorphism, hence is trivial since its order is
2
.
Thus the common restriction of

\sigma
and
\Int(u)
to
H
is inner, hence
coincides with
\Int(u^{\prime})
for some

u^{\prime}\in H(k)

(because
H
is adjoint).
That is,
uu^{\prime\,{-1}}
centralizes
H
.
Since
H
is adjoint and

\Int(u^{\prime})^{2}
is the restriction of
\sigma^{2}=1
to
H
,
we have that
u^{\prime\,2}
is trivial, so
u^{\prime}
is unipotent.
Examining the orbits of
\tau
on
\Psi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})

shows that each root space for

{\widetilde{T}}
in
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})
is
the image under projection from a root space for

T
in
\Lie(H)
.
Consequently, the group
C_{\widetilde{G}}(H)
, which is certainly contained in

C_{\widetilde{G}}(T)={\widetilde{T}}
, also
acts trivially on each root space for
{\widetilde{T}}
in
\Lie({\widetilde{G}})
,
hence is central in
{\widetilde{G}}
.
In particular,
uu^{\prime\,{-1}}
is central in
{\widetilde{G}}
.
That is,
\Int(u)
equals
\Int(u^{\prime})
on all of
{\widetilde{G}}
, so
we may, and do, assume, upon replacing
u
by
u^{\prime}
, that

u
is a unipotent element of
H(k)
.
Let
B_{H}
be a Borel subgroup of
H
with
u\in B_{H}(k)
,
and let
{\widetilde{U}}
be the
\tau
-stable unipotent radical of a

\tau
-stable Borel subgroup of

{\widetilde{G}}
containing
B_{H}

(whose existence is given by
Proposition
7.1
(
b
)).

Since
k
has characteristic exponent
2

and
\sigma
is a quasisemisimple involution of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
we have that
\sigma
is pinned.
(We are
not
(yet) claiming that
\sigma
preserves
(
{\widetilde{B}}
,
{\widetilde{T}}
), or
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, only that
\sigma
preserves
some pinning with respect to
a Borel–torus pair that it preserves.
The argument for this is the same as we used to show that

\tau
was “almost pinned”,
namely, collecting
pairs of root vectors for every pair of roots swapped by
\sigma
,
but now noting that the scalar by which
\sigma
can
act on the root space corresponding to
a
\sigma
-fixed root is
an element of
\mu_{2}(k)=\left\{1\right\}
.)
Since all pinnings of
{\widetilde{G}}
are conjugate in
{\widetilde{G}}(k)

(because
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint),
there exists
g\in{\widetilde{G}}(k)
such that

\Int(g)\sigma\Int(g)^{-1}=\Int(g)\sigma_{0}\Int(u)\Int(g)^{-1}
equals
\sigma_{0}
.
Letting
\bar{u}
and
\bar{g}
be the respective images
of
u
and
g
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)
,
we have
\bar{u}=\sigma_{0}(\bar{g}){\bar{g}}^{-1}
.
Let
{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}
be the image of
{\widetilde{U}}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
, and
let
z
be the class of the cocycle

\theta\mapsto\theta(\bar{g})\bar{g}^{-1}
in
H^{1}(\langle\sigma_{0}\rangle,{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}(k))
.

Corollary
7.4
implies that the map

{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\sigma_{0}}(k)}\longrightarrow{({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}/{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime})^{\sigma_{0}}(k)}
is surjective.
It follows from the cohomology exact sequence associated to the
inclusion
{{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}(k)}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k)}
that the kernel of the map

{H^{1}(\langle\sigma_{0}\rangle,{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}(k))}\longrightarrow{H^{1}(\langle\sigma_{0}\rangle,{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k))}

is trivial. Since the image of
z
in
H^{1}(\langle\sigma_{0}\rangle,{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}(k))
is trivial,

z
must therefore be as well. Thus
\bar{u}
equals
\sigma_{0}(\bar{v})\bar{v}^{-1}
for some
\bar{v}\in\bar{\widetilde{U}}(k)
.
Since
u
is fixed by
\tau
, so is

\bar{u}=\sigma_{0}(\bar{v})\bar{v}^{-1}
.
A straightforward explicit computation involving the root groups in
{\widetilde{U}}^{\prime}

shows that this is possible only if
\sigma_{0}(\bar{v})
equals
\bar{v}
, i.e.,
\bar{u}=\sigma_{0}(\bar{v})\bar{v}^{-1}
is trivial.
It follows that
\sigma=\sigma_{0}\circ\Int(u)

equals
\sigma_{0}
, hence acts trivially on
H
.
This is certainly a quasisemisimple action, and
the fixed-point group is all of
H
, hence smooth.

Now suppose instead that
\tau
is not pinned.
The fundamental coweight

\varpi^{\vee}_{2}
associated to

\alpha_{2}\in\Delta(({\widetilde{B}}^{\tau_{0}})^{\circ},T)

(which is the same as the fundamental coweight associated to

{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}\in\Delta({\widetilde{B}},{\widetilde{T}})
)
equals

{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}^{\vee}+2{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}^{\vee}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}^{\vee}+{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}^{\vee}
.
Put
t=\varpi^{\vee}_{2}(\zeta)
, where

\zeta
is the scalar by which
\tau
acts on
the
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{2}
-root space.
Then
\tau
equals
\tau_{0}\circ\Int(t)
, and

H
equals
C_{{\widetilde{G}}^{\tau_{0}}}(t)^{\circ}
.
(There is content to the latter assertion, as
the analogous statement is not true for
an arbitrary product of a pinned automorphism and
a semisimple, inner automorphism with which it commutes.)
In fact,
Z(H)
is a torus, and
its cocharacter lattice is spanned by
\varpi^{\vee}_{2}
.
Since
\sigma
has order
2
and preserves
Z(H)
, it
sends
\varpi^{\vee}_{2}
to
\pm\varpi^{\vee}_{2}
, so
fixes
d\varpi^{\vee}_{2}
.
By Lemma
10.1.4
, we have that

\sigma
acts quasisemisimply on

{\widetilde{H}}\mathrel{:=}C_{\widetilde{G}}(d\varpi^{\vee}_{2})^{\circ}
.
Since
\varpi^{\vee}_{2}
is a central cocharacter of
H
, we have that

H
is contained in
{\widetilde{H}}
, hence equals

({\widetilde{H}}^{\tau})^{\circ}
.

We have that
\Phi({\widetilde{H}},{\widetilde{T}})
is
the orthogonal direct sum of the
integrally closed root subsystems of
\Phi({\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{T}})
spanned by

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}\right\}
,

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}\right\}
,

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}\right\}
, and

\left\{-{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{0}\right\}
, each of which
has type
\mathsf{A}_{1}
.
Both
\sigma
and
\tau
preserve
{\widetilde{H}}
,
and
\sigma
permutes the almost-simple components of
{\widetilde{H}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
that
intersect
({\widetilde{H}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\tau})^{\circ}
nontrivially.
There are three of these components, with root systems generated by

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}\right\}
,

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{3}\right\}
and

\left\{{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{4}\right\}
.
Since
\sigma
has order
2
, it must
preserve at least one of these almost-simple components.
Let
{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
be such an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{H}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
that is
preserved by
\sigma
.
(This labelling is arbitrary; but,
if desired for notational consistency, then
we could replace
\sigma
by
its conjugate by
\tau
or
\tau^{-1}
, which
does not affect the conclusion, to
ensure that
{\widetilde{\alpha}}_{1}
belongs to
\Phi({\widetilde{H}}_{1},{\widetilde{T}})
.)
Since
\sigma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{H}}
,
Lemma
6.4
and Lemma
6.1

give that
it also acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
.
Since
the characteristic exponent of
k
is
2
,

\sigma^{2}
is trivial, and

{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
has type
\mathsf{A}_{1}
,
we have that
\sigma
has a
unipotent inner action on
{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
,
hence is trivial on
{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
.
Since the canonical projection
{{\widetilde{H}}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{H}}_{1}}

restricts to
a
\sigma
-equivariant, central isogeny
from
H=({\widetilde{H}}^{\tau})^{\circ}
onto
{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
,
Lemma
6.4
gives that

\sigma
acts quasisemisimply on
H
.

Since
\sigma
acts trivially on a
central quotient of
H
, it acts trivially on

H_{\textup{{der}}}
.
Remember that
Z(H)
is the image of
\varpi_{2}^{\vee}
, and that

\sigma\varpi_{2}^{\vee}
equals
\pm\varpi_{2}^{\vee}
.
If
\sigma\varpi_{2}^{\vee}
equals
\varpi_{2}^{\vee}
, then

\sigma
also acts trivially on
Z(H)
.
That is,
H^{\sigma}
is all of
H
.
If
\sigma\varpi_{2}^{\vee}
equals
-\varpi_{2}^{\vee}
, then

H^{\sigma}
is generated by

H_{\textup{{der}}}
and

Z(H)^{\sigma}=\varpi_{2}^{\vee}(\mu_{2})
.
Since

\varpi_{2}^{\vee}-(\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{4}^{\vee})

equals
2\alpha_{2}^{\vee}
, hence is trivial on
\mu_{2}
,
we have that

\varpi_{2}^{\vee}(\mu_{2})

equals

(\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{4}^{\vee})(\mu_{2})
,
which is contained in
H_{\textup{{der}}}
.
Thus,
H^{\sigma}
equals
H_{\textup{{der}}}
, which is again smooth.
∎

10.3.
Completion of the proof of Theorem
C

In this subsection,
we use the notation and hypotheses of Theorem
C
.
That is, we now consider, not just
our connected, reductive
k
-group
{\widetilde{G}}
, but
a reductive datum
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma)
over
k
.
We assume that every
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})

acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, with
smooth fixed-point group on
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{ad}\,{k^{\textup{{a}}}}}
.
As usual, we put
G=({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

So far in §
10
, we have assumed that

p
is
1
or a prime number, and that

k
has characteristic exponent
1
or
p
.
We now require that
k
actually have
characteristic exponent
p

(but we continue to allow the possibility that

p
equals
1
).

Remark 10.3.1
.

Suppose that
p
equals
1
, and that

\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
is unipotent.
Let
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
be an almost-simple component of
{\widetilde{G}}
.
There is some positive integer
n
such that

\gamma^{n}
preserves
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
, and
some positive multiple
N
of
n
such that

\gamma^{N}
is an inner automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
.
Since
\gamma^{N}
is an inner automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
, and
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
by Lemma
6.1
,
we have that
\gamma^{N}
is a
inner, quasisemisimple, unipotent automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
,
hence trivial
(Remark
2.2.8
(
b
)).
Thus
\gamma^{n}
acts as a
finite-order, unipotent automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
, hence is trivial.

Proposition 10.3.2
.

Suppose that
k
is algebraically closed,
and that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is a smooth, normal subgroup of
\Gamma
such that

\Gamma^{\prime}(k)
contains only semisimple elements.
For every element
\gamma\in\Gamma(k)
, we have that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\langle\gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}
is smooth and

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
.

Note
.

The group
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
is reductive by
Remark
2.2.5
and

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.12.

Proof.
We reason by induction on

\dim({\widetilde{G}})+\dim(Z({\widetilde{G}}))+\lvert Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})\rvert
.
Here,
\lvert Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})\rvert
is the cardinality of
the finite group scheme
Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})

(the dimension of its ring of regular functions), not just of
its group of
k
-rational points.
Thus, for example,
\lvert\mu_{p}\rvert
equals
p
, not
1

(unless
p
equals
1
).

If the sum is
1
, then

{\widetilde{G}}
, and the result, are trivial.

Suppose first that we have proven the result under
the additional hypothesis that

\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
is generated by
a unipotent element, and
the conclusion replaced by the claim that

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth and

\Gamma
or, equivalently, any generator of
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime})(k)

acts quasisemisimply on
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
We
fix
\gamma\in\Gamma(k)
, and write

\gamma_{\textup{semi}}
and
\gamma_{\textup{unip}}
for
its semisimple and unipotent parts.
Then, with

\Gamma
replaced by
\left\langle\gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\right\rangle
and

\Gamma^{\prime}
replaced by
\left\langle\gamma_{\textup{semi}},\Gamma^{\prime}\right\rangle
,
our additional hypothesis is satisfied; so

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\left\langle\gamma_{\textup{unip}},\gamma_{\textup{semi}},\Gamma^{\prime}\right\rangle}
,
which equals

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle,\Gamma^{\prime}}
,
is smooth, and

\left\langle\gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\right\rangle
acts quasisemisimply on

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\gamma_{\textup{semi}})^{\circ}
.
Then
[
digne-michel:non-connected
]
*Lemme 1.14 gives that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime})^{\circ}
.

Thus we may, and do, assume that

\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
is generated by
a unipotent element.
If
\gamma
is any element of
\Gamma(k)
whose
image in
(\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime})(k)
generates
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
, then
the image of its unipotent part also
generates
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Thus we may, and do, assume, upon replacing

\gamma
by its unipotent part, that

\gamma
is unipotent.
Since the result is trivial if

\gamma
or
\Gamma^{\prime}
acts trivially on
{\widetilde{G}}
,
we assume that neither does.

The bulk of the proof consists of reducing to the situation of
Proposition
10.1.9
.
This takes some work.

First, we show that we may assume that

{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint.
If it is not, then

\dim(Z({\widetilde{G}}))
or

\lvert Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{der}}})\rvert
is strictly greater than
1
.
This means that

\dim({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})+\dim(Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}))+\lvert Z({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})\rvert=\dim({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})+1

is strictly less than

\dim({\widetilde{G}})+\lvert Z({\widetilde{G}})\rvert
, so
we already have the result for
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
by
the inductive hypothesis.
Then Lemma
6.4
gives
the result for
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Thus we may, and do, assume that
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint.

Next, we show that we may assume that

{\widetilde{G}}
is almost simple (as well as adjoint).
By Lemma
6.1
,
the following assertions are equivalent:

(i
qs
)

the action of
\Gamma

on

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\circ}
is quasisemisimple; and

(ii
qs
)

for every almost-simple component
{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
of

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\circ}
,
the action of
\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{H}}_{1})
on

{\widetilde{H}}_{1}
is quasisemisimple.

Corollary
4.3.6
and
Lemma
A.20
show that

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to

\prod{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\stab_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})}
, the product taken over one
almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
from each

\Gamma^{\prime}(k)
-orbit of such components.
Therefore, another application of
Lemma
6.1
gives that
(
iiqs
) is equivalent to
the following statement:

(3)

for every almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
the action of
\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})
on

({\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\stab_{\Gamma^{\prime}}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})})^{\circ}
is quasisemisimple.

Finally, another application of
Corollary
4.3.6
and
Lemma
A.20
shows that

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to

\prod{\widetilde{G}}_{1}^{\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})}
, the product taken over one
almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
from each

\Gamma(k)
-orbit of such components.
Thus,
remembering that
{\widetilde{G}}
and hence
each of its simple components is
adjoint,
the following assertions are also equivalent:

(i
sm
)

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma}
is smooth; and

(ii
sm
)

for every almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
of
{\widetilde{G}}
,
the fixed-point group
{\widetilde{G}}_{1\,\textup{ad}}^{\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})}

is smooth.

Thus, if
{\widetilde{G}}
is not almost simple, then we know inductively that
(
3
) and
(
iism
)
hold, so that
(
iqs
) and
(
ism
) hold; and
these two together give the modified result for
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Thus we may, and do, assume that

{\widetilde{G}}
is almost simple.
In particular,
since
\gamma
acts nontrivially on
{\widetilde{G}}
,
Remark
10.3.1
gives that

p
does not equal
1
.

Now, since
\gamma
is unipotent, it has finite,

p
-power order.
We have by Remark
2.1.5
that
the image of
\gamma
in
the outer-automorphism group of
{\widetilde{G}}
has
order dividing
p
.
In particular, since an
inner, quasisemisimple, unipotent automorphism of
{\widetilde{G}}

is trivial
(Remark
2.2.8
(
b
)),
we have that

\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
is constant of order
p
, and that
the natural map from
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
to
the outer-automorphism group of
{\widetilde{G}}
is
an embedding.
This will now allow us to apply
Proposition
10.1.9
in all cases but one,
which we handle separately.

If
\Gamma^{\prime}\cap{\widetilde{G}}
is trivial, then
the images of

\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
the outer-automorphism group of
{\widetilde{G}}
are
nontrivial subgroups of order
p
and relatively prime to
p
,
respectively.
Another appeal to Remark
2.1.5
gives that

p
equals
2
,

{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
, and

\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle\ltimes\Gamma^{\prime}
maps isomorphically onto

\underline{\Out}({\widetilde{G}})
.
Then the result follows from Proposition
10.2.1
.
Thus we may, and do, assume that

\Gamma^{\prime}\cap{\widetilde{G}}
is nontrivial.

We have that
\Gamma^{\prime\,\circ}
is a torus

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 17.25.
If it does not act trivially on
{\widetilde{G}}
, then
Proposition
10.1.9
allows us to
conclude by applying the inductive hypothesis to
the action of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime\,\circ}
on

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime\,\circ})^{\circ}
.
Thus we may, and do, assume that
\Gamma^{\prime\,\circ}

acts trivially, and so, upon
replacing
\Gamma^{\prime}
by
\pi_{0}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is étale.

Suppose first that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is commutative, and contained in
{\widetilde{G}}^{\prime}
.
Since
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint, we may regard it as
the identity component of
\underline{\Aut}({\widetilde{G}})
.
If
\gamma
acts trivially on
{\widetilde{G}}
, then
we are done.
Otherwise, by
Proposition
10.1.11
(
b
),
there is a nontrivial,
\gamma
-stable
subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
that is
contained in a torus in
{\widetilde{G}}
.
Since
\Gamma^{\prime}
is commutative, and

\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
is generated by the image of
\gamma
, we have that

\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is normal in
\Gamma
.
Proposition
10.1.9
gives that

\gamma
is a quasisemisimple automorphism of

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})^{\circ}
, and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Then we may apply our inductive hypothesis to
the action of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
on

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})^{\circ}
to obtain the desired result.

Now drop the assumption that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is commutative and contained in
{\widetilde{G}}
, but
keep the assumption that
\Gamma^{\prime}\cap{\widetilde{G}}
is nontrivial.
Since
\gamma
is also nontrivial,
Proposition
10.1.11
(
a
)
shows that
\Gamma^{\prime}\cap{\widetilde{G}}
is solvable.
Now let
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
be the last term in the derived series of

\Gamma^{\prime}\cap{\widetilde{G}}
, so that

\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a commutative, normal,
\gamma
-stable subgroup of
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
hence a normal subgroup of
\Gamma
.
The special case that we have already handled shows that

\gamma
is a quasisemisimple automorphism of

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})^{\circ}
, and

C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Then we conclude by applying the inductive hypothesis to
the action of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
on
C_{\widetilde{G}}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})^{\circ}
.
∎

Theorem 0
.

Suppose, for every
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
and

({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.

(1)

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
equals

(Z({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma})^{\circ}\cdot({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.

(2)

G
is reductive.

(3)

The functorial map from the spherical building
\mathscr{S}(G)
of
G

to the spherical building
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
of
{\widetilde{G}}

identifies
\mathscr{S}(G)
with

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.

Remark 10.3.3
.

In the context of
Theorem
C
,
let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the subgroup of
\Gamma
that
acts on
{\widetilde{G}}
by inner automorphisms,
so that there is a map

{\Gamma^{\prime}}\longrightarrow{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}
.
The image in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
of every element of
\Gamma^{\prime}({k^{\textup{{a}}}})

is a quasisemisimple, inner automorphism, hence semisimple.
In particular,
the image of
\Gamma^{\prime}
is linearly reductive
by Remark
2.2.5
.

Remark
2.2.8
(
a
) gives that

\Gamma^{\prime}
contains
\Gamma^{\circ}
.
The image of
\Gamma^{\circ}
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
is
a smooth, connected group all of whose

{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
-rational points are semisimple, so

[
milne:algebraic-groups
]
*Corollary 17.25
gives that the image is a torus.
(This generalizes part of
Remark
2.2.8
(
b
).)

Proof of Theorem
C
.
By Lemma
4.4.4
, we may, and do, assume,
upon replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{s}}}}
, that

k
is separably closed.

Remark
10.3.3
shows that
the image of
\Gamma^{\circ}
in
\underline{\Aut}({\widetilde{G}})

is a torus in
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
that is
preserved by
\Gamma
, and so, by
Remark
2.2.8
(
c
),
that
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma^{\circ})
is quasisemisimple.
We have by

[
borel:linear
]
*Corollary 11.12 that

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}
is connected and by

[
conrad-gabber-prasad:prg
]
*Proposition A.8.12
that
{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}}

is reductive—in particular, smooth.
Theorem
A
(
3
)
shows that
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})
equals

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma^{\circ}({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
.
Proposition
10.1.9
shows,
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
, that

\gamma
acts quasisemisimply on
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}

and

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}^{\gamma}
is smooth.
Thus we may, and do, assume, upon replacing

{\widetilde{G}}
by
({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})^{\circ}
and

\Gamma
by its image in
\Aut(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\circ}})^{\circ})
,
that

\Gamma
is étale, hence constant.

We now proceed by induction on

\dim({\widetilde{G}})+\left\lvert\Gamma\right\rvert
.
If the sum is
1
, then

{\widetilde{G}}
, and hence the result, is trivial.

Suppose that we have proven the result for
{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
.
In particular,
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smooth, so
Corollary
4.1.3
gives
(
1
).
Corollary
4.1.2
gives that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is a smooth, connected group that is
an extension of the reductive group
({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
by
a group of multiplicative type, whence (
2
).
To handle the reduction of (
3
) to
the adjoint case, we argue once more as in the proofs of
Lemma
4.4.4
and
Theorem
A
(
3
).
Let
b_{+}
be a point of

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})\cap\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
, and

b^{\prime}_{+}
its image in
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})
.
(Functoriality of the formation of spherical buildings is discussed
only with respect to embeddings in

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§4, or
isogenies in
[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§4, Remark (iv),
but this is only needed if we insist that
the resulting map of spherical buildings be
an injection.
An arbitrary homomorphism of reductive groups still
gives a perfectly good map of
the corresponding spherical buildings in
the obvious fashion.)
Then
b^{\prime}_{+}
belongs to

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})\cap\mathscr{S}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})^{\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})}
,
hence, by assumption, is the image in

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}})
of
an element of
\mathscr{S}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})
,
which we will also denote by
b^{\prime}_{+}
.
Let
b^{\prime}_{-}
be any point of
the spherical building
\mathscr{S}(({\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ})

opposite to
b^{\prime}_{+}
.
The pullback
P_{\widetilde{G}}(b^{\prime}_{-})
to
{\widetilde{G}}
of
the corresponding parabolic subgroup
P_{{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}}(b^{\prime}_{-})
of

{\widetilde{G}}_{\textup{{ad}}}
is
a
\Gamma
-stable parabolic subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
that
is opposite to
P_{\widetilde{G}}(b_{+})
.
It follows from

[
curtis-lehrer-tits:spherical
]
*§3 that there is
a unique point
b_{-}\in\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})
that is
opposite to
b_{+}
and satisfies

P_{\widetilde{G}}(b_{-})=P_{\widetilde{G}}(b^{\prime}_{-})
.
By uniqueness,

b_{-}
is also fixed by
\Gamma(k)
, so
Lemma
4.4.3
gives that

b_{+}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(G)
.

That is, we may, and do, assume that
{\widetilde{G}}
is adjoint.
Now (
1
) is the statement that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})^{\circ}
is smooth, not just smoothable.
Since this statement is unaffected by arbitrary base change,
we may, and do, assume, upon
replacing
k
by
{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
, that

k
is algebraically closed.

By
Corollary
4.3.6
and
Lemma
A.20
,
we may, and do, assume, upon
replacing

{\widetilde{G}}
by an (absolutely) almost-simple component
{\widetilde{G}}_{1}
and

\Gamma
by
\stab_{\Gamma}({\widetilde{G}}_{1})
, that

{\widetilde{G}}
is almost simple.
Suppose that there is a nontrivial
normal subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
such that

\Gamma^{\prime}(k)
contains only semisimple elements.
Then
[
prasad-yu:actions
]
*Theorem 2.1 and Proposition 3.4
gives that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\circ}
is (smooth and) reductive, and that

\mathscr{S}(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\circ})
equals
\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\Gamma^{\prime}(k)}
.
(In particular, note that this implies that (
3
)
holds for
({\widetilde{G}},\Gamma^{\prime})
, as
k={k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.)
Proposition
10.3.2
gives that every

\gamma\in\Gamma(k)
acts
quasisemisimply, with smooth fixed-point group, on

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\circ}
.
Thus we may, and do, conclude by
applying the inductive hypothesis to
the action of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
on

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{\circ}
.

We thus may, and do, assume that
there is no such normal subgroup of
\Gamma
.
Since
\Gamma\cap\underline{\Inn}({\widetilde{G}})
is
a normal subgroup of
\Gamma
that, by
Remark
2.2.8
(
b
),
has only semisimple
k
-rational points,
it is trivial; that is, the action of

\Gamma
on
{\widetilde{G}}
is purely outer.
By Remark
2.1.5
,
we have that
\Gamma
is cyclic, or

p
equals
3
,
{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
, and

{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Out}({\widetilde{G}})}
is an isomorphism.
(It cannot happen that
p
equals
2
,

{\widetilde{G}}
is of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
, and

{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Out}({\widetilde{G}})}
is an isomorphism, since then

\Gamma
would have a
normal subgroup of order relatively prime to
p
.)
Let
\gamma
be a generator of
\Gamma

(if
\Gamma
is cyclic), or
a generator of the normal, order-
3
subgroup of
\Gamma

(in the
\mathsf{D}_{4}
case).
Then
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is smooth by assumption, so

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})^{\circ}
equals
({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
, hence
is reductive by
Theorem
A
(
2
).
Theorem
A
(
3
)
gives that

\mathscr{S}(({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})^{\circ})
equals

\mathscr{S}({\widetilde{G}})^{\gamma}
.
Since
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
is normal in
\Gamma
, the result follows by
applying the inductive hypothesis to
the action of
\Gamma/\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
on

({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})^{\circ}
.
∎

Example 10.3.4
.

Theorem
C
(
1
,
2
)
can fail if we remove the assumption
that every element of
\Gamma({k^{\textup{{a}}}})
preserves a Borel–torus pair
in
{\widetilde{G}}_{k^{\textup{{a}}}}
.
Suppose that
p
does not equal
1
, and put

{\widetilde{G}}=\SL_{2,k}
.
If
\Gamma
is the constant
k
-group generated by

\Int\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
then
G
is not reductive. (A similar counterexample also holds
in arbitrary characteristic if one replaces the above constant group by the
group of upper triangular unipotent matrices in
{\widetilde{G}}
.)
In fact, the behavior of the fixed-point group can be even worse.
If
p
equals
2
,

k
is not perfect,
and

\Gamma
is instead generated by

\Int\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
t&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
where
t
is a non-square in
k
,
then

{\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma}=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
bt&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\middle|\,\mathopen{}a^{2}-b^{2}t=1\right\}

is reduced and connected, but not geometrically reduced.
We have that

({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}
is trivial,
and

(({\widetilde{G}}^{\Gamma})_{k^{\textup{{a}}}})_{\textup{{sm}}}
equals

\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
bt&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,\middle|\,\mathopen{}a-b\sqrt{t}=1\right\}
,
which does not descend to a subgroup of
{\widetilde{G}}
.

Example 10.3.5
.

Theorem
C
(
2
)
can fail without the hypothesis about
smoothness of fixed points.

Consider the involution

{\gamma}\colon{{\tilde{g}}}\longmapsto{\Int\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right){\tilde{g}}^{-\mathsf{T}}}

of
\GL_{3}
, which
we will also regard as an automorphism of

{\widetilde{G}}\mathrel{:=}\PGL_{3}
.
Note that Proposition
8.9
(
a
) implies
that
{\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma}
is not smooth.
The map

{{\tilde{\lambda}}}\colon{t}\longmapsto{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t&1-t&1-t\\
0&1&1-t\\
0&0&t\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

is a cocharacter of
\GL_{3}
, and

\gamma\circ{\tilde{\lambda}}
equals
-{\tilde{\lambda}}
.
The general linear group of
the weight-
1
space for
{\tilde{\lambda}}
in
the defining representation
k^{3}
of
\GL_{3}
maps
isomorphically onto
C_{\widetilde{G}}({\tilde{\lambda}})
, and
the ordered basis
((1,0,0),(0,1,-1))
of
the weight-
1
space provides
an isomorphism with
\GL_{2}
that
identifies
{\tilde{\lambda}}
with
an isomorphism from
\GL_{1}
onto
Z(\GL_{2})
.
Explicit computation shows that the involution of
\GL_{2}

induced by
\gamma
is

{{\tilde{g}}}\longmapsto{\det({\tilde{g}})^{-1}\Int\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right){\tilde{g}}}
.

So far we have been agnostic about the characteristic.
Now, to fit this example into the general framework of
the rest of the section, suppose that
p
equals
2
.
Then
\gamma
acts on
\SL_{2}
as conjugation by
a regular unipotent element and,
in particular, preserves no maximal (or even nontrivial) torus,
i.e.,
\im\lambda
centralizes no maximal torus in
({\widetilde{G}}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
.
Moreover, note that since
(\GL_{2}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is contained in
\SL_{2}
,
it is the unipotent group

(\SL_{2}^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}=C_{\SL_{2}}{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}_{\textup{{sm}}}
;
in particular,
(C_{\widetilde{G}}({\tilde{\lambda}})^{\gamma})_{\textup{{sm}}}^{\circ}
is not reductive.

Appendix A
Induction of schemes with group action

In this section we quickly recall some definitions and results concerning sites and sheaves. In a way,
this is overkill for the purposes of this paper,
where ultimately we are only interested in
sheaves on one particular site. However, in order to clarify the roles that the various concepts play in
our results, it is useful to separate out this material from the main
development. For more details on the contents of this section, see
[
vistoli:descent
]
or

[
milne:etale-cohom
]
. We will ignore
set-theoretic issues; they can be dealt with in a number of different ways, each of which would be
a distraction to the main aim of this appendix.

Definition A.1
.

[
vistoli:descent
]
*Definition 2.24
A
site
is a category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a collection of sets of morphisms

\{{U_{i}}\longrightarrow{U}\}_{i\in I}
, called
covers
, subject to the following conditions.

•

If
U
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
, then
\{{\mathrm{id}}\colon{U}\longrightarrow{U}\}
is a cover.

•

If
\{{U_{i}}\longrightarrow{U}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover and
{V}\longrightarrow{U}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then every
fiber product
U_{i}\times_{U}V
exists and
\{{U_{i}\times_{U}V}\longrightarrow{V}\}_{i\in I}
is a
cover.

•

If
\{{U_{i}}\longrightarrow{U}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover and for each
i\in I
we are given a cover

\{{V_{ij}}\longrightarrow{U_{i}}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
, then
\{{V_{ij}}\longrightarrow{U}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\in J_{i}\end{subarray}}
is a cover.

The collection of covers is called a
topology
on
\mathcal{C}
.
(Often
(and originally in
[
SGA-4.1
]
*Exposé II, Définition 1.3),
this is called a
pretopology
.)

Example A.2
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is any category, then it can be given the
discrete topology
, whose only
covers are of the form
\{{\mathrm{id}}\colon{U}\longrightarrow{U}\}

as
U
ranges over the objects of
\mathcal{C}
.

Example A.3
.

If
k
is a ring and
\mathcal{C}
is the category
\mathrm{AffSch}_{k}
of affine
k
-schemes, then there are
several topologies on
\mathcal{C}
which are commonly in use, among which
are the Zariski, étale, and fppf topologies. In each of these topologies, the coverings are jointly
surjective collections
\{{j_{i}}\colon{U_{i}}\longrightarrow{U}\}_{i\in I}
of morphisms. In the Zariski
topology, each
j_{i}
is an open embedding; in the étale topology, each
j_{i}
is étale; in the fppf
topology, each
j_{i}
is flat and locally of finite presentation. These sites are called the
(big) Zariski site, the (big) étale site, and the (big) fppf site, respectively.

One can also define the
small
Zariski, étale,
and fppf sites of a ring
k
as follows: let

\mathcal{C}_{\text{Zar}}
,

\mathcal{C}_{\text{\'{e}t}}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{fppf}}
denote the full
subcategories of
\mathrm{AffSch}_{k}
consisting of those affine
k
-schemes
X
which are disjoint unions of
Zariski open subschemes of
\Spec k

(respectively, étale over
\Spec k
;
respectively, fppf over
\Spec k
). We give these categories the Zariski, étale, and fppf topologies,
respectively.

Example A.4
.

If
k
is a field, then the small Zariski site of
k
has as objects
\Spec\prod_{i=1}^{n}k
for every
integer
n\geq 0
. The small étale site of
k
has as objects all schemes
\Spec\prod_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}
, where each
k_{i}
is a finite separable extension of
k
. The (small or big) fppf site
of
k
is much larger: it includes
all

k
-algebras.

Definition A.5

(
[
vistoli:descent
]
*Definition 2.37)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are categories, then a
\mathcal{D}
-valued presheaf
on

\mathcal{C}
is a contravariant functor
{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
. If

\mathcal{C}

is a site, then a
\mathcal{D}
-valued sheaf
is a
\mathcal{D}
-valued presheaf
\mathcal{F}
on

\mathcal{C}

satisfying the following
sheaf condition
: if
\{{U_{i}}\longrightarrow{U}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover in
\mathcal{C}
, then the products
\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})
and
\prod_{i,j\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j})
exist in
\mathcal{D}
and the diagram

\mathcal{F}(U)\to\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j})

is an equalizer in
\mathcal{D}
.

If
X
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
a\in\mathcal{F}(X)
, then we will call
a
a
local
section
of
\mathcal{F}
. If
{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then we will often write
a_{Y}

to denote the image of
a
under the corresponding map
{\mathcal{F}(X)}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}(Y)}
. We note
that this notation is abusive because
a_{Y}
depends not just on
Y
, but on the
map

{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
.
However, in practice there will only be one map under consideration at a time, so this should not lead
to substantial confusion.

There is an evident notion of morphism for presheaves, and we let

\PSh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})

(respectively,
\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})
) denote the category of

\mathcal{D}
-valued
presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
(respectively, its full subcategory of sheaves). We will use the simplifying
notation
\PSh_{\mathcal{C}}\mathrel{:=}\PSh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{Sets})

and
\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}\mathrel{:=}\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{Sets})
.
By default, a
sheaf
is assumed to
be set-valued (i.e.,
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathrm{Sets}
),
while a
group sheaf
is valued in the category of groups.

One should have in mind that
\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})
has all good categorical properties
enjoyed by
\mathcal{D}
.
For example, if
\mathcal{D}
admits products, then so does
\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})
: given
two sheaves
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, one defines the product

\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}
to be the sheaf sending
X
to
\mathcal{F}(X)\times\mathcal{G}(X)
; see

[
milne:etale-cohom
]
*II, Lemma 2.12.
Similar
constructions work for all finite limits and colimits.

If
{F}\colon{\mathcal{D}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}
is a limit-preserving functor, then there is an induced functor

{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})}\longrightarrow{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{E})}
(which we will also denote by

F
) given by sending a sheaf
\mathcal{F}
to the sheaf
{X}\longmapsto{F(\mathcal{F}(X))}
. If
F
is a
forgetful functor, then we will often omit explicit mention of this functor.

Example A.6

(
[
milne:etale-cohom
]
*II, Example 2.18(a))
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category which is considered as a site with the discrete topology and

\mathcal{D}

is any category, then
\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})

equals

\PSh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is the category with a unique object and morphism, then

\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})

is naturally equivalent to
\mathcal{D}
.

Example A.7
.

Let
k
be a ring. A set-valued sheaf on the big Zariski site (respectively, the big étale site;
respectively, the big fppf site) is called a Zariski sheaf (respectively, étale sheaf;
respectively, fppf sheaf). A sheaf on the small Zariski site of
k
is the same as a sheaf on the
topological space
\lvert\Spec k\rvert
, and this serves as the motivation for the general definition of sheaves on a site.
On the
other hand, a sheaf on the big Zariski site of
k
contains
much
more information; it takes
values on (the spectrum of) every
k
-algebra.

If
X
is a
k
-scheme, then there is a set-valued functor
h_{X}
on
\mathrm{AffSch}_{k}
defined by

h_{X}(\Spec A)=\Mor_{k}(\Spec A,X)
. By
[
vistoli:descent
]
*2.55, the functor
h_{X}
is an fppf sheaf
(and therefore also a Zariski sheaf and an étale sheaf). We call the functor
{X}\longmapsto{h_{X}}
the
Yoneda embedding
.
We will (abusively) use the letter
X
to refer also to the image of
X
under the Yoneda embedding.

Just as in the topological case, if
\mathcal{C}
is a site and
\mathcal{D}
is either the category of
sets, the category of groups, or the category of abelian groups, then the inclusion

{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})}\longrightarrow{\PSh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})}
admits a left adjoint
{\mathcal{F}}\longmapsto{\mathcal{F}^{\text{sh}}}
,
called the
sheafification
functor.
See
[
vistoli:descent
]
*Theorem 2.64 for the case

\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Sets}
.

Example A.8
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a site and
S
is a set, then we define the
constant sheaf

\underline{S}

to be the sheafification of the presheaf
{X}\longmapsto{S}
on

\mathcal{C}
.
(To see the effect of sheafification in our setting, note that,
if
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathrm{AffSch}_{k}
for some ring
k
, then

\underline{S}(\Spec(k\oplus k))
is

S\times S
, not just
S
.)
If
S
is a group, then
\underline{S}
is a group sheaf on
\mathcal{C}
.

In practice, one is interested in
sheaves
on sites, and
not
in sites themselves. In our
case, we are largely interested in sheaves on the (big) fppf site of a field, and in fact
our interest lies mainly in group sheaves. One benefit of working with fppf group sheaves is that they
allow us to work with group schemes “as if” they were ordinary groups (see below). In order to
do this, we must first set up some formalism which is best understood in our abstract setting. Note
that a group sheaf on a site is the same as a group object in
the category of sheaves.

Definition A.9

(
[
milne:etale-cohom
]
*II, Theorem 2.15)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site, and let
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
be group sheaves on

\mathcal{C}
.
If
{f}\colon{\mathcal{G}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}
is a homomorphism of group
sheaves (i.e., a morphism of sheaves
such that for every object
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the map
{f(X)}\colon{\mathcal{G}(X)}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{H}(X)}

is a group homomorphism), then we define the
kernel

\ker f
of
f
by

(\ker f)(X)=\ker(f(X))
for every object
X\in\mathcal{C}
; with this definition, one can check that

\ker f
is a group sheaf. If
f(\mathcal{G}(X))\subseteq\mathcal{H}(X)
is a normal
subgroup for every object
X
, then we define the cokernel
\coker f
of
f
to be the

sheafification
of the group presheaf
{X}\longmapsto{\coker(f(X))}
. We say that
f
is a

monomorphism
if
\ker f
is the trivial sheaf
{X}\longmapsto{\left\{1\right\}}
(i.e., if
f(X)
is injective for all

X
), and we say that
f
is an
epimorphism
if
\coker f
is the trivial sheaf
(i.e., for each
X
and each
h\in\mathcal{H}(X)
, there is a cover
\{{U_{i}}\longrightarrow{X}\}
and elements
g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(U_{i})
such that
f(g_{i})=h_{U_{i}}
for all
i
). The
image

\im f
of
f
is the cokernel of the map
{\ker f}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
.
There is a natural monomorphism
{\im f}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}
, induced by
f
, which we use to regard
\im f
as a subsheaf of
\mathcal{H}
.
Using this identification, the image of
f
is the sheafification of
{X}\longmapsto{f(\mathcal{G}(X))}
.
A sequence
\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathcal{H}
is
exact
if
\ker\psi=\im\varphi
.

Example A.10
.

If
k
is a field and
n>1
is an integer, then the
n
th power map
{[n]}\colon{\GL_{1}}\longrightarrow{\GL_{1}}
is
an epimorphism of fppf group sheaves, but it is usually
not
an epimorphism
in the category of functors: there are many
k
-algebras
A
which admit elements
a\in A
which
are not
n
th roots. On the other hand, for any such
a\in A
, the algebra
B=A[x]/(x^{n}-a)
is
A
-flat and admits an
n
th root of
a
.

Before specializing to the case of fppf group sheaves, we would like to attempt to motivate why one
would be interested in the fppf site over the Zariski or étale sites when studying
group schemes. In essence, if one is interested only in smooth
k
-group schemes and smooth
homomorphisms between them, then the étale site is entirely sufficient: for instance, if

{G}\longrightarrow{Q}
is a smooth surjective homomorphism of smooth
k
-group schemes with kernel
N
,
then the sequence

1\to h_{N}\to h_{G}\to h_{Q}\to 1

is a short exact sequence of étale group sheaves (but
not
of group presheaves!). However, even
this simple statement fails when smoothness is relaxed: for example, let
k
be an
imperfect field of characteristic exponent
p>1
. The homomorphism
{[p]}\colon{\GL_{1}}\longrightarrow{\GL_{1}}
is surjective with kernel
\mu_{p}
, but the corresponding sequence

1\to h_{\mu_{p}}\to h_{\GL_{1}}\xrightarrow{[p]}h_{\GL_{1}}\to 1

of étale group sheaves is
not
exact: if
x\in k

is not a
p
th power, then there is no separable extension
k^{\prime}/k
such that
x
is
a
p
th power in
k^{\prime}
. Moreover, if one simply extends the small étale site to include
all
finite
extensions of
k
, one still encounters the strange issue that
[p]

is both a monomorphism and an epimorphism, but not an isomorphism;
this is a symptom of the fact that
\mu_{p}
is a nontrivial group scheme which has
no nontrivial field-valued points. Thus, in order to work
with group schemes “as if they are groups”, particularly in positive characteristic, one must work with
rings which are not fields. The fppf site is the right setting in which to do this.

In particular, the Yoneda embedding gives a fully faithful embedding from the category
of
k
-group schemes to the category of fppf group sheaves
on
\mathrm{AffSch}_{k}
, and it is a theorem of Grothendieck
(see

[
SGA-3.1
]
*Exposé VI
B
, Théorème 3.2
and
Theorem
A.22
)
that, if
k
is a field, then
\Sh_{\mathrm{AffSch}_{k}}(\Grp)

is closed under quotients and extensions in the sheaf category.
Thus it is reasonable to transport the notion of exact sequence from the category of
fppf group sheaves to the category of finite type flat
k
-group schemes.

Definition A.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site and

\mathcal{D}
a category.
If
X
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
, then we define a category
\mathcal{C}_{/X}

with objects being morphisms
{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
in
\mathcal{C}
, and morphisms

{({Y}\longrightarrow{X})}\longrightarrow{({Z}\longrightarrow{X})}
being morphisms
{Y}\longrightarrow{Z}
which are compatible with the maps to
X
. One can give
a natural topology on
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
. If
\mathcal{F}
is any sheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, then there is
a natural restricted sheaf
\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X}
defined by

\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X}({Y}\longrightarrow{X})\coloneqq\mathcal{F}(Y),

and with the natural restriction maps.

For sheaves
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})
, there is a set of
morphisms
\Mor_{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{D})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
. We define
an object
\underline{\Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\in\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{Sets})
by defining, for every
object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
,

\underline{\Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})(X)=\Mor_{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathcal{D})}(\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X},\mathcal{G}\rvert_{X}).

It is straightforward to check that
\underline{\Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
is a sheaf on

\mathcal{C}
.
Notice that
\underline{\Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
is a sheaf of monoids.

One special case of interest to us is when

\mathcal{D}
is the category of groups.
In that case, we will often write

\underline{\Hom}
instead of
\underline{\Mor}
.

Another case that is of interest is when

\mathcal{D}
is the category of sets, but
the sheaf
\mathcal{G}
of sets arises by
forgetting the group structure on a sheaf of groups.
Then the resulting morphism

\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
makes
each morphism set

\underline{\Mor}_{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}(\mathrm{Sets})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})

into a group, so

\underline{\Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
is
a group sheaf.

We briefly introduce two more constructions which will be useful later.

Definition A.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf of sets on
\mathcal{C}
. Define
\SubSh(\mathcal{F})(X)
to be the set of subsheaves of
\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X}
on
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
. Note that this defines a presheaf
\SubSh(\mathcal{F})
on
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma A.13
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a site and
\mathcal{F}
is a sheaf of sets on
\mathcal{C}
, then

\SubSh(\mathcal{F})
is a sheaf of sets on
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.
Let
\{{U_{i}}\longrightarrow{X}\}
be a cover in
\mathcal{C}
. We must show that the sequence

\SubSh(\mathcal{F})(X)\to\prod_{i}\SubSh(\mathcal{F})(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j}\SubSh(\mathcal{F})(U_{i}\times_{X}U_{j})

is an equalizer sequence. Note first that the left arrow is injective: let
\mathcal{G}
and

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be two subsheaves of
\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X}
such that

\mathcal{G}\rvert_{U_{i}}=\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\rvert_{U_{i}}
for all
i
. If
{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
is any morphism in
\mathcal{C}
,
then there is an equalizer sequence

\mathcal{G}(Y)\to\prod_{i}\mathcal{G}(U_{i}\times_{X}Y)\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{G}(U_{i}\times_{X}U_{j}\times_{X}Y)

and similarly for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
(Note that we have abused notation by writing, e.g.,
\mathcal{G}(Y)
instead of the more proper
\mathcal{G}({Y}\longrightarrow{X})
.)
Since all but the leftmost term in the above sequence are the same for
\mathcal{G}
and

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, it follows that
\mathcal{G}(Y)=\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(Y)
, as desired.

Now let
\mathcal{G}_{i}
be a subsheaf of
\mathcal{F}\rvert_{U_{i}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{/U_{i}}
for each

i
, and suppose that
\mathcal{G}_{i}\rvert_{U_{i}\times_{X}U_{j}}=\mathcal{G}_{j}\rvert_{U_{i}\times_{X}U_{j}}
for all

i
,
j
. For each morphism
{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we get maps
{Y\times_{X}U_{i}}\longrightarrow{U_{i}}
, and we
define
\mathcal{G}(Y)
such that the sequence

\mathcal{G}(Y)\to\prod_{i}\mathcal{G}_{i}(U_{i}\times_{X}Y)\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{G}_{ij}(U_{i}\times_{X}U_{j}\times_{X}Y)

is exact, where
\mathcal{G}_{ij}=\mathcal{G}_{i}\rvert_{U_{ij}}=\mathcal{G}_{j}\rvert_{U_{ij}}
. The fact that

\mathcal{G}
is a sheaf follows from a version of the nine lemma which we leave to the reader.
∎

Lemma A.14
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf on a site
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be the presheaf defined by
sending an object
X
to the set of pairs
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2})
in
\SubSh(\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X})^{2}

satisfying
\mathcal{G}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{2}
.
Then
\mathcal{G}
is a sheaf.

Proof.
This follows directly from Lemma
A.13
and the fact that, if
\mathcal{G}_{1}

and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
are two subsheaves of
\mathcal{G}\rvert_{X}
, then one can check that

\mathcal{G}_{1}\subset\mathcal{G}_{2}
after passing to a cover of
X
.
∎

Definition A.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a site. If
\mathcal{F}
is a sheaf of sets on
\mathcal{C}
and
\Gamma
is a
sheaf of groups on
\mathcal{C}
with multiplication morphism
m_{\Gamma}
, then an

action
of
\Gamma
on
\mathcal{F}
is a map of sheaves
{\alpha}\colon{\Gamma\times\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times\Gamma\times\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\Gamma}\times\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}\times\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Equivalently, a group action is a homomorphism
{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})}
.
We call the pair
(\mathcal{F},\alpha)
a

\Gamma
-sheaf, and will often suppress
\alpha
from the notation.

If
(\mathcal{F}_{1},\alpha_{1})
and
(\mathcal{F}_{2},\alpha_{2})
are

\Gamma
-sheaves,
then we say that a morphism
{f}\colon{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}_{2}}
is

\Gamma
-equivariant if
f\circ\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}\circ f
.
We denote the set of
\Gamma
-equivariant morphisms by
\Mor_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
. As before, there is also a sheaf of sets

\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
.
The collection of
\Gamma
-sheaves (along with
\Gamma
-equivariant morphisms) assembles into a category

{\Gamma}\text{-}{\Sh}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

If

\mathcal{F}
is a
\Gamma
-sheaf, then we define a sheaf
of sets
\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}
by sending an object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
to the set

\mathcal{F}(X)^{\Gamma}

consisting of those elements
a
of
\mathcal{F}(X)
such that, for every morphism
{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
and
every
\gamma\in\Gamma(Y)
, we have
\gamma\cdot a_{Y}=a_{Y}
. The map
{\mathcal{F}}\longmapsto{\mathcal{F}^{\Gamma}}
induces a functor

{(\cdot)^{\Gamma}}\colon{{\Gamma}\text{-}{\Sh}_{\mathcal{C}}}\longrightarrow{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Most familiar facts about group actions on sets extend straightforwardly to facts about actions of group
sheaves on sheaves of sets. We will give complete details only a couple of times;
as the reader will hopefully find, proving these extensions only requires more bookkeeping than the
usual facts, and it requires few new ideas.

Lemma A.16
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group sheaf on a site
\mathcal{C}
. The functor
(\cdot)^{\Gamma}
is right
adjoint to the functor
{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}}\longrightarrow{{\Gamma}\text{-}{\Sh}_{\mathcal{C}}}

which gives a sheaf of sets the trivial
\Gamma
-action.

Proof.
There is an evident inclusion
\Mor_{\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}^{\Gamma})\subseteq\Mor_{{\Gamma}\text{-}{\Sh}_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
for

\mathcal{F}\in\Sh_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathcal{G}\in{\Gamma}\text{-}{\Sh}_{\mathcal{C}}

(where we have denoted the sheaf
\mathcal{F}
with
the trivial
\Gamma
-action again by

\mathcal{F}
),
and we need only
check that it is an equality. Indeed, if the morphism
{\phi}\colon{\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant,

X
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
, and
f
belongs to

\mathcal{F}(X)
, then, for any
{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
and any
\gamma\in\Gamma(Y)
, we have

\phi(f)_{Y}=\phi(f_{Y})=\phi(\gamma\cdot f_{Y})=\gamma\cdot\phi(f_{Y})=\gamma\cdot\phi(f)_{Y},

so by definition
\phi(f)
belongs to
\mathcal{G}^{\Gamma}(X)
.
∎

Let
{f}\colon{\Gamma^{\prime}}\longrightarrow{\Gamma}
be a homomorphism of group sheaves on a site
\mathcal{C}
,
and let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf of sets on
\mathcal{C}
. Define an action of

\Gamma^{\prime}

on
\underline{\Mor}(\Gamma,\mathcal{F})
as follows: if
X
is an object,
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}(X)

and
\gamma\in\Gamma(X)
are local sections, and
{\phi}\colon{\Gamma\rvert_{X}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X}}
is a morphism, then we set
(\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\phi)(\gamma)=\phi(f(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}\gamma)
.

Remark A.17
.

If
{f}\colon{\Gamma_{1}}\longrightarrow{\Gamma}
is a homomorphism,
then we may equip
\Gamma
with the structure of a
\Gamma_{1}
-sheaf
via right multiplication
(that is, for every object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
and every

\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma_{1}(X)
and
\gamma\in\Gamma(X)
,
the result of acting by
\gamma_{1}
on
\gamma
is

\gamma f(\gamma_{1})^{-1}
).
If
\mathcal{F}
is a
\Gamma_{1}
-sheaf, then the

\Gamma
-action
on
\underline{\Mor}(\Gamma,\mathcal{F})

deduced from the identity map
{\Gamma}\longrightarrow{\Gamma}
preserves the
subsheaf
\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,\mathcal{F})
. Thus

\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,(\cdot))
is a functor from
\Gamma_{1}
-sheaves to

\Gamma
-sheaves.
When we take into account the group-sheaf structure on
morphism sets defined in
Definition
A.11
, we have that

\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,(\cdot))

restricts to a functor from
group
\Gamma_{1}
-sheaves to
group
\Gamma
-sheaves

Lemma A.18
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
and
\Delta
be group sheaves on a site
\mathcal{C}
, and suppose that

\Gamma

acts on
\Delta
through group automorphisms. If
\mathcal{F}
is a (
\Gamma\ltimes\Delta
)-sheaf
on
\mathcal{C}
, then the restriction morphism

{\rho}\colon{\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma^{\prime}\ltimes\Delta}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{F})}\longrightarrow{\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\Gamma,\mathcal{F})}

is a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism of sheaves.

Proof.
It is clear that
\rho
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.
To show that
\rho
is an isomorphism,
we must define an inverse morphism
\eta
. To do so, if
X
is an
object of
\mathcal{C}
and
{\psi}\colon{\Gamma\rvert_{X}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X}}
is a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant morphism,
define
{\eta(\psi)}\colon{(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta)\rvert_{X}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}\rvert_{X}}
as follows: if
{Y}\longrightarrow{X}
is a
morphism in
\mathcal{C}
and
(\gamma,\delta)
belongs to
(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta)(Y)
,
then we set
\eta(\psi)(\gamma,\delta)=\gamma\delta\gamma^{-1}\cdot\psi(\gamma)
. The fact that
\eta(\psi)
is

\Gamma^{\prime}\ltimes\Delta
-equivariant
is baked into the definition, and the fact that
\eta\circ\rho
and

\rho\circ\eta
are the respective identity morphisms is a direct calculation from the definitions.
∎

For the remainder of the appendix, let
\Gamma_{1}\subseteq\Gamma
be an inclusion of group
sheaves on some fixed site
\mathcal{C}
.
As in Remark
A.17
,
we let
\Gamma_{1}
act on
\Gamma
by
(inverted) right translation.
From now on, we will begin to be less strict about choosing objects in proofs, relying more heavily on
the terminology of local sections. We do this with the aim that it will make the following proofs less
heavy on notation, without sacrificing too much clarity.

Lemma A.19
.

The functor
{\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,(\cdot))}\colon{{\Gamma_{1}}\text{-}{\Sh}_{\mathcal{C}}}\longrightarrow{{\Gamma}\text{-}{\Sh}_{\mathcal{C}}}

from Remark
A.17
is right adjoint to the forgetful functor.

Proof.
Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be sheaves on
\mathcal{C}
, equipped with actions of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{1}
, respectively.
We define
{\eta}\colon{\Mor_{\Gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})}\longrightarrow{\Mor_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F},\underline{\Mor}_{\Gamma_{1}}(\Gamma,\mathcal{G}))}

by
\eta(\phi)(x)(\gamma)\mathrel{:=}\phi(\gamma^{-1}x)
for local sections
x
of
\mathcal{F}
and
\gamma
of
\Gamma
. Note first
that
\eta(\phi)(x)
is
\Gamma_{1}
-equivariant for every
x
: indeed, if
\gamma_{1}
is a local section of
\Gamma_{1}
, then
we have

\eta(\phi)(x)(\gamma\gamma_{1}^{-1})=\phi(\gamma_{1}\gamma^{-1}x)=\gamma_{1}\cdot\phi(\gamma^{-1}x)=\gamma_{1}\cdot\eta(\phi)(x).

Next,
\eta(\phi)
is
\Gamma
-equivariant: indeed, if
\gamma_{0}
is a local section of
\Gamma
